## Introduction

Welcome to *JavaScript QuickStart Guide* .

My name is Robert Oliver, and I’ll be your guide through learning how to use JavaScript to add fun and useful interactive elements to websites.

### I Grew to Love JavaScript and You Will Too

Let me start by being brutally honest. At first, I didn’t like JavaScript.

I found JavaScript clunky and annoying. When I needed interactivity on a website, I’d reach for a server-side language like PHP or Python to get the job done. This worked, but didn’t accomplish the immediate experience I wanted for the visitors to the websites I built because all the interactivity had to be engineered on the server—not in the visitor’s browser.

When Chrome’s V8 JavaScript engine (the part of Google Chrome that runs JavaScript) came on the scene, I was blown away by its speed. The engineers at Google certainly wove some magic into its construction, giving Chrome a significant advantage over its competitors with stellar JavaScript performance.

To add even more utility to the JavaScript language, Node.js, built on V8, was released, bringing the phenomenal performance of Chrome’s JavaScript engine to the server. Now, I could use the same language on the server that I used in the browser, and all was right with the world. Armed with this new experience, I grew to love the JavaScript language, finding incredible utility in its nuanced features.

I realize there are some potentially new concepts and terms mentioned above. Don’t worry about what it all means yet; we’ll cover JavaScript’s history and versions briefly in [chapter 1](#ch01.xhtml) . The main thing you need to know for now is that once you see the power of JavaScript and learn how easy it is to get started, you’ll love it too!

### What Can JavaScript Do?

It would be easier to answer what JavaScript cannot do. JavaScript is being used for a wide variety of tasks—far more than it was originally designed for. JavaScript is currently being used for the following applications:

» Web pages

» Full web applications

» Mobile app development

» Video games

» Desktop applications

» Blockchain technologies

» Automation and testing

Knowing JavaScript puts you in an awesome position to work on some of the fastest-growing technologies of our time.

### Why Learn JavaScript?

I’m not the only software developer to recognize all that JavaScript brings to the table. The very fact you’re reading this book shows you have the wisdom to see the value of JavaScript. It’s an incredibly pervasive web programming language. In fact, along with a few other languages, proficiency with JavaScript is frequently listed as a job requirement by employers seeking to hire web developers.

Since the introduction of HTML5, JavaScript has been fully (or at least widely) supported by all the major web browsers. You can use JavaScript to create interactive websites that run smoothly on mobile and other platforms. And now with Node.js, you can add JavaScript’s capability to connect interactive websites to the data sources that power them.

### Do I Need to Know HTML and CSS?

In a word, yes. But detailed, in-depth knowledge of the intricacies of these two markup languages is not required. The more you know about web design in general, the faster you’ll be able to put JavaScript to work on your websites. That said, in this book we’ll assume only basic knowledge of HyperText Markup Language (HTML) and Cascading Style Sheets (CSS). There’s a quick refresher on the fundamentals of web design in appendix I.

### Frontend Versus Backend

To really explore the true power of JavaScript, we’ll need to first define a few terms. The ***frontend*** of a website refers to the experience of the user in the web browser. The frontend is what users see when they read, use, or otherwise interact with a website.

The ***backend*** refers to the server and related processes that deliver the page to the frontend. This can be as simple as a basic web server sending an HTML page to the user’s web browser, or as complex as a real-time, interactive dialog between browser and server.

You can see the effects of JavaScript on websites when you hover over menus, drag elements from one area to another, or scroll with your mouse and notice that certain elements move while others stay static, and when page elements are animated. Of course, JavaScript can do more than this inside the web browser, but these are common manifestations of JavaScript code on the frontend.
Backend JavaScript isn’t as easy to see because it’s running on the server. You can’t see that code, but you can interact with it. When you add a product to the cart, place an order, or post an image to social media, JavaScript is likely working in the backend to make that happen. In fact, JavaScript is not only popular for providing website functionality, but is frequently used as the backend interface for mobile applications. It’s quite likely that your favorite social media app or mobile game is using JavaScript to save your content or track your game progress.

### How to Use This Book

As we progress through this book, you’ll not only learn JavaScript but build some interesting projects along the way—including a fully interactive website for a fictional coffee shop we’ll call the ClydeBank Coffee Shop.

In parts I through III, we’ll work in the frontend, adding fun, interactive elements to the website. Then, in [part IV](#part04.xhtml) , we’ll add backend code to the website, allowing it to process orders. Working through these exercises will give you the skills you need to build a functional e-commerce website that looks great and is a joy to use.

I’m not a fan of burdening you with a lot of theory before we get to code. Instead, we’ll take a different approach—one that emphasizes showing, not telling, how to program. You’ll be able to use this knowledge right away. I find it’s easier to understand code when you first see it run, so expect lots of example code immediately followed by a breakdown of what it does and how it works.

### Example Code

Most of the examples and all the website frontend and backend code in this book are available on GitHub at [https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide](https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide) .

We’ll be using Visual Studio Code as our editor in this book. I strongly encourage you to type out the code as we work through each example. Not only will this give you the experience of using the editor, but you’ll also form muscle memory that will help you quickly become a productive programmer.

The code is available on GitHub if you need it, but please remember that *typing the code is an important part of your learning* .

### What You’ll Need

To get the most from this book, you need a computer. Unfortunately, a phone or tablet doesn’t run the software or provide the interface you’ll need to be productive.

The good news is that JavaScript works on all modern platforms, so any computer running Windows, macOS, or Linux will work. When I say *any* , I should specify that the computer needs to be relatively recent. Any machine manufactured in the last five years will certainly be fine, but older computers may also work. If your computer is older, be sure that your operating system is still getting updates and security fixes. If not, you’re at increased risk from malware.

While Chromebooks do run Google Chrome, developing on them can be tricky, and this particular workflow is beyond the scope of this book. Using Chromebooks to code JavaScript is possible, but is not recommended for beginners.

### Getting Ready

If your computer has a web browser, you already have most of what you need to use JavaScript. You also need a code editor (more on that in a minute), as well as Node.js for the backend examples and exercises in this book.

#### Installing Google Chrome

Google Chrome is the most popular web browser at time of publication, so you likely already have it installed. If you don’t, you can download a copy from [https://www.google.com/chrome/](https://www.google.com/chrome/) . Installation is simple—just follow the instructions on screen.

If you prefer to use another Chrome-based browser, that will be fine, but note that there may be some minor differences in the instructions you’re following in this book versus what you see on-screen.

You can use Mozilla Firefox if you prefer, but there will be differences in the web browser development tools. Additionally, some JavaScript may not work precisely the same as with Chrome. When possible, I’ll note these differences, but be aware that this choice of browser might present a few challenges.

Ideally, you’ll want to have both Google Chrome and Mozilla Firefox installed, with Firefox primarily used for testing. If you’re on a Mac, you can test with Safari as well. A good web developer tests their code in as many browsers as time and patience will permit.

#### Installing Visual Studio Code

There are many ***code editors*** on the market. A code editor is a text editor that has special features that make it easier to write code. Fortunately, many of them are free/open source. Here is a list of commonly used code editors with JavaScript support, including their operating system support and license:

» Visual Studio Code (Windows, macOS, Linux; free, mostly open source)

» Notepad++ (Windows only; free, open source)

» Sublime (Windows, macOS, Linux; paid software with free trial)

» WebStorm (Windows, macOS, Linux; paid software with free trial)

» Vim (Windows, macOS, Linux; free, open source)

» Emacs (Windows, macOS, Linux; free, open source)

In this book, we’ll be using Visual Studio Code. It’s well supported on all operating systems and it’s free/open source. It’s an excellent code editor with a first-rate development environment. Having said that, if you’re fond of another editor, please feel free to use it. However, the instructions here will center around Visual Studio Code.

The easiest way to install Visual Studio Code on Windows is to use the Microsoft Store.

For macOS or Linux, go to [https://code.visualstudio.com/](https://code.visualstudio.com/) and download it for your platform. On macOS, the disk image can be mounted by double clicking it. Then drag Visual Studio Code to your Applications folder. Linux users will need to download either a .deb (Debian, Ubuntu, Linux Mint, MX Linux) package or a .rpm (Fedora, openSUSE, Red Hat Enterprise Linux) package and install it on their system.

We’ll configure it in the next section, after creating a place for our code.

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

Other than Visual Studio Code for this book’s exercises, I’m not endorsing one editor over another. Code editor choice is a personal preference, and everyone has their own opinions on the subject. However, if you’re looking for an alternative to Visual Studio Code, here are a few thoughts:

Notepad++ is an excellent code editor but is only for Windows. If you only use Windows, this might be fine, but its lack of multi-platform support creates issues for my workflow.

I haven’t had much experience with Sublime, but several colleagues of mine really like it. The price is reasonable, and it has some unique features you might find appealing.

I quite like the JetBrains products, including WebStorm, but this is a paid solution, so I didn’t want to focus on it in this book. I use other JetBrains products, like DataGrip and PyCharm, and WebStorm fits in well in their ecosystem.

Vim and Emacs are advanced editors. I use Vim in my day-to-day programming and system administration work. It has a lot of advantages. I wouldn’t recommend either Vim or Emacs to someone new to programming, however, as they both have a steep learning curve.

#### Installing Node.js

We aren’t using Node.js now, but we will in [Part IV](#part04.xhtml) .

To install Node.js, first download the files from [https://nodejs.org](https://nodejs.org) . Choose the version that matches your operating system and that is recommended for most users, which is the LTS (Long-Term Support) version. The Node.js Setup Wizard will guide you through the installation process.

Node.js doesn’t come with an interface. We’ll test the installation of Node.js in [Part IV](#part04.xhtml) , so for now, let’s move on.

#### Creating a Folder for Your Work

I like to store my work in its own folder. Regardless of my operating system (macOS, Windows, and Linux—I use them all), I create a folder called Source in my home directory. Inside that folder, I create a folder for each project. You don’t have to follow this scheme, of course, but if you’re unsure as to how you want to proceed, I recommend following along with this layout.

Make a new folder called JavaScriptQSG inside your Source folder.

### Chapter-by-Chapter

#### Part I

In [part I](#part01.xhtml) , we learn the basics of JavaScript. We get started with our JavaScript workflow, cover variables, and learn how to work with control structures like loops and conditional statements.

» In [chapter 1](#ch01.xhtml) , we use three different methods to run our first lines of JavaScript code and cover the workflow we’ll be using throughout the rest of the book.

» In [chapter 2](#ch02.xhtml) , we discuss JavaScript data structures, including variables like strings, numbers, and arrays. We also discuss variable scope.

» In [chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml) , we discuss the flow of your program. We cover if statements and other comparisons, as well as using loops.

» In [chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml) , we take a deep dive into functions, including passing arguments to them and specifying default values.

#### Part II

In [part II](#part02.xhtml) , we dig deeper, exploring powerful object-oriented concepts like classes and objects, and explore a quirky but powerful feature called closure. We also cover JavaScript Object Notation (JSON), the Document Object Model (DOM), and asynchronous programming in JavaScript.

» In [chapter 5](#ch05.xhtml) , we cover the complex yet powerful concept of closure.

» In [chapter 6](#ch06.xhtml) , we introduce object-oriented programming with classes, objects, and classless objects. We also talk about methods and properties.

» In [chapter 7](#ch07.xhtml) , we survey JSON and how to use it to exchange data with other services.

» In [chapter 8](#ch08.xhtml) , we explore the DOM and how to navigate and manipulate it with JavaScript.

» In [chapter 9](#ch09.xhtml) , we discuss asynchronous programming and events.

#### Part III

In [part III](#part03.xhtml) , we cover advanced topics in JavaScript like design principles and common patterns, along with a cornucopia of tools and techniques you’ll use every day.

» In [chapter 10](#ch10.xhtml) , we explore JavaScript design patterns.

» In [chapter 11](#ch11.xhtml) , we put the concepts we learned in [chapter 10](#ch10.xhtml) to use by building a JavaScript-driven website.

» In [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) , we cover advanced JavaScript concepts and functionality, like advanced string operations, math, and cookies.

» In [chapter 13](#ch13.xhtml) , we discuss animation in JavaScript.

» In [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) , we open the JavaScript junk drawer and discuss other topics that don’t warrant an entire chapter but are nevertheless worth covering.

» In [chapter 15](#ch15.xhtml) , we show how jQuery can save you an enormous amount of time by streamlining and simplifying a lot of tedious operations.

#### Part IV

In [part IV](#part04.xhtml) , we discuss the concepts of full-stack development and JavaScript frameworks. We cover Node.js, React, and Git.

» In [chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml) , we cover Node.js and npm (the Node package manager).

» In [chapter 17](#ch17.xhtml) , we explore the popular React framework.

» In [chapter 18](#ch18.xhtml) , we survey the insanely popular Git source code management software.

In addition, appendix I provides a whirlwind refresher course on HTML and CSS, appendix II covers the http-server Node.js package, and appendix III covers popular third-party libraries.

I’m excited to explore JavaScript with you. Let’s get started!

## Part I

## JavaScript Essentials

## | 1 |

## Getting Started with JavaScript

Chapter Overview

» The ClydeBank Coffee Shop website

» The ES6 standards on all major browsers

» The JavaScript console and development workflow

Welcome to JavaScript!

You’re about to start a journey that will not only teach you JavaScript but lead you through the construction of a fully featured website.

In addition to gaining practical experience, you’ll also have a framework you can use to build a site for yourself, a client, or an employer. This won’t be a chintzy demo site that looks like it came from a book; rather, it will contain actual functionality you’ll use throughout your career. We’ll display inventory, build a shopping cart, and even create tools to manage a newsletter.

You’ve got everything you need at your fingertips to jumpstart your JavaScript learning. I’m excited to walk with you on this journey and can’t wait to see what you’ll do with your newfound knowledge.

I know you’re eager to begin learning, but before we dive into our project, I need to talk about our workflow and introduce you to some basic JavaScript concepts.

### ES6

There’s an additional item we should discuss before we write our first line of code. Taking a quick peek at the history of JavaScript will help us understand the relative stability of the language today.

Netscape first released JavaScript at the end of 1995 ^† , providing a simple way to add interactive elements to web pages. During the 1990s, Netscape and Microsoft offered competing versions of JavaScript, leaving developers to struggle with making their websites work the same on both Netscape Navigator and Internet Explorer. If you browse web pages in the Internet Archive from this period, you’ll find lots of “Best Viewed with Netscape Navigator” and “Best Viewed with Internet Explorer” badges that displayed the designer’s preference.

The Mozilla Foundation, Netscape’s successor, worked with Ecma International (formerly ECMA, the European Computer Manufacturers Association) and others to standardize the language. The resulting standard was named ECMAScript. ECMAScript 2015 (also called ECMAScript 6) introduced major improvements to the JavaScript language. This standard is well supported by all modern browsers, and, although most browsers support more recent versions, it’s a very safe bet to design around the ECMAScript 6 (or ES6 for short) standard.

We’ll talk about later standards, but just know for now that we’ll target ES6\. As I mentioned in the introduction, there are multiple browsers with different JavaScript engines, but everything in use today will work with ES6\. I wanted to share this with you so you’ll be confident that the language you learn—even from the first line of code—will work across the web.

### Hello, JavaScript!

Enough introduction—let’s get to writing some code!

Most beginner programmers learn to display “Hello, World!” to the screen. This was popularized in the 1978 book *The C Programming Language* by Brian Kernighan and Dennis Ritchie. The first example the reader copied from the book was a quick program that simply displayed “Hello, World!” I see no reason to deviate from this convention, so let’s get started.

As with nearly all things in programming, there are several ways to accomplish this task. Let’s examine each method.

### Logging to the Console

Thanks to the Google Chrome JavaScript console, we can run code without having to crack open our Visual Studio Code editor.

Launch Google Chrome. There are several ways to access the developer tools (which contain the JavaScript console), but the simplest method is to simply press F12\. This works on Windows, macOS, and Linux, but on some keyboards (especially Macs) you’ll have to hold down the function key while pressing F12 ( [figure 1](#ch01.xhtml_fig1) ).

![img](Images/fig01.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_1.png) 

The JavaScript console in Google Chrome.

If that doesn’t work for you, or if you prefer to use the mouse, you can right click anywhere in the browser window (where the web page would normally be) and click *Inspect* . This brings up the developer tools and shows us the *Elements* tab. You can then click on the *Console* tab immediately to the right ( [figure 2](#ch01.xhtml_fig2) ).

![img](Images/fig02.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_2.png) 

The Elements tab in the Google Chrome developer tools.

Note the *Console* tab to the right of *Elements* (circled in this figure).

There may be content in the console window, especially if you have a web page loaded, but that won’t affect what we’re doing. That said, if you’re like me and you’d like a clean slate in the console, you can press CTRL+L (CMD+L on macOS) to clear it.

At the > prompt, type the following…

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-01-01.png) 

```
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

…and press ENTER. When you do, you’ll see…

```
Hello, World!
```

…shown on the console below our line of code.

You’ll also see undefined , but let’s ignore that for now as it doesn’t apply to what we are doing.

Congratulations, you just wrote your first program! That didn’t seem like a program, did it? But it is. You instructed the computer to do something, and it did it.

Before we move on, try changing the message.

```
console.log("Hello, Robert!")
```

When you press ENTER, you’ll see:

```
Hello, Robert!
```

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try changing the name in the above example to your own and run the code. If you get stuck, you can find the answer in [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) .

### Using Alert

We can do more than simply display text in the JavaScript console. We can also pop up a window with whatever text we like. On the prompt, type the following…

```
alert("Hello, World!")
```

…and press ENTER. You’ll see a pop-up with your greeting ( [figure 3](#ch01.xhtml_fig3) ).

![img](Images/fig03.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_3.png) 

Alert pop-up window showing “Hello, World!”

After clicking *OK* to dismiss the message, try changing the message around to whatever you like.

### Displaying on the Web Page

Our last two examples were done entirely in the JavaScript console, but now it’s time to create a web page. This is best done in Visual Studio Code. We won’t need much. A simple blank canvas will do.

Launch Visual Studio Code, then click on the *File* menu and click *New Text File* . Alternatively, you can press CTRL+N (or CMD+N on macOS). In the resulting window, click *Select a language* and in the drop-down menu that appears pick HTML ( [figure 4](#ch01.xhtml_fig4) ).

![img](Images/fig04.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_4.png) 

The file-type drop-down menu in Visual Studio Code.

Enter the following code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-01-02.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Hello, World!</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <p>
      <script>
         document.write("Hello, World!");
      </script>
      </p>
   </body>
</html>
```

You’ll notice that some of the lines are indented. This is intentional and done by pressing the TAB key. While not essential in HTML and JavaScript, it is recommended, as it helps you visually keep track of your lines of code. See [figure 5](#ch01.xhtml_fig5) for a graphical representation of this concept.

![img](Images/fig05.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_5.png) 

The Hello, World! HTML as seen in Visual Studio Code. Note the lines that illustrate the indented portions of code. For example, everything between the start of the head element ( <head> ) and the end of the head element ( </head> ) is indented, and this convention continues throughout the code—indenting additional times if needed.

Save the file by pressing CTRL+S (CMD+S on macOS), or by going to the *File* menu then clicking *Save* . You’ll be prompted for a name, so make sure you’re in your Source folder and save the file as hello.html .

Next, use File Explorer (Windows) or Finder (macOS) to locate the file. Double-click on the HTML file to launch a browser and load the hello.html file ( [figure 6](#ch01.xhtml_fig6) ).

![img](Images/fig06.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_6.png) 

The Hello, World! message in the browser.

Great, we showed our greeting in the browser. But you might be wondering if we really wrote JavaScript. The file is an HTML file. How is JavaScript involved?

The HTML code we entered is boilerplate; that is, very standard code you’d find on nearly any web page. The document type ( DOCTYPE ), head , meta , title , and related elements define the ***metadata*** of the page. Metadata in the context of web pages describes information about the page. The part of the HTML that displays the contents on the page is contained within <body> and </body> .

In the body, we open a paragraph with <p> , then use <script> to tell the browser we’re about to write some JavaScript code. The browser will interpret everything from <script> to </script> as JavaScript and not HTML. Within this section, we have only one line of JavaScript code:

```
document.write("Hello, World!");
```

This code tells JavaScript to put the text within the quotes directly into the web page. When this code runs, as far as the browser is concerned, this…

```
<body>
   <p>
   <script>
      document.write("Hello, World!");
   </script>
   </p>
</body>
```

…becomes this…

```
<body>
   <p>
      Hello, World!
   </p>
</body>
```

This doesn’t change our HTML file. This is done inside the memory of the browser.

If this had been a web page on a server, as in [go.quickstartguides.com/hello.html](https://go.quickstartguides.com/hello.html) , the browser would have contacted the [clydebankmedia.com](http://clydebankmedia.com) web server and asked it for the hello.html file. The server would deliver the HTML to our browser and run the code just as it did from the hello.html file in our Source folder.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try changing the greeting, perhaps by adding your name instead of using “world”. If you get stuck, you can find the answer in [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) .

### Using Separate HTML and JavaScript Files

You’ve now written JavaScript using two distinct workflows—in the JavaScript console, which is ideal for quick testing, and inside of an HTML file using Visual Studio Code. There’s one more approach we need to cover—using separate HTML and JavaScript files. This is a very typical workflow for a JavaScript developer—especially on the frontend. As such, this will be your primary workflow in this book.

We’ve already used a variation of this workflow in this chapter, but I wanted to summarize it as you’ll be repeating these steps quite a bit as you write JavaScript code.

#### Creating the HTML File

First, we’ll create an HTML file. This will allow us to not only display HTML content but to execute JavaScript and display the results. You’ve already completed this step with the hello.html file, but let’s make a new file called scratch.html in our Source folder that will be our temporary workspace for examples as we work through the book.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

To create a new file in Visual Studio Code, press CTRL+N (CMD+N on macOS), or click on the *File* menu and click *New Text File* .

Enter the following code for our scratch.html file:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-01-03.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Hello, World!</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content"></div>
   </body>
</html>
```

#### Creating the JavaScript File

Next, we create a file with our JavaScript code. Start a new file in Visual Studio Code, but this time, select JavaScript instead of HTML. When prompted for the file, enter the following line:

```
document.getElementById("content").textContent = "Hello, World!"
```

Save the file as scratch.js in the same folder as your scratch.html file.

This code is similar to the document.write() functionality before, but in this case, we specifically reference an HTML element (in this case, the div with the ID named content ). This allows us to overwrite the text inside this with “Hello, World!”

![img](Images/Important_icon.png) 

If you need a quick refresher as to what a div is and IDs associated with HTML elements, please see [appendix I](#appendix01.xhtml) .

We’ll get more into the specifics of how this works, including what the dots do and why getElementById works as it does, but for now, let’s continue.

#### Referencing the JavaScript File in Your HTML

Once the JavaScript file is created, we’ll need to reference it in our HTML file so that the browser will know both are needed in order to display the web page correctly and run our code. In Visual Studio Code, switch to the tab with the scratch.html file ( [figure 7](#ch01.xhtml_fig7) ).

![img](Images/fig07.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_7.png) 

The scratch.html file in Visual Studio Code.
The tab containing the HTML file is circled.

In this file, add the line…

```
<script src="scratch.js"></script>
```

…on the next to last line, right above </html> . Now scratch.html should look like this:

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content"></div>
   </body>
   <script src="scratch.js"></script>
</html>
```

Save the scratch.html file with CTRL+S (CMD+S for macOS).

#### Viewing the Results

To view the results, we open the scratch.html file in the browser by navigating to the Source folder we created and double-clicking on scratch.html .

If all goes well, we’ll see the greeting in the browser. If not, check that your scratch.html and scratch.js files contain the content as listed above, save them, then go to your browser and refresh (CTRL+R on Windows/Linux and CMD+R on macOS).

If this were a real web page hosted on a web server, it would work much the same way it did before with the hello.html file, except the scratch.html file references scratch.js , so the browser would first load scratch.html , see the reference to JavaScript, and request the scratch.js file from the web server.

We’ve now worked through several examples of displaying text in the browser. First, we used console.log to put our message in the JavaScript console. Next, we used alert to display our message as a pop-up. Then, we put our JavaScript directly into the HTML file and enclosed it within <script></script> .

Finally, we created two files, scratch.html and scratch.js , and linked the JavaScript file to the HTML file. This workflow will serve us well throughout most of the book.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

Using <script></script> in HTML is fine for quick testing, but using separate files makes it easier to keep track of things when working on projects for public consumption—especially large projects.

### Using Comments

Comments are an incredibly handy way of telling JavaScript to ignore the current line of text. That might seem like a strange thing to do, but good JavaScript code has plenty of comments. Think of them as reminder notes—not only for your benefit, but for the people who may need to read or edit your code.

To start a single-line comment, use two forward slashes:

```
// A nice greeting
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

Comments can come after a line of code, too:

```
console.log("Hello, World!") // A nice greeting
```

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

I’m generally not a fan of comments that come after a line of code. I prefer them directly above the code they’re documenting, as I find it easier to read. This is just my preference, though, and either way is perfectly acceptable. Do note, however, that if you need to remove the comment, you can’t just highlight the whole line and press DELETE. You must either click with the mouse or use the arrow keys to find the spot where the comment begins and press DELETE.

Additionally, we can define multiline comments with a forward slash and an asterisk. When we’re done with the text we wish to comment, we can reverse the process, inserting an asterisk and then a trailing forward slash:

```
/*
This displays a nice greeting.
Hopefully the user will like it.
*/
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

Comments are also useful to disable a particular line to isolate potential issues when testing code:

```
console.log("This line will run.")
// console.log("This line won't.")
console.log("But this one will.")
```

### A Note About Semicolons

When I first started writing JavaScript, I put a semicolon at the end of each line. I did this because I had previously written in programming languages that required it. I did this for years until a friend was examining some of my code and pointed out that I didn’t need a semicolon at the end of each line. At some level, I knew this, as I had seen plenty of code without it. My initial reaction was to ignore the advice and continue to use semicolons, as it was a long-established routine. However, after giving it some thought, I broke the habit, and now I scarcely use them.

To give an example of what this looks like, consider these two lines:

```
console.log("Hello, World!");
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

If you input them both into your JavaScript console, you’ll notice both lines work correctly. Two greetings are shown.

In fact, the only time you have to use semicolons is when you put two statements on the same line.

```
console.log("Hello, World!"); console.log("And Hello Again!");
```

I’m not anti-semicolon. If you believe they look better, or they provide some value for you, then use them. I’m just explaining why the code found in this book doesn’t use them. It’s one less key to type, and if you write 200 lines of code a day for a year, that’s over 70,000 keystrokes saved. Plus, to me, it looks a bit better without them.

You’ll see lots of JavaScript code with semicolons, and my position is slightly in the minority. The internet is full of battles in the Great Semicolon Wars™, and I find little value in getting deep into that kind of debate. While I don’t use them, I don’t push this position with any kind of zeal.

Whatever you choose, though, I would be consistent and stick to it—especially within the same program or project. If you mix these two standards, it will still work, but it can look a bit odd.

![img](Images/Caution_icon.png) 

JavaScript files can undergo ***minification*** to improve page load speeds. This process, among other optimizations, removes unnecessary spaces and line breaks. If you’re not using semicolons consistently in your code, minification might change the intended behavior due to JavaScript’s automatic semicolon insertion feature. If you want to minify your code, I recommend you end your statements with semicolons.

[![img](Images/f0024-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-1) 

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop Website – Getting Started

To get started on our JavaScript journey, it will be helpful to have a simple website to work with. If you have a website of your own, you can modify it as we go with the techniques you learn in this book. But, to ensure every reader is covered, we’re going to start with a basic yet functional coffee shop website.

The site is in four files: index.html , style.css , shop.js , and coffee-cup.svg . I recommend creating a folder in your Source folder called coffee-shop to store these files so they’re kept separate from the files you’ll create in the other exercises in this book.

The first three files can be manually entered, but typing a SVG file by hand can be a bit annoying, so if you like, you can download the file(s) from [https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide](https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide) .

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The SVG file type is not an option in Visual Studio Code at time of publication, so if you want to copy/paste its content, type it in without selecting a language, then save it as an .svg file using “Save As”.

Typing HTML and CSS by hand won’t necessarily help your JavaScript learning journey, so don’t worry about copying and pasting this code.

After you load the site in Chrome by double-clicking index.html in your Source folder, you’ll see our lovely but minimalistic coffee shop website. If you open the JavaScript console, you’ll see the following message:

```
ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!
```

This comes from shop.js . For now, shop.js only has two lines, and one of them is a comment:

```
// For now, display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")
```

And since the shop.js file is included at the bottom of index.html via a script element, whatever JavaScript code is in that file is run when you load the page.

index.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-index_html.png)

Chapter Recap

## | 2 |

## Using Variables

Chapter Overview

» Variables are containers for values

» There are multiple ways to declare variables

» Arrays are variables that hold multiple values

In the last chapter, we displayed the text “Hello, World!” in the browser. While this is useful, it doesn’t allow for any customization of the message. For example, if we wanted to say “Hello, Robert!” or use any other name, we would have to enter that name directly into the code.

If we want to greet our visitors after asking their name, we need a place to store that name until we display the greeting. Fortunately, JavaScript has a solution—variables.

***Variables*** are containers for values. These values can either be static (i.e., stay the same for the duration of the program execution) or change depending on the instructions in the program. You may remember variables from algebra class, but they aren’t specifically related to math. In fact, a variable can store a lot of different types of data.

The simplest types of variables are ***strings*** . Strings are pieces of text, or *strings* of characters that are kept in the computer’s memory. We can also store numbers and all other kinds of data, but for now, let’s start with a string.

### Using Strings

JavaScript makes it easy to assign a value to a variable. All we have to do is list the name of the variable and its value. If it’s a string, we enclose the text in quotes.

Let’s type this in the JavaScript console. Recall that you can press F12 in Chrome (or right click, then click *Inspect* , then click on the Console tab.)

```
firstName = "Robert"
```

Even though I’ve specified my name here, feel free to type your name. This creates a variable called firstName and gives it the value “Robert” (or your name).

I could have named the variable first , or anything else, but it’s a good idea to make your variable names meaningful.

Variable names in JavaScript are case sensitive, and it’s considered best practice to use ***camel case*** when naming variables. Camel case describes the “hump” within the name of a multi-word variable name. The first word in the variable name isn’t capitalized, but the first letter of subsequent words is capitalized: for example, firstName . If the variable name is a single word, it is left all lowercase.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Why didn’t I name the variable name ? Generally, you should use the simplest variable name, but, in this case, name too closely resembles window.name , which is the name of the current window. We’ll explore this functionality later, but just know that some words are not available for use as variable names.

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

For a full list of “Reserved Names”, please access your free Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript

Now, let’s make use of that variable with the following command:

```
console.log("Hello, " + firstName)
```

When you run this, we get:

```
Hello, Robert
```

This is a nice greeting, and we’ll get to know how it works in just a moment, but there’s just a little detail that’s bothering me. We need an exclamation mark at the end. Why? Well, it just looks better that way.

```
console.log("Hello, " + firstName + "!")
```

Now, when we press ENTER, we see:

```
Hello, Robert!
```

All is well again. Let’s investigate why that worked.

The original line of code to display the greeting looked like this:

```
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

In our new version, we removed the World portion and closed out the string, then added a plus sign and then the name of our variable (in this case, firstName ). Then we closed the parentheses. The plus symbol tells JavaScript to “add” the two strings together. I put add in quotes because we’re not really adding them in an arithmetic sense; rather, we’re combining them. This combining of strings in programming is called ***concatenation*** .

### Template Literals

ES6 introduced a new feature for using variables called ***template literals*** (also called ***template strings*** ). Template literals allow you to easily embed variables and evaluations (more on those later in this chapter) into strings. Recall our previous example of using the firstName variable in a console log:

```
console.log("Hello, " + firstName + "!")
```

With template literals, we can use this approach:

```
console.log(`Hello, ${firstName}!`)
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The character immediately before the H in Hello and immediately after the exclamation mark is a backtick, usually just left of the number 1 key on the keyboard.

With template literals, the value in firstName is substituted in the string. The ${ and } symbols do not appear in the console display, as they only serve to tell JavaScript that a variable name follows.

To my eyes, template literals are easier to understand—especially when multiple variables must be embedded. Consider this example:

```
console.log("Hello, " + firstName + " " + middleName + " " + lastName + "!")
```

That’s a lot of plus symbols and unsightly strings containing just one space. Here’s the template literal alternative:

```
console.log(`Hello, ${firstName} ${middleName} ${lastName}!`)
```

This results in fewer characters typed and is less error prone. I can’t tell you how many times I’ve forgotten a plus sign between string segments.

Template literals also let us more easily define multiline strings. A multiline string continues until a second backtick, so this is useful for creating very long strings.

```
myReallyLongString = `This is a really long string.
So long in fact that it spans multiple lines.
Wow! What a string!`
```

Throughout this book, I’ll use both template literals and plus sign concatenation methods, because you will encounter both in your JavaScript work. You can use one or the other, or both.

### Obtaining Input from the Console

Now that we have displayed custom strings in the console, let’s ask the user for their name and then print the personalized greeting. There are several ways to go about this, so let’s look at the simplest first—a simple pop-up prompt.

Let’s clear out our scratch.js file and add the following code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-02-01.png) 

```
firstName = prompt("Please enter your name:")
alert(`Hello, ${firstName}`)
```

Save the file, then open scratch.html in Chrome by navigating to the file in your file browser and double-clicking on it. Alternatively, if you already have the scratch.html file open, you can simply refresh the browser. When you do so, the scratch.js file will be loaded, and the above two lines will be executed by JavaScript.

You should be prompted for a name, then, when you enter a value and press ENTER, you will see an alert pop-up with the personalized greeting ( [figure 8](#ch02.xhtml_fig8) ).

![img](Images/fig08.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_8.png) 

The prompt and result after entering “Robert”.

### Collecting a String with a Form

While this approach works, you’ll almost never see data gathered from the user this way, for a variety of reasons. Pop-ups can be jarring and annoying to a user, and they provide no way to visually style the experience for the user. We can only control the text a prompt uses to ask for a value.

Most of the time, data is gathered from the user in a ***form*** . A form on a web page is an HTML element that enables us to use HTML elements like input text boxes. These provide a place for the user to type information ( [figure 9](#ch02.xhtml_fig9) ).

![img](Images/fig09.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_9.png) 

A simple input text box in HTML.

In our scratch.html file, let’s place the following code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-02-02.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <form>
            <input id="firstName" type="text" size="30"
               placeholder="Please enter your name" size="30">
            <button type="button" id="submit">Submit</button>
         </form>
         <div id="greeting"></div>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="scratch.js"></script>
</html>
```

In this HTML code, we add a form element with an input text box named firstName , then a button with the ID of submit . We also add a div with the ID of greeting to receive our message, although it’s blank for now.

In our scratch.js file, enter the following code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-02-03.png) 

```
firstNameTextBox = document.getElementById("firstName")
submitButton = document.getElementById("submit")
greetingContainer = document.getElementById("greeting")
submitButton.addEventListener("click", function() {
   greetingContainer.textContent = "Hello, " + firstNameTextBox.value + "!"
})
```

Save the file, then view it in your browser. You should see the same text box for the user’s name that you saw in [figure 9](#ch02.xhtml_fig9) . Enter a name, then click *Submit* . When you do, you'll see the greeting shown immediately below the input box.

There are several new concepts in the JavaScript code file scratch.js , so let’s go through them line by line. Before we do, I should note that there are some ideas that we’ll be glossing over for the time being, like functions and events. Don’t worry—we’ll be covering those in more detail in later chapters, so for now, let’s focus on what the code does when we click *Submit* .

In the first line, we create a variable named firstNameTextBox and set it equal to the result returned by document.getElementById(“firstName”) . The firstNameTextBox variable becomes a sort of reference to the firstName input text box.

On line 2, we do the same for the submitButton variable. And, on line 3, the greetingContainer variable becomes a reference to the div with the ID of greeting .

The last three lines relate to handling an event that happens on the page. The first line in this final block uses the addEventListener functionality of the submitButton to register a piece of code to run when the “click” event happens—in other words, when the user clicks the button. This code lies dormant until the button is clicked, and then springs to life, taking the greetingContainer and changing its textContent to give our greeting ( [figure 10](#ch02.xhtml_fig10) ).

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

The snippet 02-03.js uses string concatenation – adding strings together – instead of template literals. If you're feeling adventurous, try rewriting the following line to use a template literal instead.

greetingContainer.textContent = "Hello, " + firstNameTextBox.value + "!"

If you get stuck, reference [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) for the answer.

![img](Images/fig10.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_10.png) 

But there’s something else going on with this line, specifically toward the end:

```
greetingContainer.textContent = "Hello, " + firstNameTextBox.value + "!"
```

The firstNameTextBox is a reference to the input text box, and the value of this text box, that is, what text is placed there by the user, is obtainable by placing a dot then value after it. When this line is processed, firstNameTextBox.value becomes a string containing whatever text was entered into the text box by the user.

### Evaluating Evaluation

When firstNameTextBox.value is used to get the value of a string, we say it evaluates to a string containing whatever text was entered into the text box. The act of ***evaluating*** code means to run a piece of code and obtain a value.

Let’s examine the concept of evaluation more. Think of this…

```
"Hello, " + firstNameTextBox.value + "!"
```

…becoming this after evaluation…

```
"Hello, " + "Robert" + "!"
```

…and that resolves into this…

```
"Hello, Robert!"
```

So, after that is evaluated, the entire line resolves to this:

```
greetingContainer.textContent = "Hello, Robert!"
```

When I say resolves, it’s not as though the actual JavaScript code is changing. Rather, as JavaScript steps through your code, it makes these evaluations to derive values of things it doesn’t yet know.

Your brain does this kind of evaluation, too. For example, if I gave you the following math problem…

10 × 4 + 8

…you would first multiply ten by four. In your head, it would look like this:

40 + 8

Then, you would add 8 to 40 to obtain the answer of 48\.

Another way to look at evaluation is to think of it as simplification. In math, we call it reducing, but it’s the same concept. When we evaluate code, we resolve all its external references to unknown values, converting them to known values and performing the original intent of the code.

### Using Numbers

Now that we’ve discussed using strings in JavaScript, it’s time to work with numbers. Both strings and numbers are variables, but there are some differences.

First, let’s look at some examples of numbers in JavaScript.

```
a = 1
b = 3
c = 6
```

As you might expect, we can perform math operations on numbers.

```
d = a + b + c
```

We can mix numbers and variables that contain numbers.

```
d = 100 + a
```

You can run these lines of code directly in the JavaScript console and you’ll see the following results:

```
> a = 1
> b = 3
> c = 6
> d = a + b + c
10
> d = 100 + a
101
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The lines with the > symbol indicate the prompt where you enter the code.

Numbers can be converted into strings:

```
a = 1
b = 1
c = a + b
console.log("The answer to a + b is " + c.toString())
```

In this example, we first give a and b the value of 1\. Then, we make a new variable, c , by evaluating a + b . When this happens, c becomes 2, since 1 + 1 evaluates to 2\.

The console.log function displays strings. Both lines…

```
console.log("The answer to a + b is " + c.toString())
console.log("The answer to a + b is " + c)
```

…will yield the same result:

```
The answer to a + b is 2
The answer to a + b is 2
```

In this case, console.log is converting the number in variable c to a string before displaying it, so technically we don’t have to use toString() in this case. Nevertheless, I wanted to introduce the concept.

In that same train of thought, we can convert strings to numbers:

```
a = "1"
b = parseInt(a, 10)
```

In this example, a is a string with the number 1 in it, and b becomes a number because of parseInt converting a , the string, into a number. It’s worth taking a moment to emphasize that a string with a number is not the same thing as a number because a string is treated as text, even if it contains numbers. You’ll know a value is a string because it is enclosed in quotes. But, if the string contains only numbers, parseInt can extract those numbers and create a variable that *is* a number (i.e., a variable that can participate in math operations).

The second argument of parseInt , in this case 10, is the base, or radix, which defines the numerical system. 10 just means we’re working in ordinary base 10, as opposed to, say, binary (base 2) or hexadecimal (base 16). Specifying this helps prevent conversion issues.

The “ Int ” portion of parseInt stands for ***integer*** . An integer is a whole number, either negative or positive (or zero). We’ll talk more about integers, numbers, and math operations in JavaScript in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) .

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try some other simple calculations in the JavaScript console.

### Evaluating in Template Literals

You can evaluate an expression inside a template string within ${} . The string representation of the result of the expression is then inserted into the string.

If you enter this code into your JavaScript console...

```
console.log('The value of pi is somewhat close to ${355/113}.')
```

...the following will be displayed as the result:

```
The value of pi is somewhat close to 3.1415929203539825.
```

The number 355 was divided by 113 in the evaluation between the curly braces, and then the result, 3.1415929203539825, is inserted into the string.

Here’s another way to accomplish the very same thing:

```
piString = `The value of pi is somewhat close to ${355/113}.`
console.log(piString)
```

The only difference here is that the piString variable is defined using the text we provided and substituted in the evaluation (355 divided by 113). Then console.log shows the value of piString .

### Declaring Variables

Up until this point, we’ve been defining variables by simply giving them a value.

```
a = 1
b = "Hello!"
```

This is the simplest way of establishing variables. These are called ***undeclared variables*** because we haven’t declared the ***scope*** of the variable. Defining a variable’s scope is how we tell JavaScript what parts of the code can access and modify the variable. We’ll explore scope in depth in [chapter 5](#ch05.xhtml) , but for now, let’s examine this topic further.

There are four ways to define a variable in JavaScript:

#### Undeclared

With undeclared variables, the scope is global, meaning that the variables are assigned as soon as that part of the code is run and every line of JavaScript code on the web page can then make use of this variable.

We’ve been using this method so far in our discussion of variables, but in practice this is discouraged because it can make it difficult to find issues in the code. If a variable is global, it can accidentally be overwritten, which can interfere with an operation later in the code.

#### Var

Before ES6, you most often saw variables declared like this:

```
var a = 1
var b = "Hello"
```

While this is commonly found in code (especially older code), it can be problematic because it doesn’t strictly confine the variable’s scope. This is important when we discuss functions in [chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml) .

Now that we have the ES6 standard, I advise against using var unless you need your code to work on very old browsers. Instead, use let (see below).

#### Const

Declaring a variable with const makes the variable ***immutable*** . An immutable variable cannot be modified.

```
const a = 1
const b = "Hello"
```

You can set the value initially as we do here (e.g., a is 1 and b is “Hello”), but if we try to give the variable a new value…

```
const b = "Hello"
b = "Hello again!"
```

…we’ll get an error:

```
Assignment to constant variable.
```

This is useful to catch bugs. A ***bug*** is a problem with your code. If you set a variable as a constant and it should never change, an error will occur if you make a mistake and try to assign a new value to it.

As for scope, const will be in the scope of the code that declares the variable, rather than global by default. We’ll talk more about this when we cover functions in [chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml) .

Try to use const as often as you can unless you need to modify a variable after its initial declaration. In that case, use let .

#### Let

The let declaration is your go-to option for variables that will be modified at some point.

```
let a = 1
let b = "Hello"
```

As with const , let is in the scope of the code that declares the variable.

Right now, we’re working with simple scopes, so we don’t need to discuss this topic in depth at this point. We will be revisiting scope as we dig deeper into how variables work in JavaScript. Nevertheless, I wanted to cover the basics, and to explain why you’ll be seeing me use const and let in examples from this point forward.

[![img](Images/f0044-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-2) 

### Arrays

***Arrays*** are variables that hold multiple values. Let’s look at two examples:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
let lotteryNumbers = [1, 4, 17, 19, 24, 7]
```

In the first line, we create an array with a list of items we need at the grocery store. In the second example, we list six lottery numbers. In these examples, we aren’t mixing strings and numbers, but that would be completely acceptable.

```
let numbersAndStrings = ["Los Angeles", 364, "Houston", 942]
```

We can access those values via their ***index*** , or numerical position inside the array.

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("The second item on the shopping list is " + shoppingList[1])
```

The square brackets after the shopping list enclosing the index number will give us the item we’re looking for. But wait…why did we use an index position 1 to retrieve the second item on the list?

Strange as it might seem, most programming languages, JavaScript included, start counting their index at 0 instead of 1\. So, to access the first item, we would use index position 0, like this:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("The first item on the shopping list is " + shoppingList[0])
```

And the third item would be retrieved with this:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("The third item on the shopping list is " + shoppingList[2])
```

Arrays have a lot of functionality beyond a collection of variables. We can create a string from the items of an array with join :

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(", "))
```

This code produces:

```
Shopping List: Milk, Eggs, Cheese, Bread
```

The empty parentheses would give the default version—a list separated by a single comma with no space. Character(s) in parentheses and in quotes after join are used to join each item in the array. We could use a simple empty space if we like:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(" "))
```

This code displays:

```
Shopping List: Milk Eggs Cheese Bread
```

We can also add an item to the end of an array:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
console.log("Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(" "))
console.log("Oops, we forgot the oranges!")
shoppingList.push("Oranges")
console.log("Our New Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(" "))
```

It’s easy to change an existing item in the array:

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
shoppingList[0] = "Almond Milk"
console.log("Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(" "))
```

If we run this code, we see:

```
Shopping List: Almond Milk Eggs Cheese Bread
```

JavaScript did precisely what we asked it to, but that isn’t quite what we wanted. This data would be better separated by something other than a space, because the item “Almond Milk” has two words. We can fix that by joining with a comma (and a space):

```
let shoppingList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
shoppingList[0] = "Almond Milk"
console.log("Shopping List: " + shoppingList.join(", "))
```

This produces:

```
Shopping List: Almond Milk, Eggs, Cheese, Bread
```

Much better!

There are many more interesting things you can do with arrays. We’ve just scratched the surface. But don’t worry, we’ll explore these items throughout the rest of the book.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try adding additional items to the shopping list—or creating a list of something else entirely. You might try your favorite foods or bands. If you get stuck, you can see another example in [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) .

Chapter Recap

» A variable is a container in memory to store a value.

» A string is a type of variable that contains text. Numbers can be converted into strings. Some strings can be converted into numbers.

» Variables have scope in JavaScript and can be declared multiple ways, depending on the situation.

» Arrays are variables that contain multiple values. They can be accessed via an index.

## | 3 |

## Controlling Program Flow in JavaScript

Chapter Overview

» Comparisons help decide program flow

» Logical comparisons evaluate multiple conditions

» Loops let you repeat code

The path JavaScript takes through a program won’t always be a straight line. For code to do something more than a simple, non-repeatable task, it must make decisions based on ***comparisons*** . A comparison is made when we check the value of a variable or the result of an operation against another variable—or known value. In other words, we are asking if a variable is equal to (or greater than, less than, etc.) another value.

Additionally, as a program runs, it often performs the same operation multiple times in structures called loops. Loops work hand in hand with logical comparisons because we must check at each pass through the loop to see whether the loop should continue, or the program should move on to the next operation.

We’ll get into loops in a bit, but for now, let’s look at logical comparisons.

### Comparisons

The most common way to compare values in JavaScript is to use the if statement. First, let’s look at a simple example, then walk through exactly how comparisons work.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-01.png) 

```
let price = 4.95
if (price == 4.95) {
   console.log("The price is 4.95.")
} else {
   console.log("The price is something else.")
}
```

Here, we set the price variable to a decimal value of 4.95\. In the next line, our code tells JavaScript the following:

1\. Does the code within the parentheses (price == 4.95) evaluate to be true?

2\. If so, run the code within the curly braces.

3\. If not, run the code within the next set of curly braces, after else .

First, let’s look at the evaluation right after if . First, we list the variable, price , then we use the == (double equal sign) ***operator*** to compare it with 4.95. An operator is a character (or group of characters) that represents an operation, such as a comparison. We use the double equal sign to differentiate from the assignment operator—that is, the single equal sign that sets a variable to a value.

The code within the curly braces for both the true portion of the if statement and the false portion (the false portion being the code within the else braces) can be one line or multiple lines. And we don’t have to have an else portion, either.

```
let price = 3.95
if (price == 4.95) {
   console.log("The price is 4.95.")
}
```

In this example, a message is never displayed because the price wasn’t 4.95\. If we had the else portion as before, it would have shown a message.

We can compare other situations than equality, and even chain together multiple evaluations. I’ve assembled a table ( [table 1](#ch03.xhtml_tab1) ) that shows various comparative operators (operators that compare values).

![img](Images/table-1.png) 

| Operator | Example | Description |
| == | ( a == 1 ) | Check if both values are equal. |
| === | ( a === 1 ) | Check if both values are equal and the same type. |
| != | ( a != 1 ) | Check if values are not equal. |
| !== | ( a !== 1 ) | Check if values or types are not equal. |
| < | ( a < 1 ) | Check if the first value is less than the second. |
| > | ( a > 1 ) | Check if the first value is greater than the second. |
| <= | ( a <= 1 ) | Check if the first value is less than or equal to the second. |
| >= | ( a >= 1 ) | Check if the first value is greater than or equal to the second. |

### Types and Values

In addition to the regular comparative operators, we have === and !== which compare the *type* of value in addition to the value itself. Here are some examples:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-02.png) 

```
let a = 1
let b = "1"

if (a === b) {
   console.log("a and b are both equal and the same type")
}

if (a == b) {
   console.log("a and b are both 1")
}
```

If we run this code in the JavaScript console, we get:

```
a and b are both 1
```

JavaScript compared the value and type in the first if statement and found that the type didn’t match, as one value is a string and one value is a number. In the second if statement, the type of the values wasn’t being compared, only the value.

The != and !== operators work opposite their == and === counterparts.

```
let a = 1
let b = "2"

if (a !== b) {
   console.log("a and b are not equal or are of different types")
}

if (a != b) {
   console.log("a and b are not equal even if we ignore their types")
}
```

This code produces:

```
a and b are not equal or are of different types
a and b are not equal even if we ignore their types
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

If you receive an error in the JavaScript console when running the code above related to the redeclaration of the variable a , simply refresh the browser and try again. JavaScript is trying to prevent you from redeclaring the variable a in the same scope. Refreshing the page completely clears all variables previously defined by resetting the environment.

In the first if statement, a and b weren’t equal values, so this satisfied the first requirement of this operator, which would be enough to make the condition evaluate to true. (As it happens, the second requirement was also met, because one was a number and one was a string, thus they were different types.) Because the condition evaluated to be true, the first message was displayed.

In the second if statement, the value of a didn’t equal b , so the second message was displayed.

### Greater Than and Less Than

The greater than and less than operators are useful if we want to compare two numbers. Let’s look at an example.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-03.png) 

```
let a = 5
let b = 10
let c = 100

if (b < c) {
   console.log(`${b} is less than ${c}`)
}

if (c > b) {
   console.log(`${c} is greater than ${b}`)
}

if (a <= 5) {
   console.log(`${a} is less than or equal to 5`)
}
```

In this example, we first define three variables. Then we run through three different comparisons and execute code if the evaluations are true. In all three cases, the evaluations are true: b is less than c , c is greater than b , and a is less than or equal to 5.

### Making Logical Comparisons

***Logical comparisons*** let us compare the evaluation of multiple comparisons at once. Let’s take a closer look at these operators.

#### AND

The AND operator, styled as && in code, tests that two or more statements all evaluate to true:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-04.png) 

```
let a = 1
let b = 2

if (a == 1 && b == 2) {
   console.log("a is 1 and b is 2")
}

if (a == 1 && b == 2 && a != b) {
   console.log("a is 1, b is 2, and they are not equal")
}
```

In the first example, the message is printed because a is equal to 1 *and* b is equal to 2\. Both conditions must evaluate to true for the overall && operation to evaluate to true.

In the second example, all three statements are true. There are three evaluations here, but there can be more. No matter how many statements are chained together, all of them must evaluate to true for the if statement code to run.

This is helpful when you need to confirm that multiple variables are equal to certain values:

```
let doorPosition = "shut"
let trunkPosition = "closed"
let seatBeltStatus = "on"

if (doorPosition == "shut"
   && trunkPosition == "closed"
   && seatBeltStatus == "on") {
      console.log("Let's roll!")
}
```

In this example, I put a new line between each condition within the chained AND comparison. I did this for two reasons. First, I wanted to demonstrate this common practice to make each condition easier to read. Second, because it would run off the page otherwise. I mention this because if you chain multiple statements together, they can easily end up extending past the point that requires your editor to scroll. This is inconvenient and completely avoidable. JavaScript isn’t picky about the indentation of code.

#### OR

The OR operator, styled as || (two pipe symbols—often typed with SHIFT+BACKSLASH) in code, tests that at least one of the statements evaluates to true.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-05.png) 

```
let a = 1
let b = 2

if (a == 1 || b == 3) {
   console.log("At least one of these conditions is true.")
}

if (a == 1 || b == 2 || a == b) {
   console.log("At least one of the three conditions is true.")
}
```

In the first example, the message is printed because a is equal to 1, satisfying the condition that at least one of the evaluations is true.

In the second example, two of the three statements happen to be true; but the || operation succeeded as soon as one of the evaluations was true, so the second and third evaluations didn’t matter.

As with AND, you can chain as many statements as you like.

A common use for this operation is to check that a variable is at least equal to one of a set of pre-defined values.

```
let a = 1

if (a == 1 || a == 12 || a == 32) {
   console.log("This message will be displayed.")
}
```

If you prefer, and it makes it simpler to read, you can isolate each evaluation in parentheses using this technique: (a == 1 || a == 12 || a == 32) becomes ((a == 1) || (a == 12) || (a == 32)) . They are functionally identical.

#### NOT

The ! operator lets you test if something is not true (in other words, it is *falsy* ). You may have read the word “falsy” several times wondering if it is a typo, but it isn’t. By “falsy,” I mean that it is literally false , 0 , an empty string, null , undefined , or Not a Number ( NaN ). NaN is a value that is the result of a nonsensical math calculation, like the square root of negative one, or converting a non-numeric string to a number.

![img](Images/FunFact_icon.png) 

In JavaScript, dividing a positive or negative number by zero results in a return value of Infinity or -Infinity respectively—not NaN , which you might expect. (But dividing zero by zero does give NaN .)

I know we’re discussing several concepts about negation that might not immediately seem applicable, but I wanted to be precise about what the NOT operator does because JavaScript is a bit more verbose (and at times, esoteric) than some other programming languages when it comes to evaluations and comparisons.

Before negating the comparison, it converts the evaluation to a ***Boolean*** . Consider the following:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-06.png) 

```
let done = false

if (done) {
   console.log("All done here.")
}
```

There are two things to note in this example. First, instead of giving the done variable a string or number value, we’re giving it a Boolean value. A Boolean value is either true or false and needs no further evaluation.

If at some point during our program, we can set done = true , then the message would be displayed.

```
let done = false

// Do some things

done = true

if (done) {
   console.log("All done here.")
}
```

Using this technique, we can easily test if something is not true:

```
let done = false

// Do some things

if (! done) {
   console.log("Still not done.")
}

// Do some more things

done = true

if (done) {
   console.log("All done.")
}
```

In this case, when the first if statement is processed, the message will display because done is *not* true. Later, when we set done to true, the second if statement passes and displays the final message.

While this is a trivial example, this kind of status is useful in quite a few situations, especially in loops. We’ll use this quite a few times in the book and in our Clydebank Coffee Shop website code.

For your reference, here’s a table of logical operators ( [table 2](#ch03.xhtml_tab2) ).

![img](Images/table-2.png) 

![Images](Images/t0057-01.jpg)

### Using Switch

A switch comes in handy if a variable can contain more than a few possible values and you need to execute different lines of code for each of those functions. It acts like a switchboard, directing control of the program based on the value of a variable.

First, let’s look at an example in the JavaScript console, then we’ll repeat the example in HTML so you can see how the two tie together.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-07.png) 

```
let color = "blue"

switch (color) {
   case "red":
      console.log("The color is red.")
      break
   case "orange":
      console.log("The color is orange.")
      break
   case "purple":
      console.log("The color is purple.")
      break
   default:
      console.log("I'm not sure what color it is.")
}
```

First, we define a variable called color and set it to a string equal to “blue”. Next, we use the switch statement and specify the variable to use (in this case, color ). Curly braces start and end the switch statement, and then we use case statements to define what happens when color is certain values.

For each case statement, we provide a matching value (e.g., “red”, “orange”, “purple”, etc.), and, after a colon, the indented code is run when the variable matches the specified value. Code runs until it hits the break statement.

Since color didn’t match any of the case statements, the default statement was matched instead, so if we run this code in the JavaScript console, we’ll see:

```
I'm not sure what color it is.
```

Let’s expand on this concept by incorporating this into an HTML form. We’ll even throw in a splash of CSS for fun.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

You can find a refresher on HTML and CSS in [appendix I](#appendix01.xhtml) .

In a new file called switch.html , let’s place the following code:

switch.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <link rel="stylesheet" href="switch.css">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <form>
            <select id="colorSelector" name="colorSelector">
               <option value="red">Red</option>
               <option value="orange">Orange</option>
               <option value="yellow">Yellow</option>
               <option value="green">Green</option>
               <option value="blue">Blue</option>
               <option value="indigo">Indigo</option>
               <option value="violet">Violet</option>
            </select>
         </form>
         <div id="colorSquare"></div>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="switch.js"></script>
</html>
```

We also need to create a file called switch.css with our stylesheet:

switch.css

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch_css.png) 

```
#colorSquare {
   width: 20em;
   height: 5em;
   background-color: red;
   border: 0.1em solid gray;
   border-radius: 0.75em;
   margin-top: 1em;
}
```

Last, but not least, we’ll create the JavaScript file called switch.js .

switch.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch_js.png) 

```
colorSelector = document.getElementById("colorSelector")
colorSquare = document.getElementById("colorSquare")

colorSelector.addEventListener("change", function() {
   let color = colorSelector.value
   colorSquare.style.backgroundColor = color
   console.log(color)
})
```

If you open the file switch.html in your browser, you’ll be treated to our new spiffy color selector ( [figure 11](#ch03.xhtml_fig11) ).

![img](Images/fig11.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_11.png) 

The result of our color selector code as shown in the browser.

Let’s examine how this works. First, we define the HTML file. There are several important things to note here:

» We reference switch.css in the header so the stylesheet file will be loaded.

» Within the content div, we create a form and a select box filled with the colors of the spectrum. The ID of the select box is colorSelector .

» We create an empty div called colorSquare .

» At the bottom of the file, we reference our switch.js file.

In the switch.css file, we simply define the CSS necessary to style our color display square with the ID of colorSquare . Since the first color in the select box is red, we set the initial background color to red as well. This way, the initial value in the select box (red) matches the color in the colorSquare when the page is first loaded.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

It is customary to use camel case in JavaScript code, so I carry this tradition over to HTML code as well when naming IDs and other nameable elements. You certainly don’t have to adopt this style, but it can be helpful when interfacing JavaScript with HTML.

The JavaScript code file named switch.js first sets the variables colorSelector and colorSquare , to reference the select box and div respectively. Then, it adds an event listener to the colorSelector box, binding to the change event. This event is triggered anytime the user makes a change to the select box. When this happens, a variable named color is set to the value of the select box. We then set the backgroundColor of the style associated with the colorSquare div to the color variable. Finally, we use console.log to display the color the user selected.

The last step is for us to make sure things are working correctly. A typical user on the web won’t notice this, since most people don’t check the JavaScript console on the pages they view. However, if you open the JavaScript console and watch as you change colors, you’ll notice the color name displayed as we change the value in the select box.

We could simplify the switch.js code a bit. This…

```
colorSelector = document.getElementById("colorSelector")
colorSquare = document.getElementById("colorSquare")

colorSelector.addEventListener("change", function() {
   let color = colorSelector.value
   colorSquare.style.backgroundColor = color
   console.log(color)
})
```

…works just as well as this…

```
colorSelector = document.getElementById("colorSelector")
colorSquare = document.getElementById("colorSquare")

colorSelector.addEventListener("change", function() {
   colorSquare.style.backgroundColor = colorSelector.value
})
```

While they both work, the code with the console.log message provides a bit of information for us to make sure things are working correctly. I would normally first write the code with the console method to make sure the value was being set correctly, then once I tested it, I’d simplify it to use the simpler version.

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

If you are well-versed in CSS, you may have noticed that I’m not passing the typical color values like #FF0000 for red and #0000FF for blue. Since the colors are all part of the named color scheme in CSS, I simply used the text name in this example. However, I could achieve the same result (and provide even more color variations) by specifying a color red-green-blue (RGB) value.

```
<select id="colorSelector" name="colorSelector">
   <option value="#FF0000">Red</option>
   <option value="#FFA500">Orange</option>
   <option value="#FFFF00">Yellow</option>
   <option value="#008000">Green</option>
```
