```
   <option value="#0000FF">Blue</option>
   <option value="#4B0082">Indigo</option>
   <option value="#EE82EE">Violet</option>
</select>
```

### Using Loops

Loops instruct JavaScript to repeat a piece of code a specified number of times. An extremely simplistic way of doing that without loops would be to copy and paste the code as many times as we need.

```
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
console.log("Hello, World!")
```

This code will display our greeting 10 times. But this is extremely inefficient for several reasons. First, we must copy and paste the code each time we want to use it. That is fine for a few commands, but what if we need to process something 1,000 times? Secondly, if we need to change something later, we must edit each line. This could quickly become tiresome and error-prone. Finally, once the program starts to run, we have no control over how many times this code will execute.

Fortunately, loops provide a better way to handle this situation.

#### For Loops

For loops are an ideal solution for when we need to run code multiple times. An ***iteration*** is a single execution through a loop—one spin of the wheel, so to speak.

```
for (let i = 0; i <= 10; i++) {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

I’ll explain each part of this code, but for now, go ahead and type this code into your JavaScript console. You’ll see the following:

```
( 11 ) Hello, World!
```

That’s strange! You would expect the greeting to be displayed 10 times. Instead, it’s just displayed once. However, the number 11 is displayed in a circle next to the message. This is Chrome’s way of telling us that the message displayed 11 times.

But wait…why 11? We specified 10, right? To understand why this happened, let’s take a close look at the for statement.

```
for (let i = 0; i <= 10; i++) {
```

Three things happen inside the for statement within the parentheses. First, we define a variable named i and set it to 0\. We don’t have to use i . It could be a , b , hello , purple , or widgetCount —anything we want! At the end of this segment, a semicolon is required.

![img](Images/FunFact_icon.png) 

The variable name i is typically used by tradition. There are multiple explanations for this, including i being short for “iterator” or “index”. However, in FORTRAN, the first widely used programming language, the variable i was the first variable name that defaulted to an integer. I believe the tradition started with FORTRAN, but was reinforced by the fact that i is also the first letter of integer, index, and iterator.

The second portion, in this example i <= 10 , contains the evaluation that must be true for the loop to continue. In the first iteration, i will be less than or equal to 10, and will continue to be less than or equal to 10 until i exceeds 10\. The third portion, again separated with a semicolon, is the operation that will be applied to i on each pass of the loop. In this case, we’re using i++ , which is a shortcut for i = i + 1 . This adds 1 to i with each iteration (pass) through the loop.

The code that is looped is contained within the curly braces. This can be one or many lines.

Now that we’ve toured the for loop, let’s investigate why the message printed 11 times instead of 10\. To do this, we’ll walk through each step as though we’re JavaScript, describing what happens as we go.

Here’s the code:

```
for (let i = 0; i <= 10; i++) {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

Here’s the walkthrough:

1\. The variable i is created and set to 0.

2\. The condition, in this case i <= 10 , evaluates to true, so the loop proceeds.

3\. The first greeting is printed.

4\. We loop back to step 2, incrementing i by 1 with each loop.

5\. When i is 11, the for loop ends because i is no longer 10 or less.

If we want the message to be displayed exactly 10 times, we can use this simple solution:

```
for (let i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

Here, we’ve changed the condition from i being less than or equal to 10 to i being less than 10\. This way, when i becomes 10, the loop will stop.

You may be wondering why we’re starting at 0 instead of 1\. Computers start counting from 0, not 1\. It’s a natural side effect of the way memory and internal counters are organized for efficiency and performance.

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

You might find it odd that indexes start counting from 0\. This is not specific to JavaScript—most programming languages use 0 as their starting place. Remember when we discussed arrays in [chapter 2](#ch02.xhtml) . When we create an array, the interpreter assigns a memory address (a position in memory) for it so that it can be accessed later in your code. If you supply an index position, like 3 in groceryList[3] , it adds this number to the memory address to get the location in memory that contains the specific item we want.

![img](Images/fig12.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_12.png) 

An array for a grocery list in memory. The headings in the vertical column show an absolute position in memory, and the numbers along the horizontal top row represent the length of the string.

Let’s pretend the address of the list is 8000\. To find item number 3 ( groceryList[3] ), we take the index number of 3 and calculate the position from the math operation 8000 + (index × length), or 8000 + (3 × 8), yielding 8024 in our example in [figure 12](#ch03.xhtml_fig12) . JavaScript looks at this location in memory and retrieves the item we wanted.

If we had asked for index position 0 ( groceryList[0] ), then the math would be 8000 + (0 × 8), which means the first item would be returned as Eggs. If we started counting from 1, we’d waste the first 8 characters in the array. In the early days of computing, that was a lot! Now, it might not sound like much, but when you consider how often arrays are used, this can quickly add up.

You might wonder why we’re talking about this in for loops rather than in arrays. Often, we’re iterating through arrays, which we’ll see in the for…in loop, explained later in this section. Of course, this is an incredibly contrived example. In reality, memory management is complicated. Nevertheless, I wanted to spend a bit of time with this detour on starting indexes with 0 instead of 1 because beginner programmers commonly make this mistake.

Most for loops will start at zero, but you can set the iterator to any number. Here we count down from 100.

```
for (let i = 100; i > 0; i--) {
   console.log(`i = ${i}`)
}
```

The notation i-- reduces i by 1, just as i++ increases it by 1\. When we run this code, we’ll see:

```
i = 100
i = 99
i = 98
…
i = 3
i = 2
i = 1
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The ellipsis ( … ) in the example above denotes numbers from 97 to 4 which have been omitted in order to save space.

Chrome doesn’t condense these messages like it did with the 10 “Hello, World!” messages, because each one is different.

Note that in this code, inside the for loop we have access to the i variable. In this case, we display it, but it can be used for any purpose we wish within the curly braces.

You can nest for loops as well:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-08.png) 

```
for (let x = 0; x < 10; x++) {
   for (let y = 0; y < 10; y++) {
      console.log(`x, y = ${x}, ${y}`)
   }
}
```

When we run this on the JavaScript console, we see:

```
x, y = 0, 0
x, y = 0, 1
x, y = 0, 2
…
x, y = 9, 7
x, y = 9, 8
x, y = 9, 9
```

#### While Loops

A while loop operates similarly to a for loop in that it enables you to run code multiple times. However, rather than iterate with a fixed counter like the for loop, while loops run the code within curly braces until the conditional statement within parentheses evaluates to false.

```
let condition = true

while (condition) {
   // Run code until condition is false
}
```

This is the basic structure of a while loop. However, if you type this code directly into your JavaScript console, not only will it do nothing, it will also create an ***infinite loop*** . An infinite loop is a loop of code that runs indefinitely.

Some loops are meant to run practically indefinitely. For example, there are multiple loops executing on your computer right now that are performing a variety of background tasks. However, when you shut down your computer, these loops eventually terminate. There’s no specific operation in JavaScript to stop an infinite loop, except closing the browser—which is not something we want visitors to our websites to do.

Despite this infinite loop pitfall, while loops are very useful. Let’s look at an example:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-09.png) 

```
let v = ""

while (!v) {
   v = prompt("Please enter a value: ")
}

console.log(`You entered: ${v}`)
```

In this code, we define a variable named v and set it to an empty string. Then we start a while loop with the condition being NOT v , meaning to run the code in the curly braces until v evaluates to true. The string v won’t evaluate to true until it has a value, because if it’s empty, it evaluates as false.

We use prompt to ask for a value, and if you simply hit ENTER here, it will set v to an empty string, which will repeat the loop until there’s a non-empty string in v .

There’s a variant of the while loop that uses the verb do .

```
let v = ""

do {
   v = prompt("Please enter a value: ")
}
while (!v)

console.log(`You entered: ${v}`)
```

This operates in a similar fashion but runs the code within the curly braces after do at least once. Then, the statement inside the parentheses is evaluated, and if it’s true (in our case, this means if v is NOT true, because of the ! in front of v ), the code is looped until it evaluates to false.

#### for…in

We can iterate through arrays and retrieve the value at each position within the array. To see this in action, we’ll first need to define an array, then use for…in to display each item:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-03-10.png) 

```
let groceryList = ["Milk", "Eggs", "Cheese", "Bread"]
for (let i in groceryList) {
   console.log(groceryList[i])
}
```

When we run this on the console, we get:

```
Milk
Eggs
Cheese
Bread
```

The magic here happens within the parentheses. We declare a variable i and then specify groceryList as the array we want to iterate through. Then, inside the curly braces—the part of the code that is run for each item in the groceryList — i becomes the numerical index (e.g., 0, 1, 2, 3) of that item, starting from zero.

When the console.log(groceryList[i]) code runs each time through the loop, groceryList[i] is evaluated to groceryList[0] , groceryList[1] , etc., which accesses those items in the array.

We can also use forEach and map to iterate through arrays, but those involve some more advanced concepts, which we’ll cover in [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) .

Iterating through data is something we do a lot in JavaScript—and programming in general. As such, there are more ways to use for and while loops. Rather than cover a lot of information here that isn’t quite applicable yet, we’ll cover additional details about loops as we progress through our journey.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop Website – Dynamic Inventory

Using an array and a for loop, we can dynamically display the inventory in our coffee shop. Rather than have it in HTML, JavaScript can store this in the shop.js file.

You might wonder why you’d want to do such a thing. The main advantage in this approach is that you have a lot more options in the future when data like inventory is inside your program rather than in an HTML file. HTML is a markup language and not really a programming language. Its function is to display and format data, not apply logic and functionality to it.

First, we need to populate shop.js with our inventory.

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// Display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")

// Our inventory
let inventory = ["Regular Coffee", "Espresso", "Cappuccino", "Latte"]
let inventoryPrices = [3.00, 3.50, 4.00, 4.25]

// Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu")

// Loop through the inventory and display each item in the menu list
for (let i = 0; i < inventory.length; i++) {
   menuList.innerHTML += "<li>" + inventory[i] + " - $" +
      inventoryPrices[i].toFixed(2) + "</li>"
}
```

The HTML only needs to be modified a bit to accommodate this. The only change I made was to remove the list items ( li elements) from the coffee menu and add an id attribute to the ul element that contained them.

index.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-index_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
      <title>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <header>
         <div class="logo-container">
            <img src="coffee-cup.svg" alt="Coffee Cup" class="logo">
         </div>
         <h1>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</h1>
      </header>
      <section class="welcome">
         <h2>Welcome</h2>
         <p>Welcome to ClydeBank Coffee Shop! We serve the best coffee in town.</p>
      </section>
      <section class="shop">
         <h2>Shop</h2>
         <ul class="coffee-list" id="coffee-menu">
         </ul>
      </section>
      <section class="about-us">
         <h2>About Us</h2>
         <p>Our mission is to provide the highest quality coffee to our community.</p>
      </section>
      <section class="contact-us">
         <h2>Contact Us</h2>
         <p>Email us at <a href="mailto:support@clydebankmedia.com">support@clydebankmedia.com</a> or call us at (800) 340-3069.</p>
      </section>
      <footer>
         <p>Copyright &copy; 2024 ClydeBank Coffee Shop</p>
      </footer>
      <script src="shop.js"></script>
   </body>
</html>
```

When you save these files and view them in your browser, you should see the same menu with prices.

We’ve covered how arrays and loops work, but there is a bit of code here we haven’t used before. I’m only going to briefly cover it here because we’ll address it in greater depth in later chapters. For now, we’re using the for loop to loop through the inventory array and add HTML (specifically li elements) to the ul of our menu.

The magic happens here:

```
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu")

for (let i = 0; i < inventory.length; i++) {
   menuList.innerHTML += "<li>" + inventory[i] + " - $" +
      inventoryPrices[i].toFixed(2) + "</li>"
}
```

In this code, the menuList variable is now a reference to the coffee-menu unordered list. We’ll cover getElementById in [chapter 8](#ch08.xhtml) . Using this reference, we can add HTML to the ul element by using menuList.innerHTML and then looping through the element. The index variable i lets us correlate the inventory and inventoryPrices so that the proper price is displayed for each offering.

One last note—to ensure the proper display of the prices with two decimal places, we use the code…

```
inventoryPrices[i].toFixed(2)
```

…to ensure that the value put inside the list item element is displayed properly.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try removing .toFixed(2) and note the effect. Prices like $3.50 should display as $3.5\. You can also try changing 2 to another number, like 4\. If you do, the item priced $3.00 will be displayed as $3.0000 because you’re increasing the number of decimal places displayed inside the list item element. We’ll cover toFixed in more detail in [chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml) .

[![img](Images/f0073-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-3) 

Chapter Recap

» Comparisons let us check the value of a variable against another value to decide how the program should run. The if statement is the most common type of comparison.

» You can assess the outcomes of several comparisons by using logical operators.

» Loops allow us to run code multiple times based on comparisons and evaluations. Each time the code passes through the loop is called an iteration.

## | 4 |

## Functions

Chapter Overview

» Functions let you reuse code

» Values passed to functions are called arguments

» A function can return a value to the code that called it

A ***function*** is a named set of commands that let us bundle lines of code together to perform work and often return values. Functions allow you to reuse code and encourage the best practice of Don’t Repeat Yourself (DRY). There are numerous reasons to adopt the DRY principle. The biggest benefit is that DRY reduces code complexity, making problems easier to spot and fix. Additionally, future improvements to the code are easier if functionality is well-structured.

When we use functions, we think about our code in a more organized way. Our programs become more like a set of architecture blueprints rather than a long recitation of commands. And since functions allow us to group lines of code together, we can easily use this functionality in other files—or even other websites.

### The Hello, JavaScript Function

Our favorite greeting is back, and this time in the form of a function. Let’s look at the greeting function both on the JavaScript console and on a web page.

#### Greetings From the JavaScript Console

In the JavaScript console, enter the following code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-01.png) 

```
function greeting() {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

When you enter the last line, you’ll see:

```
undefined
```

That’s a bit underwhelming, isn’t it? The reason you see this is due to the way the console works. Any time you enter a line of JavaScript code in the console, you’ll see the result of that activity. A function—or any line of code, for that matter—evaluates to a value. In this case, when we define the function greeting , it’s stored in memory, but doesn’t evaluate to anything, because it hasn’t been run yet. As such, there is no return value to show, so the console simply says undefined .

When we defined the function greeting , we told JavaScript what we want it to do when we run this function. To use it, we simply need to enter its name with an empty set of parentheses.

```
greeting()
```

When we run this, we see our greeting on the console.

```
Hello, World!
```

Let’s change our function up a bit. This time, instead of having it display a message in the console, let’s use the alert function (a function built in to JavaScript):

```
function greeting() {
   alert("Hello, World!")
}
```

When we redefine the greeting function, we’re telling JavaScript that we want a different set of commands to be executed when we run the function. Now that we’ve changed it, let’s run it again:

```
greeting()
```

And now we see a pop-up with the greeting.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try changing the text of the greeting, or using both alert and console.log in the function.

#### Greetings From the Web

Let’s fire up Visual Studio Code and edit the scratch.html and scratch.js files.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

Typing the code is recommended because it helps you gain first-hand experience with the editor, language, and workflow.

Here’s the contents of our updated scratch.html file:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-02.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <div id="greeting"></div>
         <button id="sayHello">Say Hello</button>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="scratch.js"></script>
</html>
```

And here’s our updated scratch.js file:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-03.png) 

```
greetingContainer = document.getElementById("greeting")
sayHello = document.getElementById("sayHello")

function greeting() {
   greetingContainer.textContent = "Hello, World!"
}

sayHello.addEventListener("click", greeting())
```

Let’s save both files and then load scratch.html in Google Chrome. We’ll see our button, then the greeting right below it. But something’s wrong. We aren’t supposed to see the greeting yet! What happened?

The problem lies in this line:

```
sayHello.addEventListener("click", greeting())
```

Here, we’re registering the “click” event, which is fired anytime the user clicks on the button with the ID of sayHello , and pointing it to the greeting function.

But that’s not what’s really happening. In JavaScript, when you type a function name that you’ve defined and put parentheses after it, like we did on the console, it executes immediately. So this line…

```
sayHello.addEventListener("click", greeting())
```

…registers the event listener, and immediately runs the greeting function. Since we don’t want the greeting to show until the user clicks the button, let’s fix this. Instead, it should read:

```
sayHello.addEventListener("click", greeting)
```

If we save scratch.js and refresh scratch.html in our browser, we’ll see it works correctly, displaying the greeting when clicked.

When we register the click event for the sayHello button and use parentheses after the function, we’re telling JavaScript we want to run the function right away. When we register the click event for the sayHello button and specify the function without parentheses, we’re saying “run this function when this event happens and not before.”

### Passing Arguments

Until this point, our function, greeting , has only performed a pre-defined set of work, and then exited. It accepted no input, and other than the activity it performed (displaying a message in the console or on an HTML page), it produced no output.

Functions can do far more than just serve as a shortcut to a list of commands. We can send an ***argument*** , or multiple arguments, to them. An argument is simply a variable we provide to the function, and the act of doing this is commonly called passing. So, we say that we pass an argument to a function.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-04.png) 

```
function greeting(firstName) {
   console.log(`Hello, ${firstName}!`)
}
```

In this version of our greeting function, we accept a variable called firstName and then use that variable in our greeting. Let’s see it in action.

If we want to call it with our name, we use:

```
greeting("Robert")
```

If we run this in the console, we’ll get:

```
Hello, Robert!
```

### Providing Default Parameters for Arguments

What happens if we don’t pass a variable? If we type…

```
greeting()
```

…in the console we get:

```
Hello, undefined!
```

Probably not the best way to greet someone. In this situation, JavaScript doesn’t know quite how to handle the fact that the variable firstName has no value. In fact, it hasn’t even been defined—hence the term “undefined”.

If we pass an empty string, the variable is at least defined. If we run…

```
greeting("")
```

…we get:

```
Hello, !
```

But either case isn’t ideal. We need a way to define a default value in case one isn’t passed to the variable. Fortunately, JavaScript provides an easy way to specify a value for missing arguments. Let’s modify our function:

```
function greeting(firstName = "World") {
   console.log(`Hello, ${firstName}!`)
}
```

Now, if we call greeting without an argument, we get an acceptable value (even though “World” isn’t a name):

```
greeting()
```

…produces…

```
Hello, World!
```

Much better!

Functions can accept multiple arguments, and they can also have default parameters:

```
function greeting(firstName = "World", lastName = "") {
   console.log(`Hello, ${firstName} ${lastName}!`)
}
```

Now, if we provide both a first and last name to our function:

```
greeting("Robert", "Oliver")
```

…produces…

```
Hello, Robert Oliver!
```

And, since default parameters are provided for both arguments, we can omit both:

```
greeting()
```

However, if we do, we get an unfortunate side effect:

```
Hello, World !
```

Providing default parameters is extremely helpful, but there are times when it’s impossible to predict exactly what value will be most useful. In this case, there is an unwanted space between “World” and “!”. We can fix this with a conditional:

```
function greeting(firstName = "World", lastName = "") {
   let message = "Hello, "

   if (lastName) {
      message = message + firstName + " " + lastName + "!"
   } else {
      message = message + firstName + "!"
   }

   console.log(message)
}
```

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

An if / else conditional tells JavaScript to run code if the statement inside the parentheses evaluates to true. For more on conditionals, please refer to [chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml) .

This code is more flexible because it provides reasonable defaults, and accounts for various ways the function can be called. Rather than display the greeting directly, it first builds a string, called message , then checks lastName to see if it evaluates to true (i.e., it’s not empty). If the string has a value, the last name is used with proper spacing. If not, the last name is omitted. Let’s see the new function in action:

```
greeting()
```

…produces…

```
Hello, World!
```

Next, the code…

```
greeting("Robert")
```

…produces…

```
Hello, Robert!
```

And finally, the code…

```
greeting("Robert", "Oliver")
```

…produces…

```
Hello, Robert Oliver!
```

### Returning Values

In addition to accepting inputs, functions can also return values. We’ve already used this concept in our example code from [chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml) .

In this line of code…

```
colorSelector = document.getElementById("colorSelector")
```

…we call the method document.getElementById and pass a string “colorSelector” as its first argument. The getElementById returns a value that we place into the colorSelector variable.

I use the term “method” instead of “function” above, but we’re essentially talking about the same thing. We’ll get into methods in [chapter 6](#ch06.xhtml) , but for now, consider a method as simply another name for a function. For our purposes in this chapter, it acts just like a function does.

To return a value, we use the return statement. It wouldn’t make much sense to use our greeting function to demonstrate this because its main purpose is to display a greeting. Now that we’re learning more about JavaScript, we can use examples that pertain more to our Clydebank Coffee Shop website code. For this, let’s create a new function that calculates sales tax:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-05.png) 

```
function calculateSalesTax(subtotal, taxRate = 0.08) {
   return subtotal * taxRate
}
```

In this function, we take two arguments: the subtotal of the order, and the taxRate , which defaults to 0.08 (or 8 percent). If we don’t specify the tax rate, the default will be used. Inside the function, we use the return statement to tell JavaScript we want to send a value back to the code that called this function. The value for return is the subtotal times the tax rate. To multiply in JavaScript, we use the asterisk. We’ll discuss math functionality in greater detail in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) .

If the order comes to 42.94, we can use the function to obtain the sales tax:

```
calculateSalesTax(42.94)
```

If we type this into the console, we’ll get the result 3.4352\. If we’re using it in actual code, we’ll need to store the value in a variable to be able to do anything useful with it:

```
let salesTax = calculateSalesTax(42.94)
```

This code tells JavaScript to run the function calculateSalesTax with the argument for subtotal set to 42.94\. Then the result is returned and is stored in the salesTax variable.

To calculate the total, our code would look something like this:

```
function calculateSalesTax(subtotal, taxRate = 0.08) {
   return subtotal * taxRate
}

let subtotal = 42.94
let salesTax = calculateSalesTax(subtotal)
let total = subtotal + salesTax
```

Here, we define the calculateSalesTax function. Then, we define the variable subtotal and assign the value 42.94 for testing purposes. Then, we define the variable salesTax and use the return value from our function with the subtotal argument to obtain the amount of sales tax for the order. Finally, we define a variable called total and provide the result from adding subtotal and salesTax .

If you type this code into the JavaScript console, you won’t get any data because we aren’t logging anything to the console. However, you can type:

```
console.log(total)
```

…to see the value of total . Or you can just type:

```
total
```

…and Chrome will display the value. This is faster than using console.log if you just want to see what is contained inside a variable.

We know the total (46.3752) is correct. However, we need to make a slight change. In JavaScript, for some values, the multiplication operation does not round to two decimal places (as is the norm for displaying currency). This is a surprisingly nuanced issue in JavaScript. The most common way to do this is to use toFixed to convert the number to a string with two decimal places.

```
total.toFixed(2)
```

…displays…

```
46.38
```

But this gives us a string and not a number—which is fine if we’re only going to display it to the user, but if we need to use it in further calculations, we must convert it back to a number.

```
let realTotal = parseFloat(total.toFixed(2))
```

The parseFloat function works just like parseInt , except it converts the value to a ***float*** , which means it doesn’t have to be a whole number. The term *float* comes from the term *floating point* , which refers to a decimal in computing. This code calls parseFloat on the string created by toFixed and assigns it to a newly defined variable named realTotal .

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

This works, but it’s not ideal. This method involves unnecessary type conversion to a string and back to a number and may not be precise under certain situations. A more robust solution would be to use Math.round . We haven’t covered that, and will do so in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) , but the solution would look like this:

```
let realTotal = Math.round(total * 100) / 100
```

This prevents us from having to change the type of the variable, because it just multiplies the total by 100 and rounds the result to the nearest integer. This shifts the decimal two places to the right with the safety harness of Math.round , then divides the “safe” result by 100 for the true answer.

Let’s consider how to create a string representation of the total—suitable for display on the website—with international considerations for display automatically performed for us. This process is called ***localization*** , the act of formatting the display of currency, date, time, etc., to match a particular country or region.

JavaScript can give us a properly formatted string for our website.

```
total.toLocaleString("en-US", { maximumFractionDigits: 2 })
```

In this example, we use toLocaleString to make a string from the total, specifying two arguments. The first is the region, which in this case is “en-US”. This is the location string for the United States. In “fr-FR” (France), the decimal point would be displayed as a comma.

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

I’ve compiled a “List of Regions” tool that you can access in your free Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript.

The second argument is in a different format than we’ve seen before, utilizing curly braces, and specifying a named value with a colon. We’ll explore that when we discuss JSON in [chapter 7](#ch07.xhtml) , but for now, the important part is that we are asking for just the first two decimal places in the total .

Let’s clean this up a bit with a new function, with the default region set to the United States, and pair it with our existing code. If you live outside the US, look up your region code at the URL above and change “en-US” to match yours.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-06.png) 

```
function getFormattedCurrency(amount, region = "en-US") {
   return amount.toLocaleString(region, { maximumFractionDigits: 2 })
}

function calculateSalesTax(subtotal, taxRate = 0.08) {
   return subtotal * taxRate
}

let subtotal = 42.94
let salesTax = calculateSalesTax(subtotal)
let total = parseFloat((subtotal + salesTax).toFixed(2))
let stringTotal = getFormattedCurrency(total)
```

With this, we get the best of both worlds—the total variable has the actual numerical value in float form, suitable for use in further calculations, and stringTotal contains the total in string format, which has been formatted to suit our region.

We can see this in action by displaying the values of total and stringTotal in the console by simply typing them in to see their value. You can use console.log(total) and console.log(stringTotal) if you want.

```
> total
46.38
> stringTotal
'46.38'
```

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

If you receive an error about redeclaring any of these variables, simply refresh your browser and re-enter the code in the JavaScript console.

![img](Images/Caution_icon.png) 

There are some gotchas involved in floating point and currency calculations. There are third-party libraries that make this simpler (see [appendix III](#appendix03.xhtml) ).

### Declaring Function Expressions

A ***function expression*** is when you set a variable to a function. That sounds strange, so let’s look at an example. Consider the following function:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-04-07.png) 

```
function calculateSalesTax(subtotal, taxRate = 0.08) {
   return subtotal * taxRate
}
```

Let’s turn this into a function expression:

```
calculateSalesTax = function(subtotal, taxRate = 0.08) {
   return subtotal * taxRate
}
```

Essentially, both are the same. The calculateSalesTax function expression will produce the same result. However, there is one key difference. Declared functions, in which we use the function calculateSalesTax() syntax, are ***hoisted*** . A hoisted function is available before it is defined within the same scope.

In other words, if we use the regular function calculateSalesTax() style declaration, we can use the function above the code that defines it, as long as we’re in the same (or narrower) scope. If you use a function expression, you must first define it before you can use it.

![img](Images/f0087-01.png) 

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop Website – Menu Function

Remember our dynamic menu from [chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml) ? Let’s wrap the loop in a function so that it’s more easily reused.

Our changes will only apply to shop.js .

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// Display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")

// Our inventory
let inventory = ["Regular Coffee", "Espresso", "Cappuccino", "Latte"]
let inventoryPrices = [3.00, 3.50, 4.00, 4.25]

// Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu")

// Loop through the inventory and display each item in the menu list
function populateMenu(container) {
   for (let i = 0; i < inventory.length; i++) {
      container.innerHTML += "<li>" + inventory[i] + " - $" + inventoryPrices[i].toFixed(2) + "</li>"
   }
}

populateMenu(menuList)
```

Now the function is defined as populateMenu and accepts an argument called container , which is a reference to the element to be modified. Calling populateMenu at the end runs the function and draws our menu for our customers.

Chapter Recap

» Functions allow you to reuse code and prevent repeating yourself, thereby encouraging the Don’t Repeat Yourself (DRY) practice.

» Values can be passed to functions as arguments, and those arguments can have one or more default values, if not provided when calling the function.

» Functions can optionally pass a value back to the code that called them. This process is called returning a value.

## Part II

## Advanced JavaScript

## | 5 |

## Understanding Closure

Chapter Overview

» Closure encompasses lexical scope and nested functions

» Lexical scoping defines the scope of variables within JavaScript

» JavaScript allows for nested functions

Most other programming languages don’t let you access variables within a function that are declared outside a function without passing them as arguments. This works in JavaScript, however, and it’s part of a larger concept in JavaScript called ***closure*** . Essentially, closure describes a technique of scoping variables and keeping those scopes active, even after the containing scope has finished.

There’s a lot to unpack in that statement, so let’s break it down into two important concepts: ***lexical scope*** and ***nested functions*** . Lexical scope describes how functions in JavaScript have access to variables based on where those variables are defined within the code. Additionally, JavaScript allows for nested functions; that is, functions that contain other functions.

### Lexical Scope

Now that we’ve taken a tour of how functions work in JavaScript, let’s talk about how scope is affected by functions.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-05-01.png) 

```
let productPrice = 8.95
let productQuantity = 3

function calculateLineCost() {
   return productPrice * productQuantity
}
```

If we enter this code into the console and run the function, we get an answer.

```
calculateLineCost()
```

…produces…

```
26.849999999999998
```

We need to fix the decimal issue, but for now let’s focus on scope. The function can access the variables productPrice and productQuantity because these variables were defined outside the function, in the global scope.

Let’s change the code a bit.

```
let productPrice = 8.95
let productQuantity = 3

function calculateLineCost() {
   let lineCost = productPrice * productQuantity
   return lineCost
}
```

If we call the function now…

```
calculateLineCost()
```

…we’ll get the same value:

```
26.849999999999998
```

But we can’t access the lineCost variable from outside the function. If we try to do so, we’ll get an error.

```
console.log(lineCost)

Uncaught ReferenceError: lineCost is not defined
```

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

Using the let declaration means variables are only accessible in the same scope as the code that declares the variable.

![img](Images/fig13.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_13.png) 

A detailed look at the scope of the lineCost variable.

The variable lineCost is not in scope globally ( [figure 13](#ch05.xhtml_fig13) ). It’s only in scope within the function, so the console can’t find it. The return statement is how we get data “out” of the function by returning the value of lineCost to the code that called it.

To put it another way, variables defined *outside* the function, like these…

```
let productPrice = 8.95
let productQuantity = 3
```

…can be accessed from within the function. But variables defined within the function, like lineCost …

```
function calculateLineCost() {
   let lineCost = productPrice * productQuantity
   return lineCost
}
```

…cannot be accessed outside the function.

This scoping works one way—from larger scopes to narrower scopes.

### Nested Functions

Up until this point, we’ve only worked with two scopes: the entire file (the scope of the entire JavaScript code file, such as scratch.js ), and the scope within a function (in our previous example, the lineCost variable). But in JavaScript, functions can be defined within other functions. That might seem like a strange concept if you’ve programmed in other languages, but this can be immensely powerful.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-05-02.png) 

```
function calculateLineCost(productPrice, productQuantity = 1) {
   let lineCost = productPrice * productQuantity
   function getFormattedCurrency(region = "en-US") {
      return lineCost.toLocaleString(region, { maximumFractionDigits: 2 })
   }
   return getFormattedCurrency()
}
```

In this example ( [figure 14](#ch05.xhtml_fig14) ), the parent function is calculateLineCost , and the one and only child function is getFormattedCurrency . The lineCost is defined and set to the productPrice times the productQuantity . The productQuantity defaults to 1 from the default value specified in the first line.

After we define the lineCost , we then define the getFormattedCurrency function. There’s no need to pass the amount to it because it has access to the lineCost variable, thanks to lexical scoping. The last line of the function obtains the locale-formatted, string version of the lineCost from getFormattedCurrency .

![img](Images/fig14.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_14.png) 

The lineCost variable is accessible inside getFormattedCurrency because the getFormattedCurrency function is defined within the calculateLineCost function, which is where the lineCost variable is defined.

We can test this on the console quite easily:

```
calculateLineCost(4.95, 5)
```

…produces…

```
'24.75'
```

By the way, a function can have as many child functions as needed, and the nesting doesn’t have to go just one layer deep. You can nest functions as many layers deep as you like, though I would advise keeping your code as simple as possible to avoid needless complexity.

### Putting It All Together

Now let’s put the concepts of closures together into a meaningful and useable example. Here, we construct a function called cartItem that contains several child functions to do various tasks within it:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-05-03.png) 

```
function cartItem(price, quantity) {
   let lineCost = 0.00
   let formattedLineCost = ""

   function calculateLineCost() {
      lineCost = price * quantity
   }

   function applyQuantityDiscount() {
      if (quantity > 1) {
         lineCost -= quantity * (price * 0.01)
      }
   }

   function formatCurrency(region = "en-US") {
      formattedLineCost =
         lineCost.toLocaleString(region, { maximumFractionDigits: 2 })
   }

   calculateLineCost()
   applyQuantityDiscount()
   formatCurrency()
   return formattedLineCost
}
```

Before we do anything, we define the lineCost variable and set it to 0.00 and define formattedLineCost and set it to an empty string, to give initial values. Then, we define two child functions. The first is calculateLineCost , which simply takes the price and quantity and multiplies them together, then sets lineCost to that value. Remember that lineCost is accessible to calculateLineCost (and any other child function) within the cartItem function.

The second child function is applyQuantityDiscount , which checks to see if the quantity is greater than 1\. If it is, it uses -= to subtract the value on the right side of the statement from the variable lineCost . This is a shortcut to avoid having to use lineCost = lineCost – (quantity * (price * 0.01)) , which also works, but requires parentheses to group the math operations together and is more complex than our solution above.

The function formatCurrency is much like our previous versions of this, but provides the result to the formattedLineCost variable defined above, and thus doesn’t return a value.

Finally, we call all three functions, then return the formattedLineCost . Let’s test this before we proceed any further.

```
item = cartItem(4.95, 10)
```

When we run this, we’ll see the properly-formatted string price, which is 49.01\. So far, so good. But this code just uses lexical scoping and nested functions. Let’s add a bit of closure magic and see what happens. To make things simpler and focus on the closure, I have removed the quantity discount. We won’t need it anyway, because people rarely buy cups of coffee in bulk!

```
const cartItem = (function(price, quantity = 1) {
   let totalQuantity = 0
   let lineCost = 0.00

   function calculateLineCost() {
      lineCost = price * totalQuantity
   }

   function getFormattedCurrency(region = "en-US") {
      return lineCost.toLocaleString(region, { maximumFractionDigits: 2 })
   }

   return function () {
      totalQuantity += quantity
      calculateLineCost()
      return getFormattedCurrency()
   }
})
```

In this code, we’re doing things a bit differently. First, rather than simply define a function, we’re setting the const variable cartItem to *equal* a function. That sounds a bit strange, I know, but this means cartItem will now work more like a function than a variable. That’s all right, though, as we’ll see in a bit.

We define two variables up front: totalQuantity and lineCost . We set them to initial values, as before. The totalQuantity variable is new in this version, and I’ll explain that shortly.

Then, we define two functions. They both operate much like their previous counterparts, but the calculateLineCost function uses totalQuantity instead of quantity .

Finally, when it comes time to return something from our cartItem function, things get interesting. Rather than return a value, cartItem returns a function, so we create an ***anonymous function*** . An anonymous function is simply a function that has no name. Inside that function, we add quantity to the totalQuantity , calculate the line cost with calculateLineCost , then return the contents from getFormattedCurrency , which utilizes lineCost from the parent function.

Now let’s put it to the test in the console.

```
let item = cartItem(4.95)
```

Instead of getting a return value, we see undefined. To see the value, we’ll need to run the new item function:

```
item()
```

Now we get the line-item cost of 4.95\. But what if the customer wants another? To do this, we simply run the function again:

```
item()
```

You can press the UP arrow on your keyboard to select previously entered values in the console. Then hit ENTER when you find the one you want. This can be a huge timesaver!

And we see 9.9, which is the correct price for two items. Run this as many times as you like, and you’ll see the price increment each time!

### Why This Works

This code works due to closures by utilizing lexical scope and nested functions. The two features work hand in hand to provide a type of structure to your functions that lets you organize functionality in a logical way.

Each time the newly minted item function runs, the totalQuantity is updated, so it’s able to keep track of and return the formatted lineCost . Even though the function has finished, these variables remain in memory so that subsequent calls will increment their values.

Regular functions, like our greeting function, last only while they’re running. Once the greeting is displayed, that’s it—you won’t hear from it again, unless you call it again. Any variables it had within it are gone. However, a function with closure, like our cartItem function, spawns a container of sorts (e.g., item ), that persists as long as we need it.

We aren’t limited to one function, of course—we can create different variables:

```
let item1 = cartItem(4.95)
let item2 = cartItem(16.24)
let item3 = cartItem(8.92)
```

This creates three functions. Each of these are independent from one another but can be called again to increment their values.

Closure in JavaScript is not a simple topic to discuss, and to be honest, there was some debate about including it this early in the book. But I chose to do so because understanding these concepts now will let us unlock a lot of cool programming paradigms later in the book. This is not easy material, so don’t feel bad if it doesn’t fully make sense right away. You’ll gain more insight into closures as we work through [chapter 6](#ch06.xhtml) on objects.

Regardless, give yourself a pat on the back for working through an advanced topic this early in your JavaScript career!

Chapter Recap

» Closure is a concept that uses lexical scoping and nested functions to maintain values inside a function, even after it has finished running.

» Lexical scope describes the scope of variables based on their position in the source code.

» Functions in JavaScript can be nested within one another and can access variables in their enclosing scope.

## | 6 |

## Organizing Data and Logic with Objects

Chapter Overview

» Classes are templates for combining data and logic

» Objects can be instances of classes

» Methods are functions that are part of an object

In [chapter 4](#ch04.xhtml) , we learned about functions, which enable us to reuse code by placing it in reusable containers. We can even add some structure to this using nested functions and closure. While these are powerful techniques for organizing our data and logic, ES6 introduced ***classes*** to JavaScript.

A class serves as a template for organizing data and logic. By data, I mean variables, and by logic, I mean code—specifically, functions. When I say template, I mean that classes can act as a kind of cookie cutter to form packages of data and logic, called ***objects*** .

But wait—why do I say classes *can* act as a kind of cookie cutter? Because objects don’t necessarily have to be derived from classes. Objects can be created without a class as standalone structures. We’ll cover both cases in this chapter ( [figure 15](#ch06.xhtml_fig15) ).

![img](Images/fig15.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_15.png) 

Classes are like cookie cutters, and the cookies are like objects. But cookies don’t necessarily have to be made by cookie cutters (classes).

Before we begin, though, it’s worth mentioning that classes and objects are components of a larger type of design called ***object-oriented programming*** . Object-oriented programming is a paradigm in programming that uses objects and classes to organize and bundle data and logic.

### The Hello JavaScript Class

Remember our friend Hello, World? Well, this now infamous greeting is about to get an upgrade. Let’s turn it into a class. First, let’s set up the code, then we’ll walk through it step by step.

Type the following in the JavaScript console:

```
class Greeting {
   constructor(firstName) {
      console.log(`Hello, ${firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

Once you’re finished, all you see is the typical “undefined” message stating that the last command entered in the console evaluated to no value. We have defined a class (in other words, a template) named Greeting .

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

As a general rule, class names in JavaScript begin with a capital letter.

To do something useful with this class, we need to create an object from it. The act of creating an object (from a class, or otherwise) is called ***instantiation*** .

Now, let’s enter the following in the console. As before, feel free to use your name instead of mine:

```
let a = new Greeting("Robert")
```

When we do, we’ll see our greeting:

```
Hello, Robert!
```

Another way to say this is that the variable named a is instantiated from the class named Greeting .

There are several things going on here, so let’s examine them in detail.

First, in the class definition…

```
class Greeting {
   constructor(firstName) {
      console.log(`Hello, ${firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

…the class is opened and closed with an opening and closing bracket, just like a function. Then, we define a function inside the class named constructor , and in this case, this function takes an argument called firstName . Inside the function, we run the console.log function, which works as we expect—using firstName to construct a greeting and displaying it to the console.

But how did JavaScript know to run the console? The constructor function is a special method that runs as soon as the class is instantiated. I used the word *method* instead of *function* because functions that are inside objects are called methods.

You might ask why the constructor didn’t run as soon as we defined the class. Remember that classes are just templates—they don’t do anything useful until they are instantiated as objects. When the object named a was created, the constructor ran, displaying the message.

### Methods

***Methods*** are functions that exist inside of an object. However, unlike functions, they aren’t defined by using the function keyword. Instead, they are defined within a class by specifying only the name of the method, followed by the method’s code inside curly braces. Let’s look at an example:

```
class Greeting {
   constructor(firstName) {
      this.firstName = firstName
   }
   sayHello() {
      console.log(`Hello, ${this.firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

To use the newly-minted method, we would use…

```
let a = new Greeting("Robert")
a.sayHello()
```

…and we’ll see:

```
Hello, Robert!
```

Here, we’ve reworked the Greeting class a bit to include a new method called sayHello . Now, the method sayHello performs the greeting, and the constructor merely sets a variable named firstName to the value passed to it by the firstName argument. This variable is accessible within the class we create, but to reference it from within the class, we use the this keyword.

This will undoubtedly bring up several questions, so let’s address them. You may wonder why we don’t just use the argument passed to the constructor (in this case, it is named firstName ). Recall that arguments are accessible *within* the function (or, in this case, method), and not anywhere else. So, we must use the this keyword to create a variable that can be accessed by methods other than the constructor within the class.

The variable named firstName that belongs to the class Greeting is then accessible via this.firstName , because when we use this we are telling JavaScript that we want to reference the object.

But wait—we just said this.firstName is accessible within the Greeting class. That’s true. So why did I say that this refers to the object? When we define the class Greeting , we don’t even know what the object or objects that are created from it will be called. That is precisely the point. When we use this , we’re referencing a value in real time within the code, not within the class. The class is simply the template that creates the object, and since we don’t know what that will be called, this stands in as our pointer.

When we say that this.firstName is accessible within the class, it is. But the class doesn’t come alive until it is instantiated into an object. In some ways, these terms are used interchangeably, but class and object don’t mean the same thing.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

An object is an instance of a class. A class serves as a template for objects.

This might seem like a borderline pedantic distinction, but there is reason I bring this up—especially when discussing methods in JavaScript. Methods perform actions within a class. They are the verbs of an object. And to do work, they need data. While you can pass arguments to methods, you’ll also need to access other variables defined within the class with the this keyword.

There’s one other reason I mention the distinction between classes and objects. When I was first learning about object-oriented programming, I frequently conflated classes and objects. When I started thinking about classes as templates and objects as the results of using those templates, it made a lot more sense.

One final detail about methods: as I said previously, methods can take arguments just like functions:

```
class Greeting {
   constructor(firstName) {
      this.firstName = firstName
   }
   sayHello(greeting = "Hello, ") {
      console.log('${greeting}${this.firstName}!')
   }
}
```

Now, we can specify the specific greeting we want to use. If we accept the default value for the greeting argument…

```
let a = new Greeting("Robert")
a.sayHello()
```

…we’ll see…

```
Hello, Robert!
```

Or we can change things up a bit…

```
let a = new Greeting("Robert")
a.sayHello("Greetings, ")
```

…which shows…

```
Greetings, Robert!
```

Gives it a nice renaissance flair, doesn’t it?

### Using Properties

A ***property*** is a variable that is attached to an object. When we discussed methods, we already made use of a property. In the example, we called it a variable, and that’s correct, but in the context of an object, it’s called a property.

Properties can either be a variable (data) or a function (logic). In this example, firstName and sayHello are both properties. But for now, let’s focus on the data portion of properties.

```
class Greeting {
   constructor(firstName) {
      this.firstName = firstName
   }
   sayHello(greeting = "Hello, ") {
      console.log(`${greeting}${this.firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

In this example, firstName is a property that belongs to the object that this class will create. Let’s look at this in action to illustrate the point:

```
let a = new Greeting("Robert")
```

Our object is now named a and the firstName variable within our object is a property. We can access that property from outside the object, like this:

```
console.log(a.firstName)
```

…which displays:

```
"Robert"
```

JavaScript is flexible, so there are other ways to access variables within objects. We can also access the firstName variable within the object named a like this:

```
console.log(a["firstName"])
```

…which also displays:

```
"Robert"
```

We can iterate through the properties in an object with a for loop. To demonstrate this, let’s create a different class.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-06-01.png) 

```
class Address {
   constructor(firstName, lastName) {
      this.firstName = firstName
      this.lastName = lastName
   }
}

let customer = new Address("Robert", "Oliver")

for (let detail in customer) {
   console.log(detail)
}
```

When we run this code, we see:

```
firstName
lastName
```

JavaScript did exactly what we asked of it, but not what we wanted. Instead, let’s get the actual value of each property:

```
for (let detail in customer) {
   console.log(customer[detail])
}
```

When we run this, we get:

```
Robert
Oliver
```

The reason this works is simple—the variable detail , which is defined through each iteration of the for loop, is given the *name* of the property, not the value.

So, in this case…

```
for (let detail in customer) {
   console.log(detail)
}
```

…we only get the property name. But when we reference the customer object by the name of the property, like this…

```
for (let detail in customer) {
   console.log(customer[detail])
}
```

…we get the index specified by detail in the customer object. In this code, the object acts much like an array.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try modifying our Address class to add a country property, then use console.log to display it in the JavaScript console. If you get stuck, you can find the answer in [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) .

### Creating Objects Without Classes

Objects can be created without classes. While classes serve as a consistent template for objects, they aren’t the only generator of them. We can create an object on the fly.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

It is quite common in JavaScript to create objects without classes. You’ll probably see this pattern a lot in your programming career. It might also surprise you to know that JavaScript uses ***syntactic sugar*** to hide the fact that classes are actually functions that generate objects. Syntactic sugar is a programming term for a feature of a language that allows you to write code in a simpler or more readable way, without changing the functionality.

Classes are powerful, but JavaScript makes it easy to create objects on the fly. This is very useful for simple projects. And since you can add properties and methods after an object is created (more on this shortly), classes serve more as an organizational tool than a logical requirement.

I find it easier to use objects without classes in most cases, and I think you will, too. But it is still helpful to know about classes, and there are cases where I think using them is best—especially for large projects.

Remember our Address class?

```
class Address {
   constructor(firstName, lastName) {
      this.firstName = firstName
      this.lastName = lastName
   }
}
```

Let’s create a similar object—without the class template. This time, we’ll add some more data.

```
const robert = {
   firstName: "Robert",
   lastName: "Oliver",
   zipCode: 12345,
   zodiacSign: "Aquarius"
}
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Feel free to sub in your own data while following along.

Now the object named robert behaves in the same way it did if it had been created by a class. In fact, the equivalent class would look like this:

```
class Address {
   constructor(firstName, lastName, zipCode, zodiacSign) {
      this.firstName = firstName
      this.lastName = lastName
      this.zipCode = zipCode
      this.zodiacSign = zodiacSign
   }
}
```

However, we don’t need the class in this instance, because we created it directly.

What about methods? Can we add them in this same manner without a class? Absolutely!

```
const robert = {
   firstName: "Robert",
   lastName: "Oliver",
   zipCode: 12345,
   zodiacSign: "Aquarius",
   greeting: function() {
      console.log(`Hello, ${this.firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

To use this method, we run…

```
robert.greeting()
```

As you can see, the this keyword still works the same in an object as it did when it was defined in a class, because this operates within the object—allowing us to access properties from methods within the object.

### Adding Properties and Methods to Existing Objects

JavaScript allows you to add properties and methods to existing objects, even if they weren’t based on a class. It’s quite simple to do this. Consider our robert object:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-06-02.png) 

```
const robert = {
   firstName: "Robert",
   lastName: "Oliver",
   zipCode: 12345,
   zodiacSign: "Aquarius",
   greeting: function() {
      console.log(`Hello, ${this.firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

Let’s add phoneNumber to the object:

```
robert.phoneNumber = "555-555-1212"
```

That’s all we have to do. It’s that simple! Internally (that is, inside JavaScript’s working memory), our object will look like this:

```
const robert = {
   firstName: "Robert",
   lastName: "Oliver",
   zipCode: 12345,
   zodiacSign: "Aquarius",
   phoneNumber: "(555) 555-1212",
   greeting: function() {
      console.log(`Hello, ${this.firstName}!`)
   }
}
```

To add a method, we use the same technique:

```
robert.sayPhoneNumber = function() {
   console.log(this.phoneNumber)
}
```

Internally, our object will look like this:

```
const robert = {
   firstName: "Robert",
   lastName: "Oliver",
   zipCode: 12345,
   zodiacSign: "Aquarius",
   phoneNumber: "(555) 555-1212",
   greeting: function() {
      console.log(`Hello, ${this.firstName}!`)
   }
   sayPhoneNumber: function() {
      console.log(this.phoneNumber)
   }
}
```

If you want to change a method, just define it again. For example, if we want to change our greeting code, we can simply do this:

```
robert.greeting = function() {
   console.log(`Hello there, ${this.firstName}!`)
}
```

### Making Iterables

You can make an object so that it is iterable. In other words, you can use a loop to iterate over its contents or functionality. To do this, we simply need to give an object a next() method and specify the Symbol.iterator keyword. JavaScript will then call this method on the object with each iteration of the loop.

To demonstrate this, let’s create an object that will generate even numbers for us:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-06-03.png) 

```
evenNumbers = {}

evenNumbers[Symbol.iterator] = function() {
   let n = 0
   return {
      next() {
         n += 2
         return {value: n, done: false}
      }
   }
}

for (let n of evenNumbers) {
   console.log(n)
   if (n >= 10) break
}
```

Type this code into your console. When you finish the last bracket of the for loop, you’ll see the following:

```
2
4
6
8
10
```

It works! Well, sort of—there is a bug. And there’s another glaring issue that needs to be discussed. We didn’t create an object, or a class, did we? Is evenNumbers a function? Or an array? What is going on? We’ll get to that in a minute, as it warrants a longer discussion.

The bug is a simple problem to fix. It skips an even number. The number 0 is an even number, and it skips it and moves straight to 2 because n += 2 adds 2 to n before any value is returned. There are several ways to fix this problem.

![img](Images/Question_icon.png) 

How can you solve this issue? I’d like you to pause reading here and think about how you would approach fixing the issue. If you like, change the code, and re-run it in your console to see if that works.

Let’s examine what I consider to be the simplest way to resolve the issue:

```
evenNumbers = {}

evenNumbers[Symbol.iterator] = function() {
   let n = -2
   return {
      next() {
         n += 2
         return {value: n, done: false}
      }
   }
}

for (let n of evenNumbers) {
   console.log(n)
   if (n >= 10) break
}
```

This solution works, but admittedly, it’s not quite as elegant as I’d like. For now, though, this certainly fixes the bug.

Now that we’ve addressed the bug, let’s discuss the much larger issue about functions and objects.

### Functions Are Objects

In JavaScript, functions are objects.

I put that sentence in its own paragraph because you probably won’t believe it the first time you read it. This concept certainly confused me at first.

The goal here is not to confuse you. I realize that discussing functions then objects can muddy the waters; however, just as addition and subtraction are two different yet very related concepts, so are functions and objects.

Variables can not only store values. They can also store functions, because functions are objects, and variables can store objects. We can even pass functions as arguments to other functions!

Before we get too deep into the weeds, let’s examine the components of our previous example:

```
evenNumbers = {}

evenNumbers[Symbol.iterator] = function() {
   let n = -2
   return {
      next() {
         n += 2
         return {value: n, done: false}
      }
   }
}

for (let n of evenNumbers) {
   console.log(n)
   if (n >= 10) break
}
```

In the first line, we create an empty object named evenNumbers . If we don’t do this, we’ll get an error:

```
evenNumbers is not defined
```

Next, we assign an anonymous function to the Symbol.iterator method of the evenNumbers object. In that function, we set the variable n to −2 and then return a next method. That method adds 2 to n and then returns an object with two properties— value set to n and done set to false .

Then, the for loop iterates over the results from evenNumbers and calls the next() method for each iteration. In our for loop, we check to see if n is greater than or equal to 10, and if so, we break from the loop.

[Figure 16](#ch06.xhtml_fig16) shows a breakdown of the components of this code.

![img](Images/fig16.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_16.png) 

Each time we iterate through evenNumbers , the value of n is derived by executing the function in the iterator.

### Using Objects on a Web Page

We’ve discussed a lot of theory in this chapter about classes, objects, and iterators, but we haven’t seen how this code works on an actual web page. Let’s see a practical example of an iterator object on a web page that collects a credit card expiration date.

iterator.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-iterator_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Iterator</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <form>
            <input type="text" name="cardNumber" placeholder="Card Number">
            <select id="expMonth" name="expMonth">
            </select>
            <select id="expYear" name="expYear">
            </select>
         </form>
      </div>
   <body>
   <script src="iterator.js"></script>
</html>
```

iterator.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-iterator_js.png) 

```
// Define iterable objects
expMonths = {}
expYears = {}

// Iterator object for expiration months
expMonths[Symbol.iterator] = function() {
   let m = 0
   return {
      next() {
         m += 1
         theValue = "<option value='" + m + "'>" + m + "</option>"
         if (m > 12) {
            return {value: theValue, done: true}
         } else {
            return {value: theValue, done: false}
         }
      }
   }
}

// Iterator object for expiration years
expYears[Symbol.iterator] = function() {
   let y = new Date().getFullYear() - 1
   return {
      next() {
         y += 1
         theValue = {html: "<option value='" + y + "'>" + y + "</option>", year: y}
         return {value:theValue, done:false}
      }
   }
}

// Construct the expiration month dropdown options
for (let month of expMonths) {
   document.getElementById("expMonth").innerHTML += month
}

// Construct the expiration year dropdown options
maxYear = new Date().getFullYear() + 10
for (let year of expYears) {
   document.getElementById("expYear").innerHTML += year.html
   if (year.year >= maxYear) break
}
```

If you save these two files and open iterator.html in your browser, you should see a credit card number prompt with populated month and year fields ( [figure 17](#ch06.xhtml_fig17) ).

![img](Images/fig17.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_17.png) 

Our pre-populated credit card expiration month and year fields.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

The variable defined within a for loop (e.g., month inside of the expMonths iteration) becomes the variable with the value for that iteration through the loop. For example, when iterating through the months, the month variable contains the number of the month. This value is only accessible while inside the for loop and then is reset through each iteration.

Let’s walk through the code to see how this works. We’ll start with the HTML.

We use our standard boilerplate template and create three input fields: cardNumber , expMonth , and expYear . On the cardNumber field, we assign a placeholder so that the visitor knows they will need to enter their card number in that field. The next two fields define select inputs, but don’t provide any options—our JavaScript will do that. Finally, on the HTML page, note that the iterator.js file is referenced at the bottom with the script tag. This includes the JavaScript on the page.

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

Before we step through the JavaScript, you might wonder why we didn’t just hardcode the values in the HTML. We could have put options for each of the 12 months, and then 10 options for the years, starting with the current year. This solution certainly works, but it has two main drawbacks.

First, it wouldn’t give us an opportunity to write any JavaScript, but the second and most important reason is that the code is not evergreen. We would have to go back and update it every 10 years. I’ve had clients ask me to update credit card expiration year fields before. Rather than add years continuously, I let JavaScript do the work.

Now, on to the exciting part—the JavaScript found in iterator.js . First, we create two empty objects, expMonths and expYears . Then, we define the expMonths iterator. Since there are no more than 12 months, we’ll need to make this iterator stop at 12\. We could have the for loop that uses it do this, but there will never be more than 12 months, so it makes sense to integrate this functionality directly into the object.

To accomplish this, we first generate the HTML and store it in theValue and then check to see if m is greater than 12\. If it is, we return an object that consists of two properties— value and done . The value property contains the HTML code for the option , and the second, done , signals the iterator to stop if it is set to true .

In the second iterator, named expYears , we don’t need internal stop logic, because years can go on forever, and we don’t want to artificially limit this iterator to a fixed number of years. As such, the years iterator is a bit simpler, except that we need to start with the current year minus 1 so that the first iteration will include the current year. To get the current four-digit year, we use new Date().getFullYear() , and then we subtract 1. The rest of the iterator proceeds as usual, adding 1 to each year (tracked internally with the y variable). However, since the for loop that’s calling the iterator will need to know what year it is, we don’t just return the string of HTML in theValue ; instead, we return an object containing both the HTML string and the year. More on this in a moment.

The for loop for generating the options for the months is simple. We only add the HTML generated in each pass (internally called theValue , and returned as month in the loop). This will self-terminate when the done value is set to true (without adding the “month 13” HTML that’s returned along with the true value), and we take care of this inside the expMonths iterator.

The for loop for generating the years is a bit more complex. Since it doesn’t internally set the done property, we need to break from the loop after a set number of iterations, or else it would go on indefinitely. To do this, we first get the value for maxYear , which is simply the current year (retrieved via new Date().getFullYear() ) with 10 added to it. Then, we start the for loop and add year . html to the innerHTML property of the select input. We use year.html instead of just year because the html property of year is the HTML content that was originally theValue inside the object. Finally, we check to see if year.year (the year property of our iterator object named year ) is greater than the maxYear variable. If it is, we break out of the for loop.

There were two bugs I made when I first constructed this code for this example. I think it’s important to note this so that you see that experienced programmers make mistakes—and sometimes very simple ones at that—and to see the process we use to fix these bugs.

First, I added the line…

```
maxYear = new Date().getFullYear() + 10
```

…inside the last for loop (the loop that generates the years) instead of right before it. Technically, this worked, so it wasn’t so much a bug as a poor coding practice. We only need to calculate maxYear once, and while this worked, it was a waste of computing resources. Moving this to right before the for loop fixed the issue.

Secondly, in the year loop, I had the line…

```
document.getElementById("expYear").innerHTML += year
```

…instead of…

```
document.getElementById("expYear").innerHTML += year.html
```

Adding year to the end of the innerHTML of expYear would not have worked, because year is not a string with the year in it; rather, it is an object with two properties: html and year .

So, to reference the string of HTML, we must use year.html within the loop, and year.year contains the numerical value of the year so we can check it against maxYear .

The great part about this approach is that the objects expMonths and expYears know how to visually construct themselves, not only in a logical sense but visually, to the user, via HTML.

### Classes Versus Objects

Now that we’ve introduced classes, objects, and objects that can iterate, you may be wondering why we need classes at all. Well, we don’t. JavaScript didn’t get classes until ES6, and objects were used before then.

Developers coming from other languages like Python, Ruby, C#, and C++ naturally expect classes to be present, as those languages rely on them to construct objects.

When to use classes versus manually constructing your objects is a hotly debated topic because there are pros and cons to each approach. Personally, I prefer constructing objects from scratch rather than via classes, but I introduced them first in this chapter because you may have seen classes before with other languages. Throughout this book I focus primarily on objects, but classes have their place, too. Don’t take this focus on objects to mean that classes are not important to learn and use—they are.

You’ll develop your own styles and workflow patterns as you write your own JavaScript. It’s my job to introduce you to as many of these approaches as possible so that you can find what works best for your situation.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop Exercise: A Menu Object

Now that we’ve constructed objects, we have the knowledge we need to construct an object that combines the data of our menu inventory and the logic of displaying the menu in one place. In our updated shop.js file, the menu object now has the code necessary to display the data it contains:

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop-06_js.png) 

```
// Display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")

// The inventory object
let menu = {
   inventory: {
      "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
      "Espresso": 3.50,
      "Cappuccino": 4.00,
      "Latte": 4.25
   },
   populate: function(container) {
      for (let item in this.inventory) {
         let price = this.inventory[item]
         container.innerHTML += "<li>" + item + " - $" + price.toFixed(2) + "</li>"
      }
   }
}

// Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu")

// Populate the menu
menu.populate(menuList)
```

Editing the inventory is easier in JavaScript than in HTML because the markup is handled by the populate function. Additionally, the inventory can be supplied by an external source. In JavaScript, this is usually retrieved from a remote service. In the next chapter, we’ll show this in action.

Chapter Recap

» Classes serve as templates, much like a cookie cutter, for combining data and logic into units called objects.

» Objects can have properties (data) and methods (functions), and these can be added after an object is created.

» Objects need not necessarily be created from classes. They can be created independently.

## | 7 |

## Using JSON

Chapter Overview

» JSON encapsulates JavaScript objects

» Many programming languages use JSON

» JavaScript can parse (import) and stringify (export) JSON

***JSON*** , or JavaScript Object Notation, is a way to package a JavaScript object.

JSON has become so popular that many other programming languages have adopted it as a common exchange format—or a way to share data from one program or service to another.

The format is human-readable and the data within JSON is easily understood. Additionally, since it shares many commonalities with how objects are created in JavaScript with code, there isn’t a lot of new material to absorb when you learn JSON.

### A Simple Example

Before we dive further into JSON, let’s look at a simple example:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-07-01.png) 

```
{
   "coffeeSales": [
      {
         "blendName": "Arabica",
         "cupsSold": "120"
      },
      {
         "blendName": "Robusta",
         "cupsSold": "242"
      }
   ]
}
```

This is an array (defined by square brackets—more on syntax later), contained within an object named coffeeSales .

This is a simple sales report of different blends of coffee in JSON format. For comparison, let’s define a JavaScript object with the exact same data. The object will be called salesReport .

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-07-02.png) 

```
const salesReport = {
   coffeeSales: [
      {
         blendName: "Arabica",
         cupsSold: "120"
      },
      {
         blendName: "Robusta",
         cupsSold: "242"
      }
   ]
}
```

The two representations are very similar. And to demonstrate that they have the same data, we can use the JSON.stringify function to convert a JavaScript object to a JSON string, and JSON.parse to convert a JSON string back into a JavaScript object.
