I’ll show you an example of this, but I first want to preface this by saying that JSON is normally minified; that is, stripped of unnecessary spaces and newline characters. A string formatted with JSON takes up less space, which is ideal for saving network bandwidth, but it can be a bit difficult to type.

There is no need to type this next example out—it is merely to show the concept of JSON in a string:

```
let jsonString =
'{"coffeeSales":[{"blendName":"Arabica","cupsSold":"120"},
{"blendName":"Robusta","cupsSold":"242"}]}'
```

This is shown in the book as three lines, but in real usage it would be on one line. Also, I’ve surrounded the JSON string with a single quote mark to keep the string enclosed in a unique character—not to be confused with the double quote marks we usually use.

Once this line has run, we now have JSON in a string named jsonString . We can convert this to a JavaScript object:

```
let salesReport = JSON.parse(jsonString)
```

Now salesReport is an object. We can convert it back into JSON with JSON.stringify .

```
let newJsonString = JSON.stringify(salesReport)
```

The strings jsonString and newJsonString are identical, and we can validate this on the console:

```
console.log(jsonString == newJsonString)
```

The console will report true , showing the evaluation was successful.

Unfortunately, you can’t store functions or methods in JSON. You can only store data. That said, if two websites are exchanging data via JSON, there isn’t a need to represent logic in the JSON string, because the code on either side will know how to handle the data.

### Understanding JSON Syntax

You’ve seen an example of both beautified JSON—that is, JSON code that is formatted using tabs and newlines to display the code in a pleasing and easy-to-browse format (via the process of ***beautification*** )— and minified JSON, which favors compactness. It’s helpful to understand the ***syntax*** of JSON, or the set of rules that define its format, for situations where you are either creating JSON by hand, or validating it when there’s a problem.

There have been many times where I’ve had JSON errors in strings from other languages due to mistakes on my end or due to different parsing variations. The fact that I know the format of JSON helps me spot these issues.

The first rule is that objects are contained within curly braces (i.e., { and } ). Since JSON is the notation of objects, you’ll often see JSON enclosed in curly braces.

#### Representing Name and Value Pairs

JSON separates the name of a property (think variable) and its value with a colon, just like in JavaScript. Here’s a simple “Hello World” message in JSON:

```
{"Greeting": "Hello, World!"}
```

Beautified, this would be:

```
{
   "Greeting": "Hello, World!"
}
```

The equivalent JavaScript for this would be:

```
const hello = {
   Greeting: "Hello, World!"
}
```

And, to access the greeting, we would use:

```
console.log(hello.Greeting)
```

…which displays…

```
Hello, World!
```

The main difference between JSON and JavaScript here is that the name of the property is surrounded by double quotes.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

A property of an object in JavaScript is a piece of data (i.e., a variable) that the object possesses.

#### Representing Arrays

Arrays can be represented in JSON as well, and we’ve already used this particular construction in our sales report JSON.

Not all JSON has to start with curly braces. If you only have an array to represent, you can skip the curly braces and use square brackets. However, I like to enclose arrays in JSON in an object (e.g., coffeeSales in the example below), if only to give another developer a hint as to what the array represents if they don’t have any other documentation to reference.

```
{
   "coffeeSales": [
      {
      "blendName": "Arabica",
         "cupsSold": "120"
      },
      {
         "blendName": "Robusta",
         "cupsSold": "242"
      }
   ]
}
```

This is accessed in JavaScript like this:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-07-03.png) 

```
const salesReport = {
   coffeeSales: [
      {
         blendName: "Arabica",
         cupsSold: "120"
      },
      {
         blendName: "Robusta",
         cupsSold: "242"
      }
   ]
}
```

Now we have an object representing the sales of each type of coffee, we can iterate over it to output that information to the console.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-07-04.png) 

```
dayNumber = 1

for (let day in salesReport.coffeeSales) {
      console.log("Day " + dayNumber + ":")
      console.log("Blend: " + salesReport.coffeeSales[day].blendName +
         ", Cups Sold: " + salesReport.coffeeSales[day].cupsSold)
      dayNumber++
}
```

#### Representing Booleans

You can also store Boolean values in JSON. Recall that a Boolean value is either true or false. Let’s look at an example:

```
{
   "loggedIn": true
}
```

In JavaScript, this would be:

```
const userStatus = {
   loggedIn: true
}
```

JSON is incredibly powerful. As JavaScript programmers, we’re lucky that nearly every other programming language also speaks this format.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop: JSON

In our shop.js file, we have something like JSON in our menu object. I have bolded the data portion:

```
let menu = {
   inventory: {
      "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
      "Espresso": 3.50,
      "Cappuccino": 4.00,
      "Latte": 4.25
   },
   populate: function(container) {
      for (let item in this.inventory) {
         let price = this.inventory[item]
         container.innerHTML += "<li>" + item + " - $" + price.toFixed(2) + "</li>"
      }
   }
}
```

“Similar” is an understatement. This is regular object notation, but for it to be valid in JSON, all property names should be enclosed in quotes. Let’s convert that data to JSON:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-07-05.png) 

```
{
   "inventory": {
         "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
         "Espresso": 3.50,
         "Cappuccino": 4.00,
         "Latte": 4.25
   }
}
```

If this was provided to our menu object from an outside source in JSON format, then it could be used to power our menu. We could then update the inventory property of the menu object, even if it was already set to other values. Consider a set of new prices in JSON format:

```
let inflatedPrices = {
   "inventory": {
      "Regular Coffee": 4.00,
      "Espresso": 4.50,
      "Cappuccino": 4.75,
      "Latte": 5.00
   }
}
```

If you type this into the JavaScript console after the coffee shop web page has loaded, you can then update the inventory:

```
menu.inventory = inflatedPrices.inventory
```

Now the data has been changed. You can confirm that with:

```
console.log(menu.inventory)
```

If you want to update the web page, simply clear the innerHTML of the container and call the populate method again:

```
menuList.innerHTML = ""
menu.populate(menuList)
```

You should now see the updated prices on the web page.

Later, in [chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml) , we’ll explore how to fetch data from remote servers. Using this technique, paired with JavaScript events we’ll learn about in [chapter 9](#ch09.xhtml) , you could fetch this data from a remote server and keep the pricing data updated on the web page in real time, even if the visitor never refreshes the page!

Chapter Recap

» JavaScript uses JSON to encapsulate objects.

» Many programming languages use JSON, in large part due to its readability and simplicity.

» You can import objects with a process called parsing, and export objects with a process called stringifying. This is often done when exchanging JSON with other websites or programming languages.

## | 8 |

## Using the Document Object Model (DOM)

Chapter Overview

» Web page elements are represented in the Document Object Model (DOM)

» The browser is represented in the Browser Object Model (BOM)

» The web page can be added to or changed via the DOM

You can build an incredible website with HyperText Markup Language (HTML). And you can use Cascading Style Sheets (CSS) to style that content and even incorporate animation. But HTML and CSS alone can’t alter the page content beyond what is contained in the HTML and CSS files. While CSS can hide or show a button based on the position of your mouse, it cannot create that button—the button must already exist in the HTML.

But JavaScript can.

With JavaScript, you can change or add to any part of the web page you like. And by exchanging data using JSON, it can import completely novel data to the page from an outside source, like a web server running a program written in JavaScript, PHP, or anything else.

To make these changes, JavaScript needs to be able to access the page in a programmatic way—that is, to be able to walk through the different parts of the page, and the browser itself, using a predictable approach.

In this chapter, we’ll explore the Document Object Model (DOM) and the Browser Object Model (BOM), which are the roadmaps your JavaScript code can use to do incredible things in the browser. Since the DOM gives you near complete control over the page, adding to, changing, or removing elements is all possible directly from JavaScript.

It’s important to note that when we modify a page via the DOM, we aren’t actually changing the HTML file itself—only the contents of the page in the browser’s memory.

### The Document Object Model

The Document Object Model (or DOM) is structured much like a family tree. You can think of the document as the trunk, which contains the root element (in HTML, this is <html></html> ). The branches of the tree include the head element, ( <head></head> ), which can contain its own branches, like the title ( <title></title>) and various meta and script tags. The body branch (<body></body> ) contains the code that generates the visible portion of the web page, and thus many branches of its own.

To visualize this, consider the following code, and the visual example of the DOM with this code ( [figure 18](#ch08.xhtml_fig18) ):

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p>Hello, World!</p>
         <p>And hello again!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

![img](Images/fig18.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_18.png) 

Here is a visual representation of the HTML code.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

An object is a package of data and logic.

To use JavaScript to change an HTML element object, we need to find the object within the DOM before we can instruct JavaScript to change it. To do this, we can use several methods within the document object.

#### Finding Elements Using getElementById

If an element has an ID attribute, we can use document.getElementById to access that specific element. Let’s consider our standard HTML boilerplate code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-08-01.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p>Hello, World!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Your scratch.html may have other code within the div element named content . If this is the case, please change the code to match this listing.

If we want to access the div element with the ID of content , we would use:

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")
```

If you enter this in your JavaScript console, you’ll see undefined returned, but the variable named content will be associated with the div. We can see this by simply typing content in the console. When we do, the HTML of the element is displayed. In [figure 20](#ch08.xhtml_fig20) , I have expanded the HTML shown inside this object by clicking the triangle right before the HTML starts.

![img](Images/fig19.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_19.png) 

The content object expanded to show the HTML within the object.

![img](Images/Detour_icon.png) 

Many modern browsers automatically assign a variable for HTML elements with IDs. For example, the content variable will already contain the result of document.getElementById( " content " ) before this line is run. That said, this behavior should not be relied on for several reasons.

First, it’s not part of the ECMAScript standard. Second, defining variables for HTML elements you need to interact with produces more readable code. You won’t wonder where the content variable came from if you’re not familiar with this browser feature. Third, purposely setting the variable lets us have more precise control over the scope of the variable. And finally, this reduces the possibility of accidental conflicts and errors due to lack of support.

For these reasons, defining a variable that points to an element rather than relying on this automatic feature is a good practice. I prefer to name the variable the same name to avoid creating unnecessary variables in the browser’s memory, but the name you give any variable in your code is up to you.

While this object named content appears to only have HTML contained within, it is much more powerful than that. If you type content and then a period, you’ll see Google Chrome provide a list of all the methods and properties within this object ( [figure 20](#ch08.xhtml_fig20) ):

![img](Images/fig20.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_20.png) 

Google Chrome showing a list of methods and properties on the content object.

The list is longer than what is shown in [figure 20](#ch08.xhtml_fig20) . You can scroll it with your mouse all the way to the end. There are many methods and properties available, and while we can’t cover them all in this book, I encourage you to explore the DOM by selecting values from the list and pressing ENTER to see the value of the property or see what the method does.

You may wonder where these properties came from, since we didn’t add them to the object. The browser pre-populates DOM objects with the attributes available to each element, so they’re ready for us to use.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

If it’s a method, you must put parentheses after its name to invoke it. You’ll know if it’s a method because you’ll see something similar to f methodName() { // code } after you select an item from the list and press ENTER .

We’ll explore the most common functionality in this list in the next section, but before we do that, let’s look at three other methods to find objects in your HTML.

#### Finding Elements Using getElementsByTagName

Not all HTML elements in your code may have ID attributes, and it is sometimes useful to apply the same change to every element with a given type of tag at once. Let’s consider our boilerplate code, but in this case, we’ll have two paragraphs. If you still have the code from the previous listing in scratch.html , you can simply add the <p>And hello again!</p> code after the first paragraph.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-08-02.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p>Hello, World!</p>
         <p>And hello again!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Your scratch.html may have other code within the div element named content . If this is the case, please change the code to match this listing.

To get an array of paragraph ( <p></p> ) objects, we would run:

```
let paragraphs = document.getElementsByTagName("p")
```

Now, in paragraphs , we’ll have an array of both paragraph elements. We can access the first and second paragraphs by using paragraphs[0] and paragraphs[1] ( [figure 21](#ch08.xhtml_fig21) ).

![img](Images/fig21.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_21.png) 

Illustrating the document.getElementsByTagName method and its results.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

Indexes in arrays start at 0, not 1\.

Each of the elements in the paragraphs array is its own object. If we wanted to, we could create a variable for each:

```
let firstParagraph = paragraphs[0]
let secondParagraph = paragraphs[1]
```

Now that you know how to find all elements of a given type, you can either act on them individually (e.g., apply a CSS attribute to the third paragraph on the page), or all at once (e.g., make all paragraphs bold text).

We’ll explore the most-used functionality in the next section, but for now, let’s look at another way to obtain objects from your HTML.

#### Finding Elements Using getElementsByClassName

We can also obtain an array of objects that are HTML elements that have the same class. Let’s modify our scratch.html again to add classes to these paragraphs:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-08-03.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

In this code, each of the paragraphs has been assigned the greeting class via HTML. Just as the CSS from the greeting class is assigned to all elements with that class, the array of objects returned from document.getElementsByClassName will all be elements with that class.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

For a refresher on how CSS classes work, please see [appendix I](#appendix01.xhtml) on HTML/CSS.

As seen in [figure 22](#ch08.xhtml_fig22) , to get an array of element objects that have the greeting class, we would run:

```
let greetings = document.getElementsByClassName("greeting")
```

![img](Images/fig22.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_22.png) 

Illustrating the document.getElementsByClassName method and its results.

Armed with these three tools, document.getElementById , document.getElementsByTagName , and document.getElementsByClassName , you can now have tremendous control over HTML content in the browser—including after it has already been loaded and rendered.

Now that we have our objects representing HTML, let’s explore what we can do with them.

#### Finding Elements by Selector

You can use the document.querySelector to find elements in much the same manner you would refer to them in CSS.

Consider our HTML code:

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

To find the content div, you would use:

```
let content = document.querySelector("#content")
```

To find the *first* element that has a matching class of the name you specify, use:

```
let firstGreeting = document.querySelector(".greeting")
```

I say first, because querySelector only matches one element. If you want *all* of the elements that have a certain class, you can use querySelectorAll :

```
let allGreetings = document.querySelectorAll(".greeting")
```

Now, the allGreetings variable is a node list, which is simply a collection of nodes in an HTML document. This node list is much like an array and, most importantly, is iterable.

```
for (let greeting of allGreetings) {
   console.log(greeting)
}
```

Using a for loop, we can perform an action on each element that has the greeting class.

![img](Images/Important_icon.png) 

The node list returned by querySelectorAll is not updated if the DOM is updated after you retrieve the list, so if you have code or an event handler that could update elements in the DOM that might be in the results, it’s best to obtain the result again to ensure it is completely up to date. Alternatively, you could use getElementsByClassName , the results of which are always up to date.

You can also select elements as well, just as you can with CSS. For example, if we wanted all paragraphs, we could use:

```
let allParagraphs = document.querySelectorAll("p")
```

The querySelector and querySelectorAll methods can be a bit slower than getElementById , getElementsByTagName , and getElementsByClassName . The difference in simple cases is insignificant, so don’t let that scare you from using it. That said, if performance is critical—especially if you plan to use this in a loop—you may wish to use another selector.

[![img](Images/f0141-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-5) 

### Changing an Element’s CSS Attributes

Using the objects obtained from the three DOM-related methods we’ve learned, we can change the CSS attributes or the CSS class on one or many elements on the page.

Let’s consider our example with two greetings:

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

We have two options here. First, we can set the font-weight attribute of the div with the ID of content to bold. Second, we can iterate through all paragraph tags on the page and set them to bold.

To set a specific element’s attribute, first assign a variable to the element with document.getElementById .

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")
content.style.fontWeight = "bold"
```

You can put those two lines in your scratch.js file and load your scratch.html file with the HTML content above to see the effect. All text should be bolded. If not, doublecheck that your scratch.html file contains the HTML in this example with two greetings.

There are some things to note here. The content object’s style property contains all the CSS styles you can assign to the element. But unlike CSS attribute names that have dashes, like font-weight , you access these values in JavaScript using the standard camel case capitalization technique in JavaScript (i.e., fontWeight ). Also, the formatting change only persists until the page is refreshed.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

The HTML file isn’t actually modified. The only content that is modified is within the DOM, which is located in the browser’s memory.

Now let’s modify all the paragraph elements. But before we do that, let’s refresh the page in our browser so that we start from a blank slate. Now, let’s obtain an array of all paragraph objects.

```
let paragraphs = document.getElementsByTagName("p")
```

Now that we have this, we can loop through the array and set the style for each:

```
for (let p of paragraphs) {
   p.style.fontWeight = "bold"
}
```

If we run this code, we’ll see all the text turn to bold.

What if we want to modify only the paragraphs within the content div? In our example so far, this is a trivial request because our two paragraphs are only inside the content div. So that we can verify our results, let’s change up our scratch.html file a bit:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-08-04.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
      <div id="moreContent">
         <p class="greeting">This should not become bold.</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

To obtain the paragraphs within the content div, we need to first grab the content div itself:

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")
```

Then, we can use the tree-like nature of the DOM to get only the paragraphs within this part of the page:

```
let paragraphs = content.getElementsByTagName("p")
```

Rather than use getElementsByTagName on the document object, we instead used this on the content object, giving us just the paragraphs from the content div and not the entire HTML document ( [figure 23](#ch08.xhtml_fig23) ).

![img](Images/fig23.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_23.png) 

To make those paragraphs bold, the code is identical, because paragraphs is still an array of objects:

```
for (let p of paragraphs) {
   p.style.fontWeight = "bold"   
}
```

If you want to see a full list of CSS attributes, you can type the following in the JavaScript console:

```
content.style.
```

When you type the final dot, a dropdown menu containing all the CSS attributes will appear. Feel free to experiment with these as you scroll through them, and set them to different values.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

How would you set the paragraphs on this page to red? Try to do this in the JavaScript console (or your scratch.js file) on your own. If you get stuck, please consult [appendix IV](#appendix04.xhtml) for the answer.

### Changing an Element’s Class

Changing CSS attributes is simple enough, but in complex documents, you’ll probably want to set up a new CSS class for a desired visual outcome (e.g., bolded text, a different color, moving an element, etc.). Using CSS classes instead of modifying individual CSS attributes is generally more desirable because it allows you to reuse code more easily. Additionally, a web designer with no knowledge of JavaScript can apply a class you create to another element to expand the effect of your code.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

When we speak of class, we’re not referring to JavaScript classes, but rather to CSS classes. For an overview of CSS, please see [appendix I](#appendix01.xhtml) .

Let’s use our greeting code again in scratch.html , but this time, we’ll change one of the CSS classes so we can tell the difference:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-08-05.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
      <div id="moreContent">
         <p class="differentText">This should not become bold.</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

To find the elements with the differentText class, we use…

```
let differentTexts = document.getElementsByClassName("differentText")
```

…in our scratch.js file. Or we can use this on the JavaScript console with this HTML loaded in our browser. After running this JavaScript, differentTexts will be an array of just one object, differentTexts[0] . We can then change the class with the following JavaScript code:

```
differentTexts[0].classList.add("greeting")
differentTexts[0].classList.remove("differentText")
```

After running this code, the first (and in this case, the only) element in the differentTexts array, has the new class ( greeting ) added and the old class ( differentText ) removed.

If there is going to be more than one element that has the differentText class, we can iterate through all of them and make this change. This is generally good practice because you can always be sure all elements are changed (if this is what you want), so you can add extra elements with this class in the HTML without having to change the JavaScript code.

```
for (let d of differentTexts) {
   d.classList.add("greeting")
   d.classList.remove("differentText")
}
```

If you’d like to add a CSS class to an element and not remove or change another class, simply use the classList.add method. Subsequently, you can use the same technique to remove a class.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

An element can have more than one CSS class. This can be very useful in web design.

### Editing HTML with the DOM

We can add, edit, and delete HTML on a page with JavaScript using the DOM. We’ve already done this before in limited ways, but now we’ll explore the various functionalities we can use to dynamically alter the rendered website.

To demonstrate this ability, let’s look at our scratch.html file, which contains this HTML from the last time we edited it:

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Scratch Pad</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>
         <p class="greeting">And hello again!</p>
      </div>
      <div id="moreContent">
         <p class="differentText">This should not become bold.</p>
      </div>
   </body>
</html>
```

To edit this HTML, we first need to grab a reference to the element we want to edit. First, let’s remove the moreContent div. To do that, we use getElementById to grab a reference to it.

```
let moreContent = document.getElementById("moreContent")
```

We can then use the remove method to remove the div from the page:

```
moreContent.remove()
```

When we run this via the JavaScript console, the moreContent div is removed from the page. Again, the HTML file isn’t edited, it is only removed in the browser’s rendered version of the page. If you think of the moreContent div as a branch on a tree, it’s as though rather than trimming that branch from the tree, we took a picture of the tree and removed the branch from the picture. The original branch is still there.

Now, let’s edit the HTML. Again, we grab a reference to the element we want to edit, then use the innerHTML method to edit its contents:

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")
content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>'
```

I used single quotes to enclose the value of innerHTML , as I embedded double quotes inside the HTML surrounding the word greeting . I also could have used double quotes on the outside and single quotes for the class definition. The important thing is to not use double quotes or single quotes for both, because the quotes around greeting would break JavaScript’s understanding of the string being provided to innerHTML .

When you enter lines in the JavaScript console, the text on the page will instantly change.

By the way, you don’t have to use two lines of JavaScript code to get this done. Instead, we can use:

```
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, World!</p>'
```

While this approach works well for a single change, it becomes less efficient if you need to reference it later in your code.

Let’s say you need to edit the HTML multiple times. For the sake of example, we’ll demonstrate this five times in a row on the same div, but in a real situation you would probably edit the div at different points in the code.

```
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">
Hello, p.1!</p>'
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">
Hello, 2!</p>'
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">
Hello, 3!</p>'
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">
Hello, 4!</p>'
document.getElementById("content").innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">
Hello, 5!</p>'
```

Rather than reference it like that each time, it’s a lot simpler and easier to maintain to use this approach:

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")

content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, 1!</p>'
content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, 2!</p>'
content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, 3!</p>'
content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, 4!</p>'
content.innerHTML = '<p class="greeting">Hello, 5!</p>'
```

When I say easier to maintain, the most immediate benefit here is that changing the value assigned to the content variable is a simple task, requiring the editing of one line of code versus many.

Modifying the DOM is a relatively costly exercise in terms of CPU time. While you won’t notice it in most of your JavaScript code, these inefficiencies can add up in tight loops and complex operations, so avoiding inline DOM manipulation when you expect to perform many operations is a good idea.

### Using the Browser Object Model

In addition to the HTML document, the browser provides an object model centered around the browser itself. This includes multiple objects, the most important of which—and the one we will cover here—is the window object.

You’re already using the window object and don’t know it because the window object is simply one level up from the document object.

This…

```
let content = document.getElementById("content")
```

…is the same as this…

```
let content = window.document.getElementById("content")
```

…but fortunately, we don’t have to specify window because the browser automatically handles this for us.

That said, the window object is incredibly useful. It provides a lot of information that can be helpful to web applications. Let’s look at some of that functionality.

We can use the innerWidth and innerHeight to obtain the current size, in pixels, of the ***viewport*** . The viewport is the portion of the browser’s window that is useable for HTML content, minus any scrollbars or similar browser features ( [figure 24](#ch08.xhtml_fig24) ). In our scratch.html example we’ve been building, this would be the white part of the screen where elements can be displayed.

![img](Images/fig24.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_24.png) 

The viewport of the browser window.

To demonstrate the innerWidth and innerHeight functionality of the window object, let’s build a simple page that displays these values and updates them whenever the window is resized.

resize.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-resize_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Resize</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p>
            Window width: <span id="width"></span><br>
            Window height: <span id="height"></span>
         </p>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="resize.js"></script>
</html>
```

resize.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-resize_js.png) 

```
let widthValue = document.getElementById("width")
let heightValue = document.getElementById("height")

function displayWindowSize() {
   widthValue.innerHTML = window.innerWidth
   heightValue.innerHTML = window.innerHeight
}

displayWindowSize()
window.addEventListener("resize", displayWindowSize)
```

If you load the resize.html file you created in your browser, you’ll see it displays the size of the window in pixels. You can test the resize event by resizing the browser. The values should change as you shrink and enlarge the window.

Let’s walk through the details of this code to see how it works. In the HTML, we use two span elements with IDs of width and height to denote where the values will go. I chose to use span because it is commonly used within elements—specifically for small values like numbers. Also, the labels for the values “Window width:” and “Window height:” don’t change, no matter the value. They are displayed in HTML rather than written to the document via JavaScript, thus preventing the need to constantly redraw them. We don’t have that luxury with the width and height spans because they change. Any time we can reduce the amount of work JavaScript or the browser have to do, the more effective and efficient our code will be.

As for resize.js , the code is relatively simple. We start by assigning the span elements with IDs of width and height to widthValue and heightValue respectively. Then, we define a function called displayWindowSize that sets the innerHTML of those two spans to the innerWidth and innerHeight of the window object.

Next, we call the displayWindowSize function once to show the initial value (otherwise it would only work if we resized the browser), then finally add an event listener on the resize event that will call the function displayWindowSize .

There’s more functionality in the window object. We’ll cover this in [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) .

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop: The DOM Menu

Let’s open our trusty shop.js file again and incorporate what we learned about the DOM into our populate method. The way we’re currently adding li elements is far from elegant. We can do better.

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// Display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")

// The inventory object
let menu = {
   inventory: {
      "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
      "Espresso": 3.50,
      "Cappuccino": 4.00,
      "Latte": 4.25
   },
   populate: function(container) {
      for (let item in this.inventory) {
         let price = this.inventory[item].toFixed(2)

         // Create a new li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add text content to the li element
         li.textContent = `${item} - $${price}`

         // Append the li element to the container
         container.appendChild(li)
      }
   }
}

// Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu")

// Populate the menu
menu.populate(menuList)
```

Here, we create a new li element (that’s not yet anywhere in the document), add content to the element consisting of the item name and price separated by a dash and a dollar sign, then append that newly created element to the container via appendChild . (Don’t be confused by the “double” dollar sign—the first one is an actual dollar sign, and the second one is part of the template code ${price} to insert the price variable.) This is a far better solution and makes it easier to change the design in the future if we choose to use another element.

Chapter Recap

» Web page elements are represented by objects in the DOM (Document Object Model). These are organized like branches on a tree.

» The browser is represented in the Browser Object Model and sits above the Document Object Model.

» You can add to or change the content of a web page via the DOM. This doesn’t actually edit the HTML file; rather, it edits the contents of the page in the browser’s memory.

## | 9 |

## Asynchronous JavaScript

Chapter Overview

» JavaScript allows for asynchronous code execution

» Event handlers let you run code when certain events occur

» Promises ensure certain code runs in a pre-defined order

Often in programming, we run code in a ***synchronous*** fashion; that is, the code runs in a particular order. However, when we use JavaScript on our web pages, we will often operate in an ***asynchronous*** fashion. Asynchronous programming allows for multiple bits of code to run at once and usually without blocking the operation of other parts of the code.

We’ve already used asynchronous techniques in this book. Registering an event handler to run a function when something happens on the page is asynchronous, because the code can execute at the same time as other things are happening. We don’t sit and wait for the event to occur before we move on to the rest of our code. That said, there is an order to things.

In JavaScript, code within functions is processed in a synchronous order, but the language can manage these functions and other related blocks of code in an asynchronous manner.

### Order of Execution

When you load a web page, a lot of things seem to happen all at once. The web page itself—that is, the main HTML contents—is loaded from the web server. Various other assets are loaded, like images, stylesheets, and even JavaScript files. Of course, these may also be in the browser’s disk cache, but either way, they must be loaded into memory and rendered or executed.

These things seem to happen all at once, and this is because, when you load a web page, multiple threads are spun up to handle different aspects of loading the page. A ***thread*** is a separate execution path of code that can run at the same time as other threads. Often, these are executed on separate cores on your computer or device’s central processing unit (CPU), so they run at (more or less) the same time.

One or more JavaScript files can be loaded. When this happens, unless the async or defer attributes are specified in the script tag, the JavaScript files that are included first are executed first, thus they run in the order you specify in your HTML file. The async attribute tells the browser that the code can be executed asynchronously, and thus the browser will start running the code in it as soon as it is loaded. The defer attribute tells the browser to wait until the page has finished loading before running the code.

```
<!-- This code will be executed in the order the files are listed -->
<script src="scratch1.js"></script>
<script src="scratch2.js"></script>

<!-- This code will be executed right away in the background -->
<script src="scratch.js" async></script>

<!-- This code will be executed after the page has fully loaded -->
<script src="scratch.js" defer></script>
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The <!-- pattern of characters starts a comment in HTML. The pattern --> ends the comment. For a refresher on HTML, please see [appendix I](#appendix01.xhtml) .

[![img](Images/f0156-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-6) 

### Running Code After the Page Loads

There are good reasons why you might not want your JavaScript to run until the full page has loaded. One common use case is when you include JavaScript that modifies HTML or CSS or writes new content to the page. It’s possible to try to make a change to a part of the DOM that doesn’t yet exist.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

DOM stands for Document Object Model.

By waiting until the page has finished loading before executing code that needs the page to be fully loaded, you can be sure that you won’t have any dependency issues—that is, code that requires certain elements to be in place trying to run before they’re present. You can use the defer attribute as described previously in this chapter, but a more common way to address this is to run your code when the DOMContentLoaded event fires.

When we say an event *fires* , we mean that the event is triggered to run. Since the events happen largely outside of our control, they are fired from another source (i.e., the browser, or the user’s interaction with the browser), and thus it is commonly said that an event fires rather than is triggered or happens. Plus, saying that an event fires just sounds cooler, right?

To run code when this event occurs, we use addEventListener .

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-09-01.png) 

```
function reliesOnPageLoaded() {
   // This code needs to be executed after the page is loaded
}

document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", reliesOnPageLoaded)
```

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

From a performance perspective, my first choice is to let the browser decide when to load and run my JavaScript code. I say this for the same reason I don’t try to implement my own database engines or write my own operating systems. Programmers far smarter than I have spent countless hours optimizing the performance of the JavaScript engines in browsers. If you follow good practices (which we’ll discuss throughout this book), you should get good performance.

### Using Event Handlers

We’ve already used event handlers throughout this book, and in the last section we used document.addEventListener to run a function when the page finishes loading. This is a common approach in JavaScript, so there’s no way we could have delayed discussion about it until now. But I have glossed over this functionality in large part as we’ve proceeded, promising a more detailed discussion along the way.

Before discussing event handlers, let’s talk about the events themselves. You’ve seen a few already, like DOMContentLoaded , click , change , and resize , but there are plenty more. Let’s look at some of the most common events ( [table 3](#ch09.xhtml_tab3) ).

![img](Images/table-3.png) 

| Event Name | Event Description |
| DOMContentLoaded | This event is fired when the page is fully loaded. |
| click | This event is fired when a button is clicked. |
| submit | This event is fired when a form is submitted. |
| change | This event is fired when an input element is changed. |
| keydown | This event is fired when the user presses a key. |
| mouseover | This event is fired when the user moves their mouse over a particular element. |
| mouseout | This event is fired when the user moves their mouse away from an element where they had previously moused over. |
| ontouchstart | This event is fired on a device with a touch screen when the user begins touching an element. |
| ontouchend | This event is fired on a device with a touch screen when the user lifts their finger from an element. |

A list of commonly used JavaScript events on web pages.

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

There are many more events. For a full list, please access “JavaScript Events” in your Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript

To capture these or any other events we simply use the document.addEventListener method. How we use this depends on what kind of event we’re trying to handle.

If it’s an event not tied to an element, we refer to it as a ***global event*** . A global event occurs within the document rather than from some interaction the user has with an HTML element.

For a global event, we specify the name of the event and the function to call as we did in the previous section. This code assumes that a function named reliesOnPageLoaded exists.

```
document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", reliesOnPageLoaded)
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Of course, you don’t have to name your function reliesOnPageLoaded , you can call it anything you like.

***HTML events*** are events that occur as a result of a user interaction with the page. To bind a function to a specific HTML element, we must first obtain an object that references the element, then call the method addEventListener on that object. In this case, after running this line of code, the submitButton variable is now a reference to an element with the ID of submit .

```
submitButton = document.getElementById("submit")
```

Then we can add the event handler. This code assumes that we have a function called runWhenClicked .

```
submitButton.addEventListener("click", runWhenClicked)
```

We don’t have to specify a function name. We can also provide it inline, like this:

```
submitButton.addEventListener("click", function() {
   alert("You clicked me!")
})
```

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

Generally, I prefer specifying function names rather than using inline functions. This gives me three advantages. First, if I later decide to add additional code to the event handler, it is easier to do. Secondly, it forces me to think a bit more about how to better structure my code.

Finally, and perhaps most importantly, if I want multiple event handlers to run the same code, I need only specify the function name multiple times, rather than repeat the same code over and over. Repeating the same code however many times is required is error-prone and a pain, especially if I later need to change that function.

### Inline Event Handlers

HTML allows you to specify JavaScript to run when certain events occur, without the need for separate JavaScript code. While this can be handy in a pinch, I don’t advise it, because it mixes JavaScript directly with your HTML, which can make it harder for non-developers to edit HTML and make it harder to track down a problem when something goes wrong. But you will see this paradigm sometimes, so we’ll briefly discuss it.

If we want to display an alert when the user clicks a button, we can use this approach:

```
<button onclick="alert('You clicked me')">Click me!</button>
```

This essentially registers the JavaScript code…

```
alert('You clicked me')
```

…to run when the button’s click event is fired. You can put any JavaScript code you want in this attribute within the quotes, but if you’re going to use more than one line, you should call a function instead.

```
<button onclick="showAlertMessage()">Click me!</button>
```

You can define this alert function in an included JavaScript file, or directly within a <script></script> block on the page.

As I said, this is fine for a quick project or a simple HTML file where you don’t want to include a separate JavaScript file, but for anything beyond the most basic purposes, I would strongly recommend avoiding this technique.

### Self-Referring Event Handlers

If you want to refer to the element that triggered the event within the event handler, you can use the this keyword. Consider our previous example:

```
submitButton.addEventListener("click", function() {
   alert("You clicked me!")
})
```

If we wanted to change the text label (i.e., the textContent attribute) of the button, we would use:

```
submitButton.addEventListener("click", function() {
   this.textContent = "You clicked me!"
})
```

When the user clicks the button, the text will change from whatever it was to “You clicked me!”

You can also use closure with this concept. Let’s consider an example where we want a button to display how many times it’s been clicked.

clicked.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-clicked_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Click This Button</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <button id="theButton">Clicked 0 times!</button>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="clicked.js"></script>
</html>
```

clicked.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-clicked_js.png) 

```
let theButton = document.getElementById("theButton");

theButton.addEventListener("click", (function() {
   // Initially set the clickCount to 0
   let clickCount = 0

   return function() {
   // Increment the clickCount
      clickCount += 1
      // Update the button's textContent
      this.textContent = `Clicked ${clickCount} times!`
   }
})())
```

If you load the clicked.html file in your browser, you’ll initially see that the button says “Clicked 0 times!”. After clicking the button five times, it will read “Clicked 5 times!” ( [figure 25](#ch09.xhtml_fig25) ).

![img](Images/fig25.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_25.png) 

Button on initial page load and after clicking five times.

### Setting Background Tasks with setTimeout

The setTimeout() function can execute code in the background. To see this in action, let’s fire up our trusty JavaScript console and define the following function:

```
function timeMachine() {
   console.log("Hello from the past!")
}
```

Now to fire this function five seconds in the future:

```
setTimeout(timeMachine, 5000)
```

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

When providing a function as an argument, as in the case of timeMachine to setTimeout , do not use parentheses. If you need to pass arguments to it, you can define an anonymous function as the argument, like this: setTimeout(function() { timeMachine("Hello") }, 5000) .

After you run the setTimeout line, in five seconds (or 5,000 milliseconds), you’ll see the message from past you.

You can use this functionality to create time-delayed events on web pages. Let’s say you want to make a link visible only after 10 seconds. That’s not an entirely contrived example—I remember once being asked by a company to have a link visible to proceed to the next page only after the visitor had watched a brief video. The setTimeout function worked well for this. Let’s see it in action.

timeout.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-timeout_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>You Will Watch This Video</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <div id="current">
            <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dQw4w9WgXcQ"
               target="_blank">Watch the Video</a>
         </div>
         <br><br>
         <div id="next" style="display: none;">
            <a href=https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XqZsoesa55w
               target="_blank">Watch the Next Video</a>
         </div>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="timeout.js"></script>
</html>
```

timeout.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-timeout_js.png) 

```
let nextLink = document.getElementById("next")
function showNextLink() {
   nextLink.style.display = "block"
}
setTimeout(showNextLink, 5000)
```

In the HTML file, the only thing of note is that I set style="display: none;" for the second link so it isn’t visible when the page loads.

In the JavaScript file, we set nextLink to the div with ID next , then define a function that sets the display CSS attribute on the nextLink object to block so that it’s visible (recall that it was set to none by default in the HTML file). Then, we use setTimeout with the function we defined and a timeout of 5,000 milliseconds.

I wanted to include a full YouTube video embedded in the code, but this doesn’t work when viewing an HTML file locally, so you’ll have to imagine the link is a video. You can click it if you want, but either way, when the page loads, after five seconds the next video link will show. I set it to five seconds so that you can see the results quickly, but feel free to set it to any value you like to test longer lengths. If you wanted to set it to three minutes, you would use 180,000, since the value is specified in milliseconds.

### Using setInterval to Periodically Run Code

JavaScript can run a function repeatedly at a set interval using the aptly named setInterval . Here’s an example:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-09-02.png) 

```
let tickCount = 0

function incrementTicks() {
   tickCount += 1
   console.log(`There have been ${tickCount} ticks so far.`)
}

const interval = setInterval(incrementTicks, 1000)
```

If you run this on the console, you’ll see…

```
There have been 1 ticks so far.
There have been 2 ticks so far.
```

…and so on.

The first argument to setInterval is the function to call, and the second argument is the delay between calls in milliseconds (1,000 being one second). You can optionally specify additional arguments after the delay, and these will be supplied to the function you call in the order you provide.

You can stop the function being executed by running:

```
clearInterval(interval)
```

You can clear the interval within the function that is being called, which is useful to have a self-limiting interval:

```
let tickCount = 0

function incrementTicks() {
   tickCount += 1
   console.log(`There have been ${tickCount} ticks so far.`)
   if (tickCount >= 5) {
      clearInterval(interval)
   }
}

const interval = setInterval(incrementTicks, 1000)
```

Here, we clear the interval if the tickCount is greater than or equal to five.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Be careful when using setInterval , especially when using a low millisecond delay (less than 1,000). If the function takes a while to complete, you can encounter slow performance and unpredictable results.

We’ll cover more on setInterval when we discuss animation in [chapter 13](#ch13.xhtml) .

### Making Promises We Can Keep

We can use a JavaScript ***promise*** to ensure proper out-of-order execution. A promise is an object that contains code that can take a long time to execute. This code is commonly called the producing code. The consuming code—that is, the code that relies on the results of the producing code—is “promised” a value or an error. The best way to explain this is to look at an example:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-09-03.png) 

```
let examplePromise = new Promise(function(resolve, reject) {
   // First set success to false so that only a successful
   // process will trigger a success

   success = false
   // Perform any long-running task
   // If task successful, set success = true

   if (success) {
      resolve("Success!")
   } else {
      reject("Failure!")
   }
});

examplePromise.then(function(value) {
   console.log(value)
})
```

Let’s walk through this example code to see what happens ( [figure 26](#ch09.xhtml_fig26) ).

First, we define the promise. The object is examplePromise , and inside this function we perform our long-running task. It will often include a setTimeout function, or some other functionality that takes a while, like requesting data from another site. If the process was successful, the success variable is set to true , and the resolve method is called with any arguments desired to pass outside the promise code. If success is false , then we call the reject method, along with any arguments desired. Now our promise is created.

To trigger the functionality inside the promise, we call examplePromise.then , along with a function that makes use of the value returned. This value comes from either the resolve or reject method based on how the code progressed inside the function (i.e., the producing code).

![img](Images/fig26.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_26.png) 

The point of a promise is to run code in the background, but then have a process wait for that code to finish so that code can be executed when that happens, without having to tie up the main execution of the JavaScript code.

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Try setting the success variable to true to see how the previous example behaves.

Chapter Recap

» JavaScript generally follows a top-down execution pattern, but also allows for asynchronous code execution.

» You can use event handlers to register functions that will run when certain events occur. These events will run asynchronously.

» JavaScript promises allow you to define pre-defined functions or anonymous functions that are run after other tasks complete, thereby guaranteeing a certain order of execution.

## Part III

## Architecting JavaScript

## | 10 |

## JavaScript Design Patterns

Chapter Overview

» Design patterns help you organize your code

» Decentralized construction helps you reuse components

» Singletons and prototypes are two commonly used design patterns

To get the most out of JavaScript, or any programming language for that matter, it’s critical to understand what a ***design pattern*** is and how it works in JavaScript. A design pattern is the methodology you use to lay out the data and logic in your program. If your data and logic represent the players and the instruments in an orchestra, and the sheet music represents your classes, the design patterns you use could be thought of as your conductor.

I’m going to take an interesting approach with this chapter and start by telling you what I do in my day-to-day programming work, and then describe other common patterns from a more theoretical point of view. This isn’t to say my method is superior to others. In fact, I would argue against my method being the best for every situation—or even most situations. So, the patterns I’ll show you are born out of experience rather than theory, because that’s the way I learned.

The goal in showing you my approach is to give you a starting place to develop your own strategies for dealing with programming issues you face.

### Decentralized Construction

I watched a lot of Star Trek™ as a child, and one of the things that captivated me the most on the show was the concept of the Borg. There were many interesting aspects to this supervillain, but the one that caught me the most is that they favor extreme decentralization as a design philosophy. In other words, rather than have one central control system that dictated all functions, each individual portion of their infrastructure contained the code and technology to bootstrap any component they needed. This strategy made the Borg resilient, and I’ve found this strategy useful when solving a lot of technical problems. Having a single object know everything about your application isn’t exactly possible, but we’re after a guiding principle here rather than a strict roadmap.

![img](Images/Important_icon.png) 

In programming, we often encourage the separation of concerns, especially isolating code from design and content. In web programming, content is the HTML, design is the CSS, and code would be JavaScript. The concept that I’m about to introduce will break these barriers.

I’m doing this primarily as a teaching tool to encourage you to think about objects encapsulating all three aspects of the web. I also want to demonstrate that objects can render themselves, making that object responsible for its entire lifecycle—both in logic and when displayed to the user.

Now, I said I wouldn’t start with theory, so let’s get right to the code.

The first strategy here is to let your data know how to display itself. Remember our color selection code from [chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml) ? Don’t worry if you don’t, we’ve covered a lot since then. But you can find it in switch.html , switch.css , and switch.js . Let’s rewrite this using the decentralized construction pattern.

First, let’s look at the key to all of this—our color selector input code:

```
<select id="colorSelector" name="colorSelector">
   <option value="red">Red</option>
   <option value="orange">Orange</option>
   <option value="yellow">Yellow</option>
   <option value="green">Green</option>
   <option value="blue">Blue</option>
   <option value="indigo">Indigo</option>
   <option value="violet">Violet</option>
</select>
```

Rather than list this in static HTML, we can create an object that contains these colors. Furthermore, this object can possess the ability to ***render*** itself; that is, to have the ability to display itself.

To demonstrate this technique, let’s create three new files, switch2.html , switch2.css , and switch2.js . First, let’s show the code, then we’ll go through it line by line to see how it works:

switch2.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <link rel="stylesheet" href="switch2.css">
      <title>Switch 2</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <form>
            <div id="colorSelection">
            </div>
         </form>
      <div id="colorSwatch"></div>
      </div>
   </body>
   <script src="switch2.js"></script>
</html>
```

switch2.css

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch_css.png) 

```
#colorSwatch {
   width: 20em;
   height: 5em;
   background-color: red;
   border: 0.1em solid gray;
   border-radius: 0.75em;
   margin-top: 1em;
}
```

switch2.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch2-01.png) 

```
const colorSelector = {
   colors: {
      "red": "#FF0000",
      "orange": "#FFA500",
      "yellow": "#FFFF00",
      "green": "#008000",
      "blue": "#0000FF",
      "indigo": "#4B0082",
      "violet": "#EE82EE"
   },
   render: (parent, id, swatch, className = "") => {
      let select = document.createElement("select")
      select.id = id
      if (className) {
         select.className = className
      }
      for (let color in colorSelector.colors) {
         let option = document.createElement("option")
         option.value = colorSelector.colors[color]
         option.innerHTML = color.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + color.slice(1)
         select.appendChild(option)
      }
      select.addEventListener("change", function(event) {
         swatch.style.backgroundColor = event.target.value
      })
      parent.appendChild(select)
   }
}

let colorSwatch = document.getElementById("colorSwatch")
let colorSelection = document.getElementById("colorSelection")
colorSelector.render(colorSelection, "colorSelect", colorSwatch)
```

Let’s start with the HTML code. It hasn’t changed much from switch.html , except that it doesn’t list the select input, and we now have a div called colorSelection rather than a select called colorSelector . Additionally, we reference switch2.js . I also renamed the id of the color display box to colorSwatch . The CSS code in switch2.css is identical, except the ID has been changed to colorSwatch to match.

Now let’s look at switch2.js . First, it creates an object named colorSelector . This object has two main components—the data, found in colors , and the render method, which uses some interesting code to create a select input, and the options it contains, from seemingly thin air. And it did this without using a bunch of ugly string concatenation.

Let’s examine the render method in further detail. The method itself takes four arguments: the parent element (named parent ); the id , which is the ID given to the newly-created select ; the swatch argument, which is the color swatch element; and className , which is, by default, set to an empty string. We’ll see how that works shortly.

The first step in constructing this select element is to use document.createElement to make a select element (provided as the argument " select " in this case). I know it may seem odd to create an element out of thin air without defining it in the HTML file, but that’s essentially what document.createElement does. This element is stored in the variable named select , but isn’t active on the page just yet.

Next, we assign the id argument to our newly minted select element’s id property, then we check to see if the className argument is empty. If it isn’t, we set the select element’s className to the value provided from the argument. Then, we iterate through the colors. For each one, we create a new option element, set the value of the option element to the HTML color code of the color, then set the innerHTML to the name of the color.

But there’s something a bit strange going on with the name, isn’t there? Couldn’t I just have the code option.innerHTML = color to display the name? Absolutely, but the name would be all lowercase, and I want the first letter to be capitalized.

To do this, we take the string color and use charAt(0) , which returns the first character, then use toUpperCase to convert that first character. But now we just have the first character of the color name, and although it’s now capitalized, we need the rest of the string because we’re assigning the value to innerHTML , which will be used as the string between the option tags (i.e., the visible name for the option ). To do that, we add back color , but use slice , with the argument of 1, to return the color name starting from position 1 (which is the second character).

I know we’re glossing over the functionality of slice for now. We’ll explore more about slice in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) on advanced strings. Additionally, the multiple dots between methods can be a bit confusing, so see [figure 27](#ch10.xhtml_fig27) on how these values are passed between methods.

![img](Images/fig27.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_27.png) 

In much the same way as equations are simplified in algebra or fractions are reduced, dot notation performs an operation on the value and then returns that value inline. This is then chained to the next operation if there’s more than one, until a single value can be assigned (in this example, to option.innerHTML ).

After the code so far in the iteration of the loop, we have a full option tag, which looks like this:

```
<option value="#FF0000">Red</option>
```

We then use the appendChild method on the select object (the one created via createElement ) to append this newly created option to the select input. We then repeat this as many times as necessary to iterate through the colors.

After we’re done with the for loop, we have a fully constructed select input with option elements ready to go, but there’s one final step we need to take to make this functional. To add the event handling, we use the addEventListener on the select object to specify an inline function that will handle what happens when the select input is changed (i.e., another option is selected).

The event argument is provided as an argument to this event handler, which contains information about the event that just fired. In this case, it gives us the target of the event, which is the select input element. It might seem strange to refer to it this way, but remember that we don’t know ahead of time what the ID of this select input will be, so we’ll need a way to reference it. In this case, we let the event argument give us that information. The event.target value gives us the select element, so we can use event.target.value , which obtains the value of the target from the event, and sets the swatch backgroundColor CSS style to the selected value (which is the value attribute of the option that is currently selected by the user).

Now our select input has data, options, and an event handler—yet it still isn’t on the page. It just exists in memory at this point, in the select object. To put it on the page, we use parent.appendChild(select) , which adds the select to the Document Object Model as a child element of the parent (whichever element was passed in to the render method as its parent argument). This effectively inserts the completed code inside the element of the parent.

Our object is defined, but nothing really happens until these three lines at the bottom:

```
let colorSwatch = document.getElementById("colorSwatch")
let colorSelection = document.getElementById("colorSelection")
colorSelector.render(colorSelection, "colorSelect", colorSwatch)
```

The first two variables obtain the IDs of the swatch and the parent div that will contain the select box, then the third line calls the render method on the colorSelector object we created, passing the colorSelection div as the parent, the string “colorSelect” as the ID to use for the new select input element, and the colorSwatch element so the event code knows which element’s background color to change.

At first glance, this seems like an incredibly convoluted way to create a simple HTML select input. Couldn’t we have just specified this in the HTML and been done with it? Yes, of course. However, this approach offers several advantages:

» The color names and values exist in JavaScript instead of HTML, so, if these change, the HTML doesn’t have to be modified. Furthermore, the color names and values can come from an outside source, like a database or an external service.

» The select input knows how to display and handle its own events. If this code needs to be put on multiple HTML pages, there is no need to insert it over and over: it can simply be added to any page by including the switch2.js file and ensuring the necessary parent div is created and supplied as an argument to the render method.

» Additional events can be defined, and the code to handle them rests within the object, keeping everything in one place so it can easily be maintained by you or other programmers.

These are just a few of the many advantages to this decentralized construction approach, but the reusability of it is what has been most useful to me. Having one JavaScript file or object that can do so much, especially over multiple pages, can save you a tremendous amount of time. What’s more, if a change is needed to all of them across an entire site, modifying one simple file takes care of the job.

This concept of self-constructing elements lets you look at web page construction in a whole new way. When your data comes from an outside source, like a third-party service or a database, this can save you hours of time. Now that we’ve discussed the general concept, let’s look at some additional patterns that we can use to enhance this code and make our lives even easier.

### Utility Functions

Let’s explore some common functions that will solve problems you’ll encounter frequently in your day-to-day work. I call functions that make things easier “utility functions.” They usually perform a single task and can sometimes be just one line of code.

Remember this line?

```
option.innerHTML = color.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + color.slice(1)
```

We can put this into a function and use it wherever we need.

```
function capFirstLetter(string) {
   return string.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + string.slice(1)
}
```

Armed with this function, our render method looks a bit cleaner:

```
function capFirstLetter(string) {
   return string.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + string.slice(1)
}

const colorSelector = {
   colors: {
      "red": "#FF0000",
      "orange": "#FFA500",
      "yellow": "#FFFF00",
      "green": "#008000",
      "blue": "#0000FF",
      "indigo": "#4B0082",
      "violet": "#EE82EE"
   },
   render: (parent, id, swatch, className = "") => {
      let select = document.createElement("select")
      select.id = id
      if (className) {
         select.className = className
      }
      for (let color in colorSelector.colors) {
         let option = document.createElement("option")
         option.value = colorSelector.colors[color]
         option.innerHTML = capFirstLetter(color)
         select.appendChild(option)
      }

      select.addEventListener("change", function(event) {
         swatch.style.backgroundColor = event.target.value
      })
      parent.appendChild(select)
   }
}
```

Granted, that’s not improving things dramatically, but it does make it a bit simpler. Let’s keep going. How about a function to change the background color of an element:

```
function changeBgColor(element, color) {
   element.style.backgroundColor = color
}
```

Now let’s use the function:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-10-01.png) 

```
function capFirstLetter(string) {
   return string.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + string.slice(1)
}

function changeBgColor(element, color) {
   element.style.backgroundColor = color
}

const colorSelector = {
   colors: {
      "red": "#FF0000",
      "orange": "#FFA500",
      "yellow": "#FFFF00",
      "green": "#008000",
      "blue": "#0000FF",
      "indigo": "#4B0082",
      "violet": "#EE82EE"
   },
   render: (parent, id, swatch, className = "") => {
      let select = document.createElement("select")
      select.id = id
      if (className) {
         select.className = className
      }
      for (let color in colorSelector.colors) {
         let option = document.createElement("option")
         option.value = colorSelector.colors[color]
         option.innerHTML = capFirstLetter(color)
         select.appendChild(option)
      }

      select.addEventListener("change", function(event) {
         changeBgColor(swatch, event.target.value)
      })

      parent.appendChild(select)
   }
}
```

You might ask, what did we save with these two functions? Well, in this example, only a few characters. But, if you change the background color of your elements a lot, or you need to frequently capitalize the first letter of your strings, these two utility functions can save you a tremendous amount of time. In fact, on a very large website, they could save you hours of coding. Additionally, you’ll be able to take these functions and use them on other projects!

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

I have a repository filled with frequently used functions that I have written over the years. I recommend you start one and fill it with anything you find useful, be it code you write or code you find on the web. It doesn’t have to be fancy—just a file called mycode.js on your computer or cloud storage account works well. Or you can store your functions in your favorite note-taking application. Feel free to add these two utility functions capFirstLetter and changeBgColor to your new library of code.

There’s also another advantage to using this utility function approach. By containerizing your code into bite-sized chunks of reusable logic (be they objects, functions, or both), you can make it easier to apply a logic change across your entire project. For example, if you find a more efficient way of performing a task, changing a few lines of code gives you the ability to apply that change to the rest of the site (provided those files use script tags to include the JavaScript file that defines the function).

![img](Images/On_your_Own_icon.png) 

Are there other utility functions you can create to optimize the render method? I see a few that we could use. Try to identify at least one of them, and if you’re feeling adventurous, try writing it!

### Exploring Common Patterns

Now that we’ve seen my day-to-day approach, let’s sample a bit of design pattern theory and look at common design paradigms. You’ll see elements of my approach in these as well, and I hope these will also spark ideas for enhancements to your programming workflow.

#### Singletons

A ***singleton*** is a design pattern that restricts a class to instantiate only a single object. This ensures that only one object instance of a class can be created.

You might wonder why you’d want to restrict a class to only having one object instantiated from it. A common use case of this is when a class is tasked with managing important data that should not be duplicated. To see how this could be a problem, consider the following code:

```
let db = new sqlite3.Database("mydata.db", (error) => {
   if (error) {
      // Handle the error
   }
})
```

In this code, we open an SQLite database called mydata.db . We’ll talk more on SQLite in [chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml) , but for now, the pattern of code is more pertinent to this discussion.

This code creates a connection with the database file (or server, if you are using a different database system like MySQL, Microsoft SQL, or PostgreSQL) and assigns the object to the variable db . We only want there to be one such object, because having multiple handles to the database open without managing their states correctly could create issues. Additionally, if an object was created many times by mistake, the database server could become overwhelmed with requests.

The absolute simplest way to declare a singleton is to skip classes altogether and simply create an object without a class. This is what we did in the colorSelector object in the previous section. However, if you want to create a class and ensure only one copy of it can be in use, you can use this technique:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-10-02.png) 

```
class MySingletonClass {
   constructor() {
      if (MySingletonClass._instance) {
         throw new Error("You can only create one instance of this class.")
      }
      MySingletonClass._instance = this
   }

   // Rest of class code would go here
}
```

To obtain the first object, you would use:

```
let mySingletonObject = new MySingletonClass()
```

If you try to obtain another object from this class…

```
let mySecondSingletonObject = new MySingletonClass()
```

…you’ll get the following error:

```
Uncaught Error: You can only create one instance of this class.
```
