In this case, the constructor checks to see if _instance is declared, and if so, throws an error (more on this in a moment). If it isn’t declared, it sets the variable _instance to this , which is shared by all objects from that class.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The _instance variable isn’t built into the ES6 standard. The underscore at the start is a general convention among JavaScript developers to indicate that the variable is private to the object. By private, I mean that it isn’t shared with other instances of the class.

When we specify this as a value, we mean the current object where the code is executing. So, in this case, this becomes the object that will be returned by the constructor (and then referenced by mySingletonObject ). But giving it any value at all will make the code in the if (MySingletonClass._instance) block execute next time you try to create an instance of the class, so that throws the error and exits the constructor, therefore not creating the new object.

When I say “throws an error”, I mean that the line…

```
throw new Error("You can only create one instance of this class.")
```

…creates an error in much the same way that JavaScript would raise an error if an error condition arose from within your code. In this case, we’re preventing a second object from being created, but the error text can be anything.

We’ll cover errors in depth in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) .

#### Prototypes

JavaScript’s ***prototypal inheritance*** provides a way to give objects the ability to acquire methods and properties from other objects. In fact, any object can be used as a prototype, giving you tremendous flexibility when creating new objects.

Let’s rework our colorSelector object to be more useful using the prototype pattern. We don’t have to change the HTML or CSS, just the JavaScript:

switch2.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-switch2.png) 

```
// Utility functions

function capFirstLetter(string) {
   return string.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + string.slice(1)
}

function changeBgColor(element, color) {
   element.style.backgroundColor = color
}

// Data for selector

const colors = {
   "red": "#FF0000",
   "orange": "#FFA500",
   "yellow": "#FFFF00",
   "green": "#008000",
   "blue": "#0000FF",
   "indigo": "#4B0082",
   "violet": "#EE82EE"
}

// Define function to be executed on the change event

function changeColor(swatch, event) {
   changeBgColor(swatch, event.target.value)
}

const selectorPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, onChange, swatch = "", className = "") => {
      let select = document.createElement("select")
      select.id = id
      if (className) {
         select.className = className
      }
      for (let value in values) {
         let option = document.createElement("option")
         option.value = values[value]
         option.innerHTML = capFirstLetter(value)
         select.appendChild(option)
      }

      select.addEventListener("change", (event) => {
         onChange(swatch, event)
      })

      parent.appendChild(select)
   }
}

let colorSwatch = document.getElementById("colorSwatch")
let colorSelection = document.getElementById("colorSelection")

let colorSelector = Object.create(selectorPrototype)

colorSelector.render(colors, colorSelection,
   "colorSelect", changeColor, colorSwatch)
```

The prototype class, now called selectorPrototype , is this:

```
const selectorPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, onChange, swatch = "", className = "") => {
      let select = document.createElement("select")
      select.id = id
      if (className) {
         select.className = className
      }
      for (let value in values) {
         let option = document.createElement("option")
         option.value = values[value]
         option.innerHTML = capFirstLetter(value)
         select.appendChild(option)
      }

      select.addEventListener("change", (event) => {
         onChange(swatch, event)
      })

      parent.appendChild(select)
   }
}
```

And the code to create our colorSwatch object from this prototype is at the end:

```
let colorSelector = Object.create(selectorPrototype)

colorSelector.render(colors, colorSelection,
   "colorSelect", changeColor, colorSwatch
```

We still call the render method, but I had to change it a bit to make it work with this design pattern because the changeColor function, now defined outside the object, must be passed as an argument so that the addEventListener line can register this function as the event.

But you might notice something strange with the event listener. Rather than pass it like this…

```
select.addEventListener("change", onChange(swatch, event))
```

…we must use this…

```
select.addEventListener("change", (event) => {
   onChange(swatch, event)
})
```

…because onChange(swatch, event) would run the onChange function immediately and not when the event occurs. Remember that functions passed as arguments cannot have parentheses after them, otherwise they will be executed immediately.

But we need to pass arguments to it, so instead we must use an anonymous function that takes the event argument as a parameter (which is why event is in parentheses) and then runs onChange with swatch and event as the arguments. But it’s enclosed in the anonymous function, so it only executes when the event fires.

One final note about this improved switch2.js example: the colors (and the function to be run upon the change event) are now defined outside the object. We need to do this in order to make this object more generic, or else we wouldn’t be able to change the data that would serve as the values for the select input element.

Having this prototype gives us the ability to quickly spin up a new select element whenever we like with just two lines of code. Furthermore, now that the colors data (internalized within the object as values so it is appropriate for more than color names) is outside the prototype, we can feed a variety of data sets to the objects created from selectorPrototype , letting us use this for colors, sizes, states, countries, credit card expiration months and years, and pretty much anything else you can think of.

For example, if you’re using it for sizes, you might define a data set like this:

```
const sizes = {
   "s": "Small",
   "m": "Medium",
   "l": "Large",
}
```

And then create a select element like this:

```
let sizeSelector = Object.create(selectorPrototype)

sizeSelector.render(sizes, sizeSelection, "sizeSelect", changeSize)
```

The variables are different here, but the concept is the same. I made swatch and className optional in this revamped version, so if they don’t apply—as we probably wouldn’t have a swatch with sizes—we can omit them.

The possibilities here are endless. This selectorPrototype is now part of your library, which you can use in other projects. I encourage you to consider additional uses for this pattern and create your own prototypes.

There are other design patterns in JavaScript, and we’ll run across a few more of these as we progress through the book, but the main goal of this chapter, including explaining these common approaches, is to introduce you to the concept of architecting your code in repeatable ways. This will save you considerable time and make it easier to modify and expand your code in the future.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop: Prototyping our Menu

The coffee shop code—specifically the menu object—in [chapter 8](#ch08.xhtml) uses my favorite paradigm of self-constructing data objects:

```
let menu = {
   inventory: {
      "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
      "Espresso": 3.50,
      "Cappuccino": 4.00,
      "Latte": 4.25
   },
   populate: function(container) {
      for (let item in this.inventory) {
         let price = this.inventory[item]

         // Create a new li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add text content to the li element
         li.textContent = item + " - $" + price.toFixed(2)

         // Append the li element to the container
         container.appendChild(li)
      }
   }
}
```

The data is contained within the object, but it doesn’t necessarily have to be. It can be retrieved from an outside source, but the main thing to see here is that the populate method contains the logic necessary to display the object. The menu object is an independent chunk of data that comes with its own way to represent it to the user.

We can make this even more functional by abstracting the populate method into a prototype we can use for all lists. Here’s the updated code. We’ll have to make a small tweak to index.html first, however. The JavaScript code is commented step by step to explain what is happening in each line.

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

For this file, you need only change <ul id= " coffee-menu " ></ul> to <div id= " coffee-menu-container " ></div> because the list prototype will build this element for us.

index.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-index_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
   <meta charset="UTF-8">
   <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
   <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
   <title>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</title>
</head>
<body>
   <header>
      <div class="logo-container">
         <img src="coffee-cup.svg" alt="Coffee Cup" class="logo">
      </div>
      <h1>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</h1>
   </header>
   <section class="welcome">
      <h2>Welcome</h2>
      <p>Welcome to ClydeBank Coffee Shop! We serve the best coffee in town.</p>
   </section>
   <section class="shop">
      <h2>Shop</h2>
      <div id="coffee-menu-container">
      </div>
   </section>
   <section class="about-us">
      <h2>About Us</h2>
      <p>Our mission is to provide the highest quality coffee to our community.</p>
   </section>
   <section class="contact-us">
      <h2>Contact Us</h2>
      <p>Email us at <a href="mailto:support@clydebankmedia.com">support@clydebankmedia.com</a> or call us at (800) 340-3069.</p>
   </section>
   <footer>
      <p>Copyright &copy; 2024 ClydeBank Coffee Shop</p>
   </footer>
   <script src="shop.js"></script>
</body>
</html>
```

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// Display a quick message in the console.
console.log("ClydeBank Coffee Shop is now open!")

// The list prototype
const listPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, separator = " - $", className = "") =>
{

      // Create the unordered list element
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Assign the HTML element ID
      ul.id = id

      // If className is set, assign it, otherwise ignore
      if (className) {
         ul.className = className
      }

      // Loop through the values array and create a new li element
      // for each
      for (let key in values) {
         // Create a key/value pair in the variables k and v
         let k = key
         let v = values[key]

         // Create the li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add the text content to the li element with the separator
         li.textContent = k + separator + v.toFixed(2)

         // Append the li element to the unordered list
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // Append the unordered list to the parent element
      parent.appendChild(ul)
   }
}

let inventory = {
   "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
   "Espresso": 3.50,
   "Cappuccino": 4.00,
   "Latte": 4.25
}

// Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu-container")

// Create the menu object from the prototype
let menu = Object.create(listPrototype)

// Render the menu
// We supply the separator because we need to specify
// the CSS class and you must provide all optional variables
// to the left of the optional variable you are specifying
menu.render(inventory, menuList, "coffee-menu", " - $", "coffee-list")
```

Chapter Recap

» Design patterns not only help you organize your code but make it easier to maintain and enhance your programs.

» The decentralized construction design pattern encourages component reuse, potentially saving you considerable time over the course of a large project.

» A singleton is a type of class that is restricted to having just one instance. JavaScript’s prototypal inheritance is a technique that gives objects the ability to acquire properties from other objects.

## | 11 |

## Building a JavaScript-Driven Website

Chapter Overview

» A single-page application can power your whole website

» outerHTML and appendChild build new branches on the DOM

» JSON can be used to streamline arguments for methods

Once upon a time, HTML ruled the web. The three components that make up a modern web page—content, styling, and functionality—all lived within a single document. I remember seeing plenty of websites that were simply a collection of HTML files and images.

Then CSS came on the scene and redefined the role of HTML from content plus styling to simply content. CSS allowed us to move the design language and theme away from the HTML files. Now, changing the look and feel of a large website was as simple as editing one file. Our HTML code was cleaner and more easily recognized, too. Life was good.

But where did JavaScript fit in? Mostly as a smattering of <script></script> elements throughout the page, and perhaps a generic site-wide script.js file (or something similar) that was included on our pages. These often had utility functions, or were perhaps responsible for a drop-down menu at the top of the page. They were more of an add-on rather than part of the core of the site.

There were exceptions, of course, but this describes the general evolution of the website through the last two decades. Now, it’s quite common to have JavaScript drive the bulk of the site, leaving HTML for the job it is best suited for—content and page structure. This makes HTML more the seating plan of the orchestra rather than the conductor.

This isn’t just using technology for technology’s sake. This JavaScript-centered construction gives us a lot of advantages. First, it makes our code more reusable. By using techniques like the prototype-centered decentralized construction approach I showed you in [chapter 10](#ch10.xhtml) , JavaScript gives us the ability to make reusable design elements from code, letting us think in terms of site building using self-styling and self-animating building blocks.

In fact, your entire site can be built using a single HTML page, giving rise to the popular ***single-page application*** paradigm. This is a web application that updates content on a single page, providing an interactive experience without requiring a full page reload. There are frameworks like jQuery and React.js that will make our jobs easier, but for now, we need to understand how this approach to web design works at the fundamental level before using a pre-made library to help us.

JavaScript libraries come and go, but your knowledge of JavaScript internals will last a lifetime.

### Enhancing the Coffee Shop

Now it’s time to enhance our sample coffee shop with modern JavaScript techniques in a way that would be reusable with any type of product to sell. While the colors of our sample site might not go along with a shop you might want to build, those are easily changeable. The main code that does the heavy lifting is in the shop.js file anyway. The HTML and CSS are interchangeable with anything else you decide to use, as long as the proper HTML element IDs and class names are preserved. You can copy and paste the parts you need into your own project or copy it all for each project you start and edit it as needed. The choice is yours!

Either way, from this point forward, most examples will be specifically geared toward our end project. Along the way, we’ll learn even more about JavaScript! Since there will be more code going forward, the code will be more heavily commented, rather than having a detailed walkthrough after each code sample. I’ll still introduce new concepts, but the code comments will play a larger role in describing the examples.

Here’s our updated index.html file:

![img](Images/FunFact_icon.png) 

Web servers often load index.html as the default HTML page when a visitor accesses a website. That’s why we’re using index.html as our front page.

index.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-index_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
   <meta charset="UTF-8">
   <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
   <!-- Load Google's Open Sans font -->
   <link href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css?family=Open+Sans:400,700" rel="stylesheet">
   <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
   <title>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</title>
</head>
<body>
<div id="main">
   <header>
      <div class="logo-container">
         <img src="coffee-cup.svg" alt="Coffee Cup" class="logo">
      </div>
      <h1>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</h1>
   </header>
   <section class="welcome">
      <h2>Welcome</h2>
      <p>Welcome to ClydeBank Coffee Shop! We serve the best coffee in town.</p>
   </section>
   <section class="shop">
      <h2>Shop</h2>
      <div id="coffee-menu-container">
      </div>
   </section>
   <section class="about-us">
      <h2>About Us</h2>
      <p>Our mission is to provide the highest quality coffee to our community.</p>
   </section>
   <section class="contact-us">
      <h2>Contact Us</h2>
      <p>Email us at <a href="mailto:support@clydebankmedia.com">support@clydebankmedia.com</a> or call us at (800) 340-3069.</p>
   </section>
   <footer>
   <p>Copyright &copy; 2024 ClydeBank Coffee Shop</p>
      </footer>
</div>
<script src="shop.js"></script>
</body>
</html>
```

style.css

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-style_css.png) 

```
/* Reset some default styles in browsers */
body, h1, h2, p, ul {

}
```

If you open this index.html page in your browser again, you’ll see our familiar page. But that’s about to change. Let’s add what every modern website needs these days—a hamburger menu. We could construct this with an image, but let’s use an SVG, so it’s scalable. Let’s add the following code to shop.js :

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop-01_js.png) 

```
// ClydeBank Coffee Shop

// Define hamburger menu object
const hamburgerMenu = {
   render: (parent, id = "hamburger", width = 40, height = 40) => {

      // Construct the SVG
      let svg = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "svg")
      let rect1 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect2 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect3 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")

      // Set the attributes for the SVG
      svg.id = id
      svg.classList.add("hamburger")
      svg.setAttribute("width", width)
      svg.setAttribute("height", height)
      svg.setAttribute("viewBox", "0 0 100 80")
      svg.setAttribute("fill", "#cad317")

      // Set the attributes for the rectangles
      rect1.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect1.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect2.setAttribute("y", "30")
      rect2.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect2.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect3.setAttribute("y", "60")
      rect3.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect3.setAttribute("height", "20")

      // Add the rectangles to the SVG
      svg.appendChild(rect1)
      svg.appendChild(rect2)
      svg.appendChild(rect3)

      parent.appendChild(svg)
   }
}

// The list prototype
const listPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, separator = " - $", className = "") => {

      // Create the unordered list element
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Assign the HTML element ID
      ul.id = id

      // If className is set, assign it, otherwise ignore
      if (className) {
         ul.className = className
   }

      // Loop through the values array and create a new li element
      // for each
      for (let key in values) {
         // Create a key/value pair in the variables k and v
         let k = key
         let v = values[key]

         // Create the li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add the text content to the li element with the separator
         li.textContent = k + separator + v.toFixed(2)

         // Append the li element to the unordered list
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // Append the unordered list to the parent element
      parent.appendChild(ul)
   }
}

let inventory = {
   "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
   "Espresso": 3.50,
   "Cappuccino": 4.00,
   "Latte": 4.25
}

// Run only when the document is loaded and ready to go
document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function(event) {

   // The main div
   const header = document.getElementsByTagName("header")[0]

   // Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
   let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu-container")

   // Create the menu object from the prototype
   let menu = Object.create(listPrototype)

   // Render the inventory menu
   menu.render(inventory, menuList, "coffee-menu", " - $", "coffee-list")

   // Render the hamburger menu
   hamburgerMenu.render(header)
})
```

When we visit index.html in the browser, we’ll see a hamburger menu icon at the top.

Right now, the hamburger menu only knows how to construct itself with SVG elements, which is a nifty feat, but it needs the ability to respond to user interaction. But we’re putting the cart before the horse here, right? If we click it, we need to activate a menu, but we don’t have a menu. So let’s build one of those in the same manner as our hamburger menu icon.

Here’s our new shop.js that includes the event handler for the main menu and a few other enhancements:

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop-02_js.png) 

```
// ClydeBank Coffee Shop

// Define hamburger menu object
const hamburgerMenu = {
   render: (parent, id = "hamburger", mainMenuId = "mainMenu", width = 40, height = 40) => {

      // Construct the SVG
      let svg = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "svg")
      let rect1 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect2 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect3 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")

      // Set the attributes for the SVG
      svg.id = id
      svg.classList.add("hamburger")
      svg.setAttribute("width", width)
      svg.setAttribute("height", height)
      svg.setAttribute("viewBox", "0 0 100 80")
      svg.setAttribute("fill", "#cad317")

      // Set the attributes for the rectangles
      rect1.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect1.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect2.setAttribute("y", "30")
      rect2.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect2.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect3.setAttribute("y", "60")
      rect3.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect3.setAttribute("height", "20")

      // Add the rectangles to the SVG
      svg.appendChild(rect1)
      svg.appendChild(rect2)
      svg.appendChild(rect3)

      // When the hamburger is clicked, show the menu

      // But first, get the menu
      let menu = document.getElementById(mainMenuId)
      svg.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
         // Show the menu
         menu.style.display = "block"
      })

      parent.appendChild(svg)
   }
}

// Define a menu
const mainMenu = {
   render: (parent, links, id = "mainMenu", hamburgerId = "hamburger",
      minWidth = "300px", maxWidth = "800px",
      backgroundColor = "#F4F1DE", borderColor = "#3B1C0B",
      linkColor = "#3B1C0B") => {

      // Create the div element
      let div = document.createElement("div")

      // Set attributes
      div.id = id
      div.style.minWidth = minWidth
      div.style.maxWidth = maxWidth
      div.style.top = "-1px"
      div.style.left = "-1px"
      div.style.position = "absolute"
      div.style.height = "70%"
      div.style.backgroundColor = backgroundColor
      div.style.border = "1px solid " + borderColor

      // We want the menu to be hidden by default
      div.style.display = "none"

      // Construct the menu
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Add some padding to the ul element and make the text bigger
      ul.style.padding = "1em"
      ul.style.fontSize = "1.25em"

      // Build the menu
      for (let link in links) {

         // Create the needed elements
         let li = document.createElement("li")
         let a = document.createElement("a")

         // Make sure the link is readable
         a.style.color = linkColor

         // Hide the dot
         li.style.listStyle = "none"

         // Create the link
         a.href = links[link]
         a.innerHTML = link

         // Add the completed HTML of the link
         // (i.e., <a href="...">...</a>) to the list item
         li.innerHTML = a.outerHTML

         // Add the list item (li) to the unordered list (ul)
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // When the parent of the menu (i.e., the main div)

      // is clicked, hide the menu
      div.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
         // Hide the menu
         div.style.display = "none"
      })

      // Add the list's completed HTML to the div
      div.innerHTML = ul.outerHTML
      parent.appendChild(div)
   }
}

// The list prototype
const listPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, separator = " - $", className = "") => {

      // Create the unordered list element
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Assign the HTML element ID
      ul.id = id

      // If className is set, assign it, otherwise ignore
      if (className) {
         ul.className = className
      }

      // Loop through the values array and create a new li element
      // for each
      for (let key in values) {
         // Create a key/value pair in the variables k and v
         let k = key
         let v = values[key]

         // Create the li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add the text content to the li element with the separator
         li.textContent = k + separator + v.toFixed(2)

         // Append the li element to the unordered list
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // Append the unordered list to the parent element
      parent.appendChild(ul)
   }
}

// Menu structure
// (for now, these are all links to the home page)
const menuLinks = {
   "Home": "index.html",
   "About": "index.html",
   "Contact": "index.html"
}

// Our inventory
let inventory = {
   "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
   "Espresso": 3.50,
   "Cappuccino": 4.00,
   "Latte": 4.25
}

// Run only when the document is loaded and ready to go
document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function(event) {

   // The main div
   const header = document.getElementsByTagName("header")[0]

   // Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
   let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu-container")

   // Create the menu object from the prototype
   let menu = Object.create(listPrototype)

   // Render the inventory menu
   menu.render(inventory, menuList, "coffee-menu", " - $", "coffee-list")

   // Render the menu (hidden by default)
   mainMenu.render(main, menuLinks)

   // Render the hamburger menu
   hamburgerMenu.render(header)
})
```

There’s a lot of new code here, but most of the changes are concentrated in the mainMenu object. Here we build a simple self-rendering object in much the same way we did with the hamburger menu. If you click on the hamburger menu, it will pop up the mainMenu and then a click of the mainMenu div hides it. This is accomplished by toggling the display CSS property.

This code is commented, but there are a few new concepts and items we should explore in greater depth.

#### Getting the Full Picture with outerHTML

While the innerHTML property represents the HTML contents between the start and end tags of an element (for example, the contents between <div> and </div> ), the outerHTML property represents both the HTML contained within the tags and the tags themselves ( [figure 28](#ch11.xhtml_fig28) ). The outerHTML property is useful when we want the entire HTML that composes the element, which is needed in the previous example to construct a div from a completed unordered list.

![img](Images/fig28.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_28.png) 

With outerHTML , we can reference the entire element, including its enclosing tags, innerHTML , outerHTML , </ul> .

#### Solving the Chicken and Egg Dilemma

Which came first, the menu or the hamburger? While I don’t think that problem will exactly keep philosophers up at night, it does pose an interesting question: which needs to be rendered first?

First, we have to wrap both the mainMenu and hamburgerMenu render methods inside the DOMContentLoaded event listener, or else they won’t be able to reference the elements needed to display on the page. So that *must* come first.

Then, we render the mainMenu first so that the hamburgerMenu ’s render method, which contains this line…

```
let menu = document.getElementById(mainMenuId)
```

…will be able to fetch the element correctly. By the way, the mainMenuId variable is given a default value in the render argument list, but, like all default parameters, it can be overwritten if desired.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

When planning your JavaScript code, try to avoid a situation where two methods rely on one another. While there are ways to address this situation, it’s usually better to find an alternative solution. To be clear, it’s fine for one method to rely on another, but for both to rely on the other can create an abundance of problems.

### Using JSON as a Blueprint

You may have noticed that the argument list on our render methods is getting uncomfortably long. We can make this a lot more programmer-friendly by having our arguments derived from JSON. This gives us quite a few advantages, including:

» The attributes of the objects can be edited in human-friendly JSON code, rather than by editing the object code itself. This is especially useful for non-developers who might want to work on this.

» By accepting JSON, the objects can now obtain information from remote sources, like Node.js, Python, PHP, or other services.

Consider this code…

```
render: (parent, id = "hamburger", mainMenuId = "mainMenu", width = 40, height = 40) => {
```

If we rewrote this method’s argument list with JSON, it would work like this:

```
render: (args) => {
```

Then to call it, we would use:

```
args = {
   "parent": header,
   "id": "hamburger",
   "mainMenuId": "mainMenu",
   "width": 40,
   "height": 40,
}

hamburgerMenu.render(args)
```

Inside the render method, to use these values, we’d use:

```
svg.setAttribute("width", args.width)
svg.setAttribute("height", args.height)
```

Using this approach keeps the argument list a lot easier to read, provides easier expandability, and makes it easier to use remote input. The downside is that we must specify the values we had previously given default values, so if you use a lot of default values in your argument list, you might want to consider that before moving to a JSON-based argument list.

### Handling Links with JavaScript

To link to another HTML document, you would typically use:

```
<a href="another-page.html">Another page</a>
```

This takes you to an entirely different page, and the entire DOM and your JavaScript code start running anew. To accomplish this while keeping the same ***state*** —that is, the current set of variables, objects, and condition of your page and its logic and data—you need to treat links differently.

The simplest approach is to put separate pages in their own div elements, like this:

```
<div id="main">
</div>

<div id="about">
</div>

<div id="contact">
</div>
```

Then, via CSS, or directly inline if you like, set the about and contact divs to hidden by setting display: none . If you don’t do this, all pages will appear at once, and that’s probably not what you want.

Here’s what that might look like on a blank HTML page:

```
<div id="main">
</div>

<div id="about" style="display: none;">
</div>

<div id="contact" style="display: none;">
</div>
```

To apply this to our coffee shop index.html , you could treat sections as pages, like this:

```
<section id="about" class="about-us" style="display:none;">
   <h2>About Us</h2>
   <p>Our mission is to provide the highest quality coffee to our community.</p>
</section>
<section id="contact" class="contact-us" style="display: none;">
   <h2>Contact Us</h2>
   <p>Email us at <a href="mailto:support@clydebankmedia.com">support@clydebankmedia.com</a> or call us at (800) 340-3069.</p>
</section>
```

When we need to switch pages (through clicks in the menu), we set the display property to block for the div we want to see and hidden for the rest.

For now, in the menuLinks property, I have set every page to index.html , like this…

```
const menuLinks = {
   "Home": "index.html",
   "About": "index.html",
   "Contact": "index.html"
}
```

…but you could change each to…

```
const menuLinks = {
   "Home": "#",
   "About": "#",
   "Contact": "#"
}
```

Finally, we’ll need to add a global variable to know where we’re at. You can place this anywhere in the shop.js file you like as long as it’s in the main scope. To keep things simple, let’s add it to the top of the file, like this (new code in bold):

```
// ClydeBank Coffee Shop

var currentPage = "main"

// Define hamburger menu object
const hamburgerMenu = {
```

Now, to make the “links” work (rather than just navigating to #, which goes to the current page), you would need to add an event listener when you click on the links. To reduce the possibility of typing errors in a more complex function, here’s the entire shop.js again with this modification (new code modifications in bold):

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// ClydeBank Coffee Shop

var currentPage = "main"

// Define hamburger menu object
const hamburgerMenu = {
   render: (parent, id = "hamburger", mainMenuId = "mainMenu", width = 40, height = 40) => {

      // Construct the SVG
      let svg = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "svg")
      let rect1 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect2 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")
      let rect3 = document.createElementNS("http://www.w3.org/2000/svg", "rect")

      // Set the attributes for the SVG
      svg.id = id
      svg.classList.add("hamburger")
      svg.setAttribute("width", width)
      svg.setAttribute("height", height)
      svg.setAttribute("viewBox", "0 0 100 80")
      svg.setAttribute("fill", "#cad317")

      // Set the attributes for the rectangles
      rect1.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect1.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect2.setAttribute("y", "30")
      rect2.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect2.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect3.setAttribute("y", "60")
      rect3.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect3.setAttribute("height", "20")

      // Add the rectangles to the SVG
      svg.appendChild(rect1)
      svg.appendChild(rect2)
      svg.appendChild(rect3)

      // When the hamburger is clicked, show the menu
      // But first, get the menu
      let menu = document.getElementById(mainMenuId)
   svg.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
         // Show the menu
         menu.style.display = "block"
      })

      parent.appendChild(svg)
   }
}

// Define a menu
const mainMenu = {
   render: (parent, links, id = "mainMenu", hamburgerId = "hamburger",
      minWidth = "300px", maxWidth = "800px",
      backgroundColor = "#F4F1DE", borderColor = "#3B1C0B",
      linkColor = "#3B1C0B") => {

      // Create the div element
      let div = document.createElement("div")

      // Set attributes
      div.id = id
      div.style.minWidth = minWidth
      div.style.maxWidth = maxWidth
      div.style.top = "-1px"
      div.style.left = "-1px"
      div.style.position = "absolute"
      div.style.height = "70%"
      div.style.backgroundColor = backgroundColor
      div.style.border = "1px solid " + borderColor

      // We want the menu to be hidden by default
      div.style.display = "none"

      // Construct the menu
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Add some padding to the ul element and make the text bigger
      ul.style.padding = "1em"
      ul.style.fontSize = "1.25em"

      // Build the menu
      for (let link in links) {

         // Create the needed elements
         let li = document.createElement("li")
         let a = document.createElement("a")

         // Make sure the link is readable
         a.style.color = linkColor

         // Hide the dot
         li.style.listStyle = "none"

         // Create the link
         a.href = links[link]
         a.innerHTML = link

         // If the link is a pound sign, treat differently
         if (links[link] == "#") {
            // When the link is clicked, hide the menu
            a.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
               // Prevent the default action (i.e., following the
               // link)
               event.preventDefault()
               // If "Home" is link title, make newPage "main"
               let newPage
               if (link == "Home") {
                  newPage = "main"
               } else {
                  // Otherwise, set lowercase version of the new
                  // page name
                  newPage = link.toLocaleLowerCase()
               }

               // Hide the current page if not main, since it
               // contains everything else
               if (currentPage != "main") {
                  document.getElementById(currentPage).style.display = "none"
               }

               // Show the new page
               document.getElementById(newPage).style.display = "block"

               // Set the currentPage variable to the new page
               currentPage = newPage
            })
         }

         // Add the list item (li) to the unordered list (ul) with
         // the link
         li.appendChild(a)
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // When the parent of the menu (i.e., the main div)
      // is clicked, hide the menu
      div.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
         // Hide the menu
         div.style.display = "none"
      })

      // Add the list's completed HTML to the div
      div.appendChild(ul)

      // Now add the main div to the parent
      parent.appendChild(div)
   }
}

// The list prototype
const listPrototype = {
   render: (values, parent, id, separator = " - $", className = "") => {

      // Create the unordered list element
      let ul = document.createElement("ul")

      // Assign the HTML element ID
      ul.id = id

      // If className is set, assign it, otherwise ignore
      if (className) {
         ul.className = className
      }

      // Loop through the values array and create a new li element
      // for each
      for (let key in values) {
         // Create a key/value pair in the variables k and v
         let k = key
         let v = values[key]

         // Create the li element
         let li = document.createElement("li")

         // Add the text content to the li element with the separator
         li.textContent = k + separator + v.toFixed(2)

         // Append the li element to the unordered list
         ul.appendChild(li)
      }

      // Append the unordered list to the parent element
      parent.appendChild(ul)
   }
}

// Menu structure
// (for now, these are all links to the home page)

const menuLinks = {
   "Home": "#",
   "About": "#",
   "Contact": "#"
}

// Our inventory
let inventory = {
   "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
   "Espresso": 3.50,
   "Cappuccino": 4.00,
   "Latte": 4.25
}

// Run only when the document is loaded and ready to go
document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function(event) {

   // The main div
   const header = document.getElementsByTagName("header")[0]

   // Obtain reference to the menu list by ID
   let menuList = document.getElementById("coffee-menu-container")

   // Create the menu object from the prototype
   let menu = Object.create(listPrototype)

   // Render the inventory menu
   menu.render(inventory, menuList, "coffee-menu", " - $", "coffee-list")

   // Render the menu (hidden by default)
   mainMenu.render(main, menuLinks)

   // Render the hamburger menu
   hamburgerMenu.render(header)
})
```

Some notes about this new code:

» We add the currentPage variable in the main scope, so we always know what “page” we’re on. Note that in this context, “page” really means which div we’re showing. By default, we set it to main .

» We change the menuLinks JSON to use pound signs ( # ) instead of actual pages, so that our click event handler knows if it needs to stay on the same page and make another div visible, or to navigate to an entirely different page.

» If the link is a pound sign, we add an event listener for the click event to hide the existing page, defined by currentPage , then show the new page, which is the name of the page in lowercase letters.

» We use event.preventDefault() to remove the default action that occurs when you click on a link (i.e., navigating to an entirely different page) and instead perform the actions within the rest of the event handler that we specify.

» Before we’re done with handling the click, we set the currentPage to the newPage , which is just the lower-case name of the page (derived from the JSON we passed to it), or to main when the name of the page is “Home”.

» Rather than use outerHTML, we append each element we construct to its parent element. This preserves the events we add (specifically, the event to click on the menu item) so they don’t get overwritten.

### Where’s the CSS?

There’s a giant pink elephant in the room—the lack of CSS. We could, and arguably should, use CSS to apply style to these elements instead of JavaScript, and there are some advantages in doing so.

However, the reason this code omits CSS is primarily to focus on the language rather than depending on a CSS file to do the heavy lifting of display style. The more lines of JavaScript you write, the more familiarity you’ll have with it. Plus, creating a style entirely in JavaScript, ***parameterized*** (essentially meaning converted into variables) by JSON, gives us the advantage of allowing a remote system, perhaps a backend script running on our web server, to handle styling the page.

***Content delivery networks (CDNs)*** , or globally distributed networks that accelerate web page delivery by caching assets as close to the visitor as possible, generally can’t run backend code. In that case, you must make a call to a remote server to obtain JSON to perform functionality that is beyond the scope of simple HTML. And, given the cache time with CDNs, I’ve often found it simpler to modify some backend code to change functionality rather than edit the HTML.

Your mileage may vary, because the situations you will be coding JavaScript for will be different from mine or anyone else’s. If the advantages of JSON and defining the style in JavaScript don’t appeal to you, or are counterproductive in your case, you’re certainly welcome to use CSS in the Coffee Shop project. We used CSS files in parts I and II, and adding CSS to this project would be extremely simple. If you choose to do this on your own site, please keep in mind that you’ll need to remove the JavaScript code that sets the style, because the **stylesheet is loaded *before* the JavaScript code runs in the** DOMContentLoaded **event.**

For example, consider this code in our menu:

```
// Set attributes
   div.id = id
   div.style.minWidth = minWidth
   div.style.maxWidth = maxWidth
   div.style.top = "-1px"
   div.style.left = "-1px"
   div.style.position = "absolute"
   div.style.height = "80%"
   div.style.backgroundColor = backgroundColor
   div.style.border = "1px solid " + borderColor
```

If we defined top and left , for example, in our CSS, it would look something like this:

```
#mainMenu {
   top: -1px;
   left: -1px;
}
```

If you define these or other values that you also set in the JavaScript, simply remove those lines from the shop.js file so it doesn’t override them once the JavaScript code runs.

Congratulations! Even though you’re still learning, you’ve started work on a simple JavaScript framework that you can use throughout your JavaScript career.

[![img](Images/f0219-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-7) 

Chapter Recap

» You can power your entire website with just one HTML page and JavaScript code.

» You can build new branches on your DOM by using outerHTML and appendChild with code rather than with HTML.

» Methods that accept many arguments can accept a single JSON object, which allows for both simpler syntax and remote control.

## | 12 |

## Advanced JavaScript Techniques

Chapter Overview

» JavaScript has powerful string functionality

» The Math object enables advanced math functions

» Arrow functions let you shorten function definitions

We’ve covered a lot of ground in JavaScript so far, and even started our own basic but usable site generation system in shop.js . But before we develop the rest of the site, we need to round out our knowledge with a few more advanced concepts.

Arguably, some of these concepts are not all that advanced; however, JavaScript and the technology that surrounds it (HTML, CSS, the browser, etc.) create a complex ecosystem. Up until this point, we’ve covered the major topics that you need to know in order to be productive as a web developer. With some more coverage of the finer points of JavaScript, you’ll be able to tackle some of the most difficult challenges.

This chapter will skip around a bit, but our goal will be to cover the rest of the topics necessary to write a fully-fledged single-page application: our coffee shop website. The only topic missing will involve communicating with a backend server, and we’ll cover that in [chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml) .

### Advanced String Methods

We’ve done some basic operations with strings, but for any in-depth JavaScript work, you’ll need to take advantage of more of the string toolkit that JavaScript offers.

#### slice

The slice method enables you to take a portion of a string or an array and return a new string or array with only the selected part.

First, let’s look at how you use it with a string:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-12-01.png) 

```
let greeting = "Hello, World!"
let subject = greeting.slice(7, 12)
console.log(subject)
```

Here, we’re creating a new string, starting at position 7 (considering the first character is in position 0) and ending at (but not including) position 12\. The code above would produce:

```
World
```

Now, let’s extract a slice from an array:

```
let shoppingList = ["milk", "eggs", "apples", "grapes", "hamburger"]
let justFruit = shoppingList.slice(2, 4)
console.log(justFruit)
```

Here, we’re obtaining the items from position 2 (again, starting at 0) until position 4 (but not including position 4). In other words, we’re obtaining items 2 and 3 from the array with a zero-indexed starting position. Our new array is displayed as:

```
[ 'apples', 'grapes' ]
```

#### toLowerCase

We’ve used toUpperCase with this line of code in switch2.js …

```
option.innerHTML = color.charAt(0).toUpperCase() + color.slice(1)
```

…which, as the name suggests, converts the string to uppercase. The toLowerCase method converts a string to all lowercase characters. Like toUpperCase , or pretty much any other method that is or produces a string, you can chain this with other methods, like in the example above.

#### length

There are many times where it’s useful to know the length of a string. Here are a few examples:

```
var theString = "Hello, World!"
console.log(theString.length)
```

To determine the length of an input field, you would use…

```
let firstName = document.getElementById("firstName")
if (firstName.value.length > 0) {
   console.log("You have entered a value for first name")
}
```

When checking an input box, note you’ll have to use elementName.value.length instead of elementName.length because the value of the input box is available from the value property, which is a string.

You’ll notice that rather than a method, with parentheses after it, length is a property. Strings have the length property that is set to the length in characters of the string.

#### split

Splitting a string into an array can come in handy. A common use for this technique is to split a URL into various parts, which can be used to determine what content to show. For example, if the URL is [https://example.com/about/our-team](https://example.com/about/our-team) , I can show a div named “about”, and possibly, the listing of team members of an organization.

To obtain the URL, I would use…

```
const theURL = window.location.href
```

…which obtains the URL from the browser and stores it in theURL .

Then, to split that into parts separated by forward slashes…

```
const urlParts = theURL.split("/")
```

To make this one line of code, you can use…

```
const urlParts = window.location.href.split("/")
```

…because the split method can be used on the string that window.location.href provides.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

Using one line of code to obtain the URL parts by chaining operations together with a dot between them is a great timesaver. However, if you need the intermediate parts of the operation for separate purposes (e.g., you will use theURL and urlParts separately) it’s best not to combine operations.

Assuming the URL is [https://example.com/about/our-team](https://example.com/about/our-team) , if we look at the result…

```
console.log(urlParts)
```

…we’ll see:

```
(5) ['https:', '', 'example.com', 'about', 'our-team']
```

The result is an array of five strings (one of which is empty, because there were two consecutive slashes with nothing in between them). We can then use the includes method on the array to see whether certain values are in the array:

```
if (urlParts.includes("our-team")) {
   // Show div for about and our-team content
}
```

#### replace

When working with strings, it’s fairly common to have a need to replace certain parts of the string with another value. Consider the following string:

```
let comment = "A not nice thing about someone."
```

Imagine this is a comment from a grumpy user. The replace method comes to the rescue!

```
let filteredComment = comment.replace("not nice", "nice")
console.log(filteredComment)
```

This code results in the following:

```
"A nice thing about someone."
```

In this particular case, we could have also replaced “not” with an empty string, which would accomplish mostly the same thing:

```
let comment = "A not nice thing about someone."
let filteredComment = comment.replace("not", "")
console.log(filteredComment)
```

I say “mostly” because there’s an extra space after “A” in this string, which you can replace with this:

```
let filteredComment = comment.replace("not ", "")
```

As with pretty much any other functionality within JavaScript, you can use replace inline:

```
let filteredComment = "A not nice thing about someone.".replace("not nice", "nice")
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The replace method can work with regular expressions. We cover regular expressions in [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) .

#### substring

The substring method does just what the name implies; it lets us grab a subset of a string.

When we run this code…

```
let greeting = "Hello, World!"
console.log(greeting.substring(6, 13))
```

…the result is…

```
World!
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

It is difficult to show in print, but there is a space before the word “World”.

The first argument is the starting position, and the second argument is the end position (which is not included in the result). Both start from zero, as shown in [figure 29](#ch12.xhtml_fig29) .

![img](Images/fig29.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_29.png) 

With substring and other operations on strings, indexing starts at zero.

If you only provide one argument…

```
console.log(greeting.substring(7))
```

…you’ll return the remainder of the string starting from the specified position, which, in this example returns:

```
World!
```

You can provide the return value of the length method to obtain the last x characters of the string, where x is the number returned in this example from greeting.length – 6 .

```
console.log(greeting.substring(greeting.length - 6))
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The substring method does not alter the original string; it only returns the portion you specify as a new string.

#### trim

The trim method removes the ***whitespace*** , or characters (or a group of characters) that separate other characters or words. Whitespace usually consists of a space character. Being able to remove the whitespace from a string has many uses. One of the most practical benefits of being able to trim whitespace is that you can strip spaces that were added accidentally—either before input from the user, or after user input.

```
let greeting = "    Robert Oliver  "
console.log(greeting.trim())
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The trim method does not alter the original string; rather, it returns the trimmed value as a new string.

### Math in JavaScript

Until this point, we’ve only performed basic math operations in JavaScript. We’re not going to get into deep mathematical discussion in this book, but it would be helpful to expand our knowledge of JavaScript’s math capabilities.

#### Order of Operations

JavaScript follows the same order of operations you learned in school. You might remember PEMDAS ( **P** lease **E** xcuse **M** y **D** ear **A** unt **S** ally) or BODMAS order of operations ( [table 4](#ch12.xhtml_tab4) ).

![img](Images/table-4.png) 

![Images](Images/t0227-01.png)

The order of operations in JavaScript.

To see this in action, let’s try the following code:

```
let result = (3 * 2) / 4 - (2 + 7) + 4 ** 3
console.log(result)
```

This results in:

```
56.5
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The exponent operator (**) was added in ECMAScript 2016\. At time of publication, all major browsers have this capability. However, in existing code you may see code use Math.pow(x, y) for exponents (which returns x to the power of y).

#### Referencing Mathematical Constants

Via the Math object, JavaScript has some handy math constants we can use. For example, the code…

```
console.log(Math.PI)
```

…returns…

```
3.141592653589793
```

![img](Images/table-5.png) 

| Code | Description | Value |
| Math.E | Euler’s number, the base of the natural logarithm. | 2.718281828459045 |
| Math.LN10 | The natural logarithm of 10\. | 2.302585092994046 |
| Math.LN2 | The natural logarithm of 2\. | 0.6931471805599453 |
| Math.LOG10E | The base 10 logarithm of e. | 0.4342944819032518 |
| Math.LOG2E | The base 2 logarithm of e. | 1.4426950408889634 |
| Math.PI | The ratio of the circumference of a circle to its diameter. | 3.141592653589793 |
| Math.SQRT1_2 | The square root of ½. | 0.7071067811865476 |
| Math.SQRT2 | The square root of 2\. | 1.4142135623730951 |

A table of mathematical constants in JavaScript, accessible as part of the Math object.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

You might wonder about the value of constants, especially pi, which, for many simple operations, can be rounded to 3.14 or 3.14159 and could be inserted into the top of your code like const pi = 3.14159 or similar. While this works, JavaScript math constants give you consistency and accuracy, so I suggest you use them whenever possible instead of defining your own.

#### Rounding

There are three rounding methods in JavaScript: Math.round() , Math.floor() , and Math.ceil() .

You would use Math.round() to round a number to the nearest integer, rounding down for values below 0.5 and rounding up for values greater than or equal to 0.5\.

```
// roundedNumber will be set to 1
let roundedNumber = Math.round(1.2)
```

If you want to round down to the nearest integer, use Math.floor() .

```
// roundedNumber will be set to 1
let roundedNumber = Math.floor(1.6)
```

To round up, use Math.ceil() .

```
// roundedNumber will be set to 2
let roundedNumber = Math.ceil(1.3)
```

#### Generating Random Numbers

Random numbers are important in programming. I have often used them when developing games, but they can be helpful in security and many other applications. Fortunately, JavaScript makes it easy to generate random numbers, with the random method on the Math object.

By default, the random number generated will be between 0 and 1\. Let’s try it in the console:

```
console.log(Math.random())
```

You’ll get a number that looks something like this:

```
0.06813061476164983
```

But we usually don’t need a number between 0 and 1\. Often we’ll need a number between 1 and 10, or between 1 and 100\. To generate a number between 1 and 100, we can use a technique like this:

```
let randomNumber = Math.floor(Math.random() * 100) + 1
console.log(randomNumber)
```

When we run this code, randomNumber will be populated with a value between 1 and 100\. But how does this work?

We must consider the innermost parentheses first when evaluating the chain of operations. So, in that case, Math.random() generates the random number between 0 and 1\. Then, this number is multiplied by 100\. The Math.floor() method, which encapsulates it, rounds the number down to the nearest integer, but this would give us a number between 0 and 99, which is not what we wanted. Adding 1 ensures that randomNumber will be a number between 1 and 100\.

#### Advanced Math Operations

JavaScript provides a great deal of advanced math capability. There are many methods available, including trigonometric functionality.

![img](Images/table-6.png) 

| Method | Description | Example |
| Math.abs | Return the absolute value (i.e., make the number positive) | Math.abs(4.1) |
| Math.cos | Return the cosine of an angle | Math.cos(1) |
| Math.log | Return the natural logarithm (base e) | Math.log(5) |
| Math.sin | Return the sine of an angle | Math.sin(1) |
| Math.tan | Return the tangent of an angle | Math.tan(1) |

A chart of some of the most common math methods.

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

To see a full list, access “Math Object Functionality” in your free Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript

### Dealing with Time

Calendar dates and time in JavaScript are handled through the Date object. Let’s look at a few common use cases.

To obtain the current date and time, simply create a new Date object:

```
const now = new Date()
console.log(now)
```

This outputs text similar to this:

```
Thu Apr 06 2023 20:38:26 GMT-0500 (Central Daylight Time)
```

That’s quite verbose, so you can break this down into parts you might need:

```
const now = new Date()
const month = now.getMonth() + 1
const day = now.getDate()
const year = now.getFullYear()
const hours = now.getHours()
const minutes = now.getMinutes()
const seconds = now.getSeconds()
```

In JavaScript, the numbering of the 12 calendar months begins at 0\. This means that January is 0 instead of 1\. We therefore need to add 1 to each month to get the numbering we are used to.

Now that we have the individual portions, we can construct a string to our liking:

```
console.log(`${month}-${day}-${year} ${hours}:${minutes}:${seconds}`)
```

You can obtain a more readable date string with the toDateString method:

```
const now = new Date()
console.log(now.toDateString())
```

This code displays:

```
Thu Apr 06 2023
```

There is also a method called toTimeString that simplifies the time display:

```
const now = new Date()
console.log(now.toTimeString())
```

This code displays:

```
21:14:22 GMT-0500 (Central Daylight Time)
```

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

There’s a lot of date/time functionality in JavaScript and we only have the space here to cover the essentials. To see a full list, access “Date Object Methods” in your free Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript

### Handling Errors

No code is error-free. Even excellently written code often has a dependency upon an external source or condition that is not entirely predictable. Being able to handle the unexpected is important if you want your program to run well. Luckily, JavaScript makes robust error handling available to us.

JavaScript uses the try , catch , and finally keywords to control error handling. Let’s take a quick look at how they work.

```
try {
   // This code might cause an error
} catch (error) {
   // This code handles the error
   // The error object contains details about the error
} finally {
   // This code always runs, regardless of the error
}
```

When this code runs, if an error occurs in any part of the try block, execution is immediately sent to the catch block. Inside the scope of the catch block, the error object is populated with details about the error that occurred. The finally block is optional, and if it isn’t included, execution continues past the try block as normal. However, if the finally block exists, its code will be run whether or not an error occurs.

You can also preemptively trigger an error via the throw keyword. This is most helpful inside functions when arguments specified would otherwise cause an error, or lead to an undesired state:

```
function greeting(firstName) {
   if (firstName.length === 0) {
      // The string firstName is empty
      throw new Error("The firstName argument is empty.")
   }
   console.log("Hello, " + name + "!")
}
```

I’m using three equals signs === on the length comparison to 0 to be sure that the value is 0 and that the value returned is a number (i.e., the same type).

If you were to call greeting with an empty string inside a try block, like this…

```
try {
   greeting("")
} catch (error) {
   // This code would run because an error was thrown
   console.log('An error occurred: ${error.message}')
}
```

…you would get this error message in the console:

```
An error occurred: The firstName argument is empty.
```

If we don’t wrap this in a try block and instead run it on the console, we get an uncaught error, as shown in [figure 30](#ch12.xhtml_fig30) .

![img](Images/fig30.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_30.png) 

An uncaught error. The error.message is still shown.

The error object contains some details about the error, including:

» error.name – The type of error (e.g., TypeError, SyntaxError, ReferenceError, RangeError, or simply Error).

» error.message – A general description of the error, often presented in a more friendly manner.

It’s a good idea to try to catch your errors, no pun intended. If something doesn’t go as expected, a backup plan through catch or finally will help ensure your user has a good experience.

### Using the Arrow Function

Arrow functions were introduced to JavaScript in ES6\. They act as a shortcut when creating functions and are called arrows because of their syntax (an equals sign followed by a greater-than sign, or => ).

Traditionally, you define functions like this in JavaScript:

```
function greeting() {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

But you can define functions just like you define them in an object, like this:

```
greeting = function() {
   console.log("Hello, World!")
}
```

Arrow functions give us the ability to skip the word function :

```
greeting = () => {
   console.log("Hello, World")
}
```

And if the function has one line and that line is a return statement, like this…

```
greeting = function() {
   return "Hello, World!"
}
```

…you can shorten it to this…

```
greeting = () => "Hello, World!"
```

### Using Cookies

A ***cookie*** is a small fragment of data from a website that is stored within a web browser and that the website can retrieve later. JavaScript and backend languages like Python, PHP, Ruby, etc., can take advantage of cookies.

JavaScript has variables, but these variables are lost when the page reloads. Cookies store variables permanently, until they expire, until the website code removes them, or until they are cleared by the user.

Cookies have a very unfortunate reputation, but most of the evils we associate with them are features of third-party cookies. A third-party cookie is a cookie set by a website that is not the primary website the user is visiting. Third-party cookies can be used for tracking and other nefarious purposes. But cookies set by the primary website can be very useful. For example, these cookies store user preferences. In the case of our ClydeBank Coffee Shop site, cookies come in handy to store information about which products the user would like to purchase.

One note about cookies though before we proceed—they don’t work inside your browser when you are opening HTML files directly from your computer using file URL, (like file:// ). Cookies are only functional when the HTML files are served by a web server via HTTP or HTTPS protocols.

#### Creating a Cookie

Using cookies with JavaScript is simple, but not exactly straightforward. I say this because, while setting cookies is easy, managing them is a bit complicated without some helper functions. First, let’s examine how to set a cookie:

```
document.cookie = "firstName=Robert"
```

This creates a cookie named firstName with the value of Robert .

#### Reading a Cookie

Reading a cookie is…a bit of a mess. When you read the value of document.cookie , you get all of the site’s cookies in a string, separated by semicolons. That’s not very useful, especially if you have multiple cookies. Instead, a function like this makes things a lot simpler. The code is commented to describe what each part does:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-12-02.png) 

```
function getCookie(name) {
   // Add the equal sign to the name (name=)
   let cookieName = name + "="

   // Decode cookie string to handle cookies with special characters
   let decodedCookie = decodeURIComponent(document.cookie)

   // Check if there are any cookies to process
   if (!decodedCookie) {
      return null
   }

   // Split document.cookie on semicolons into an array
   let cookieArray = decodedCookie.split(";")

   // Iterate through the name=value pairs in the cookieArray
   for (let i = 0; i < cookieArray.length; i++) {
      let cookie = cookieArray[i]

      // Remove any leading spaces using a regular expression
      cookie = cookie.replace(/^\s+/, "")

      // If cookie is found, return its value
      if (cookie.indexOf(cookieName) === 0) {
         return cookie.substring(cookieName.length, cookie.length)
      }
   }

   // If cookie name was not found, return null
   return null
}
```

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

This code contains a regular expression. We’ll cover those in [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) .

With this function, you can now easily retrieve a cookie:

```
let firstName = getCookie("firstName")
```

There are two things we haven’t covered in this function: indexOf and the decodeURIComponent .

First, indexOf returns the position of the first occurrence of the value specified in the string. In this case, we’re checking to see the position of cookieName in the cookie string. If it’s 0, that is, at the beginning, then it evaluates to true and the return line is run (returning the portion of the cookie starting at the end of the cookieName length). This, in essence, returns its value.

The decodeURIComponent function converts any special characters into a readable form. This is most often used when handling data that’s been sent via the URL, or, in this case, via a cookie.

If your cookie string is…

```
"myName=Robert%20Oliver"
```

…then running decodeURIComponent(“myName=Robert%20Oliver”) will yield the result:

```
myName=Robert Oliver
```

#### Updating a Cookie

To set a different value for the cookie, simply overwrite it, like this:

```
document.cookie = "firstName=Marsha"
```

It should be noted that document.cookie doesn’t quite function like a normal string. Instead, adding a key/value pair like in the example above appends the cookie and its value to the list of cookies.

#### Expired Cookies

You can set an expiration date on a cookie so that it will automatically be removed at a certain time. The following function is helpful to generate the string containing the time necessary for this:

```
function createCookieExpiration(hours) {
   // Create a new Date object
   let expirationDate = new Date()
   // Add the correct number of milliseconds to the existing time
      expirationDate.setTime(expirationDate.getTime() + (hours * 60 * 60 * 1000))
   // Return the time in a format expected by the browser
   return expirationDate.toUTCString()
}
```

Now we can set the expiration time. In this example, the cookie expires in one week. I specified 168 because 7 days times 24 hours is 168 hours.

```
expTime = createCookieExpiration(168)
document.cookie = "firstName=Robert; expires=" + expTime
```

#### Removing a Cookie

To remove a cookie, you can simply set the cookie’s expiration date to a date in the past. Frequently, this is done with the help of a function. Here’s one I use:

```
function removeCookie(cookieName) {
   document.cookie = cookieName + "=; expires=Thu, 01 Jan 1970 00:00:00 UTC;"
}
```

Unless your user invents a time machine to travel back to the 1960s, this will always work.

![img](Images/FunFact_icon.png) 

Thursday, January 1st, 1970, at midnight is used because this is the UNIX epoch time. This time is used by UNIX, the ancestor of many modern systems, as the start time for time-based calculations, because it was developed in the late 1960s and early 1970s, and this time guarantees a positive number for the current time.

#### Setting Cookie Scope

You can set cookie scope by setting a particular path that a cookie applies to. You do this with a single forward slash (i.e., / ), so when you use…

```
document.cookie = "firstName=Robert"
```

…you are actually saying…

```
document.cookie = "firstName=Robert; path=/"
```

However, you can change it to apply to only a certain area of the site. Let’s say we have a store in the /store subfolder of a website. To have a cookie only apply to that store, you can use:

```
document.cookie = "total=24.95; path=/store"
```

While on that page, you can access this cookie, but otherwise this cookie isn’t visible to JavaScript.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop Frontend

Now that we’ve covered the last remaining frontend topics we’ll cover in this book (other than jQuery and React), we can now enhance our shop.js with enough code to make an extremely simple but usable e-commerce single-page application.

The only thing missing from this code will be the ability to talk to a backend server. We’ll cover how to do that separately in [chapter 16](#ch16.xhtml) .

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

The single-page app functionality (i.e., showing hidden divs with page content, as covered in [chapter 11](#ch11.xhtml) ) is not used in this example because the focus is on handling the shopping cart. Nevertheless, this can be added by changing the menu links to point to div names, adding content to those divs, then enhancing the mainMenuPrototype with code from [chapter 11](#ch11.xhtml) to handle this.

First, the CSS file. We’re only adding one class, so I’ll list that first:

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

To save paper, I have omitted the entire CSS file. Instead, add this to the end of the file. However, the full file is available in the JavaScript Git repository: github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide.

style.css

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-style_css.png) 

```
/* Cart styles */
.add-to-cart-button {
   margin-left: 1em;
}
```

Next, here’s the index.html file. It’s unchanged from the previous time we used it but I’m including it here for easy reference:

index.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-index_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
   <meta charset="UTF-8">
   <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
   <!-- Load Google's Open Sans font -->
   <link href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css?family=Open+Sans:400,700" rel="stylesheet">
   <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
   <title>ClydeBank Coffee Shop</title>
</head>
<body>
<div id="main">
   <header>
      <div class="logo-container">
```

Finally, here’s the full shop.js with all its ecommerce goodness!

shop.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-shop_js.png) 

```
// ClydeBank Coffee Shop
// Copyright (C) 2024 ClydeBank Media, All Rights Reserved.
// Written by Robert W. Oliver II, Author of JavaScript QuickStart Guide

// The locale and currency to use for formatting
// Alter these to suit your needs
const locale = "en-US"
const currency = "USD"

// Menu structure
const menuLinks = {
   "Home": "index.html",
   "About": "index.html",
   "Contact": "index.html"
}

// Our inventory
const inventory = {
   "Regular Coffee": 3.00,
   "Espresso": 3.50,
   "Cappuccino": 4.00,
   "Latte": 4.25
}

// Random number to add to the end of IDs that aren't
// specified to prevent collisions. Only one per page load is needed
const randomIdPostfix = Math.floor(Math.random() * 100000)

// This object is used here to format currency
// (see addToCart() and listPrototype for use)
const intlFormat = new Intl.NumberFormat(locale, {
   style: "currency",
   currency: currency,
})

// Retrieve a cookie by name
function getCookie(name) {
   // Add the equal sign to the name (name=)
   let cookieName = name + "="

   // Decode cookie string to handle cookies with special characters
   let decodedCookie = decodeURIComponent(document.cookie)

   // Check if there are any cookies to process
   if (!decodedCookie) {
      return null
   }

   // Split document.cookie on semicolons into an array
   let cookieArray = decodedCookie.split(";")

   // Iterate through the name=value pairs in the cookieArray
   for (let i = 0; i < cookieArray.length; i++) {
      let cookie = cookieArray[i]   

      // Remove any leading spaces using a regular expression
      cookie = cookie.replace(/^\s+/, "")

      // If cookie is found, return its value
      if (cookie.indexOf(cookieName) === 0) {
         return cookie.substring(cookieName.length, cookie.length)
      }
   }
   // If cookie name was not found, return null
   return null
}

function replaceNonAlphanumericWithDashes(input) {
   // Regular expression to match spaces and non-alphanumeric
   // characters
   let regex = /[^a-zA-Z0-9]+/g
   // Replace matches with dashes
   let output = input.replace(regex, "-")
   return output
}

// Obtain the cart and return it as JSON
function getCart() {
   // Retrieve the "cart" cookie
   let cartCookie = getCookie("cart")

   // If it exists, parse contents and return the result
   // Otherwise return an empty object
   if (cartCookie) {
      return JSON.parse(cartCookie)
   } else {
      return {}
   }
}

function saveCart(cart) {
   // Save the updated cart back to the cookie
   document.cookie = "cart=" + encodeURIComponent(JSON.stringify(cart))
}

// Add an item to the cart
function addToCart(itemName, itemPrice, itemQnty) {
   // Load the existing cart from the cookie (if it exists)
   // If it doesn't exist, cart will be empty
   let cart = {}
   if (document.cookie) {
      cart = JSON.parse(document.cookie.replace("cart=", ""))
      // This strips "cart=stringifiedJSON" into "stringifiedJSON",
      // then parses that and assigns it to the cart object
   }

   // Create an object with the name, price, and quantity to add
   const item = {
      name: itemName,
      price: itemPrice,
      quantity: itemQnty
   }

   // If a cart item with the same name already exists,
   // increment quantity. If not, add item to cart
   if (cart[itemName]) {
      cart[itemName].quantity += itemQnty
   } else {
      cart[itemName] = item
   }

   // Log to console for diagnostic purposes
   console.log("Added " + JSON.stringify(item) + " to cart!")

   // Save the cart back to the cookie
   saveCart(cart)
}

// Remove item from cart
function removeFromCart(itemName) {
   // Retrieve the "cart" cookie
   let cartCookie = getCookie("cart")

   // If it exists, parse contents
   if (cartCookie) {
      let cart = JSON.parse(cartCookie)

      // If the item exists in the cart, remove it
      if (cart[itemName]) {
         delete cart[itemName]

         // Save the update cart back to the cookie
         saveCart(cart)
      }
   }
}

// Define a button prototype
const buttonPrototype = {
   // HTML element id (defaults to hamburger)
   // Plus random postfix to prevent collisions
   id: "button" + randomIdPostfix,
   // The text to be displayed on the button
   text: "Click Me",
   // The color of the button
   color: "#CAD317",
   // The border style of the button
   borderStyle: "1px solid #3B1C0B",
   // The click event handler
   clickEvent: false,
   // The CSS class name (defaults to "")
   className: "",
   // The render method (with the parent element as an argument)
   render: function(parent) {
   // Set the variables from properties of this object with the same
   // name
   let {id, text, color, borderStyle, clickEvent, className} = this

   // Construct the button
   let button = document.createElement("button")

      // Set the attributes
      button.id = id
      button.textContent = text
      button.style.border = borderStyle

      // Assign the clickEvent argument (passed as function) to the
      // onclick event
      button.onclick = clickEvent

      // If className is set, assign it, otherwise ignore
      if (className) {
         button.className = className
      }

      // Set the background color
      button.style.backgroundColor = color

      // Add the button to the parent element
      parent.appendChild(button)
   }
}

// Define hamburger menu prototype
const hamburgerMenuPrototype = {
   // HTML element id (defaults to hamburger)
   // Plus random postfix to prevent collisions
   id: "hamburger" + randomIdPostfix,
   // The ID of the mainMenu div (defaults to mainMenu)
   mainMenuId: "mainMenu",
   // The width and height of the hamburgerMenu (by default, 40)
   width: 40,
   height: 40,
   // The color of the hamburgerMenu
   color: "#CAD317",
   // The render method (with the parent element as an argument)
   render: function(parent) {
      // Set the variables from properties of this object with the
      // same name
      let {id, mainMenuId, width, height, color} = this

      // Construct the SVG
      const svgUrl = "http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"
      let svg = document.createElementNS(svgUrl, "svg")
      let rect1 = document.createElementNS(svgUrl, "rect")
      let rect2 = document.createElementNS(svgUrl, "rect")
      let rect3 = document.createElementNS(svgUrl, "rect")

      // Set the attributes for the SVG
      svg.id = id
      svg.classList.add("hamburger")
      svg.setAttribute("width", width)
      svg.setAttribute("height", height)
      svg.setAttribute("viewBox", "0 0 100 80")
      svg.setAttribute("fill", color)

      // Set the attributes for the rectangles
      rect1.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect1.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect2.setAttribute("y", "30")
      rect2.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect2.setAttribute("height", "20")

      rect3.setAttribute("y", "60")
      rect3.setAttribute("width", "100")
      rect3.setAttribute("height", "20")

      // Add the rectangles to the SVG
      svg.appendChild(rect1)
      svg.appendChild(rect2)
      svg.appendChild(rect3)

      // When the hamburger is clicked, show the menu
      // But first, get the menu
      let menu = document.getElementById(mainMenuId)
      svg.addEventListener("click", (event) => {
         // Show the menu
         menu.style.display = "block"
      })

      parent.appendChild(svg)
   }
}
```
