```
   // Set the mimetype to application/json, so the browser
   // knows how to handle the response
   res.setHeader("Content-Type", "application/json")

   // Set CORS headers to allow any page to send requests
   // This is only for development, don't do this in production!
   res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Origin", "*")
   res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Methods", "GET, POST, PUT, DELETE")
   res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Headers", "Content-Type")

   // Send the JSON object as a string
   res.end(JSON.stringify(exampleJSON))
})

server.listen(port, hostname, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${hostname}:${port}/`)
})
```

hello-json1.html

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-hello-json1_html.png) 

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>Hello!</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <p id="message">Hello </p>
      </div>
      <script>
         const url = "http://localhost:3000"
         fetch(url).then(response => response.json())
         .then(data => {
            const message = document.getElementById("message")
            message.innerHTML += data.firstName + "!"
         }
         )
      </script>
   </body>
</html>
```

If you load the hello-json1.html file in your browser, you’ll simply see Hello . It works, but that’s not quite what we want. We have to start the node-json1.js script using *Start Debugging* from the Run menu. Once you do this and refresh, you should see:

```
Hello Robert
```

There are several things to note about both pieces of code. First, in node-json1.js , we construct an exampleJSON object that contains a firstName property of “Robert” (or whatever name you assigned). Then, the status code of 200 is set to signify to the web browser that the request was successful. This is one of the standard response codes in the HTTP protocol.

![img](Images/FunFact_icon.png) 

The HTTP response code 404, the code for “file not found”, is considered a failed response. In fact, any status code in the 400–599 range is a failure in some way, whereas status codes in the 200–299 range are considered successful. Status codes 300–399 are successful but feature a redirect of some kind to another URL.

Next, CORS headers are set to allow any page to make a request. The * argument means any domain, and this is dangerous in production. Ideally, AJAX requests should be made on the same domain, or perhaps a subdomain, like api.example.com (replacing example.com with your actual website domain). In this case, you would explicitly set the value “Access-Control-Allow-Origin” to api.example.com to allow just that domain.

Finally, we convert the JSON object into a string and respond with it.

The rest of the work is done by the JavaScript on the HTML page. The URL is set to [http://localhost:3000](http://localhost:3000) , and then fetch makes the request to that URL. We obtain the reference to the paragraph element, then reference the JSON received in the data object—specifically the firstName property—and add that to the innerHTML of the paragraph element.

You might wonder why we went through all this work to retrieve a string (the value of the firstName property) when we could have just put this value in the HTML directly. This example code may be simple, and it’s true that there’s not much interesting data being passed between the Node.js server and the HTML file via the AJAX request. However, this illustrates the concept of passing data between an HTML page with JavaScript and a backend Node.js server.

If you needed to access data on a server in a way that shouldn’t be accessible to the general public, or perform some service like send an email, access a database, process a credit card, or call some API with credentials you can’t share with the public, an ideal setup is to have AJAX connect to a backend Node.js server to carry out the action for you.

### Accessing GET and POST Variables

You can send variables to your Node.js code via GET and POST. For example, if the URL is…

```
http://127.0.0.1:3000/?firstName=Robert&lastName=Oliver
```

…the firstName and lastName GET variables will be set to Robert and Oliver , respectively.

Here’s an example that will allow you to read those GET variables and make use of them within Node.js:

node-get.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-node-get_js.png) 

```
const http = require("http")
const url = require("url")

const hostname = "127.0.0.1"
const port = 3000

const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
   // Parse the URL and obtain the GET vars, also
   // known as the query string
   const parsedUrl = url.parse(req.url, true)
   const getVars = parsedUrl.query

   res.statusCode = 200
   res.setHeader("Content-Type", "application/json")

   // Display the getVars in the browser
   res.end(JSON.stringify(getVars))
})

// Start the server
server.listen(port, hostname, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${hostname}:${port}/`)
})
```

When you run this code via Visual Studio Code (select *Start Debugging* from the *Run* menu), you’ll launch Node.js and it will listen to port 3000 on the local machine. Then, open your browser and go to this URL (changing it to your name, if you wish):

```
http://127.0.0.1:3000/?firstName=Robert&lastName=Oliver
```

You should see…

```
{"firstName":"Robert","lastName":"Oliver"}
```

…in the browser as the response, because the getVars variable was converted to a string via JSON.stringify .

The magic happens with the url.parse method, which extracts the GET variables from the URL.

If you add…

```
console.log(parsedUrl)
```

…before the res.end line, then stop (red square) then start ( *Start Debugging* from the *Run* menu), you can see the contents of the parsedUrl object in the terminal within Visual Studio Code (the same pane that shows what port and address Node.js is listening to). Once you click the greater-than sign on the left-hand side before Url to expand the line, it should look like [figure 34](#ch16.xhtml_fig34) .

![img](Images/fig34.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_34.png) 

Properties in the parsedUrl object, displayed in the Visual Studio Code console.

POST variables work a bit differently. They aren’t part of the URL string, so accessing them involves reading data from the request in chunks and adding those chunks to a string for each chunk received. Let’s look at the example code:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-16-05.png) 

```
const http = require("http")
const hostname = "127.0.0.1"
const port = 3000

const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
   if (req.method === "POST") {
      let body = ""
      // Collect data chunks, adding each to the body string
      req.on("data", chunk => {
         body += chunk.toString()
      })

      // When end event fires, log the string and end the request
      req.on("end", () => {
         console.log(body)
res.end("OK")
      })
   }
})

// Start the server
server.listen(port, hostname, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${hostname}:${port}/`)
})
```

It was easy to send GET variables on the URL for testing, but sending POST variables is a bit more difficult. You can use jQuery’s $.post method to send POST data, similar to the example found under AJAX with jQuery in this chapter.

### Responding to Different URLs

So far, we’ve just responded to the home page with Node.js, but you can respond to any URL path you wish. Consider this example:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-16-06.png) 

```
const http = require("http")
const url = require("url")

const hostname = "127.0.0.1"
const port = 3000

const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
   const parsedUrl = url.parse(req.url, true)

   switch(parsedUrl.pathname) {
      case "/":
         // Display the Home page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("Home page contents.")
         break
      case "/about":
         // Display the About Us page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("About Us page contents.")
         break
      case "/contact":
         // Display the Contact page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("Contact Us page contents.")
         break
      default:
         // Display a 404 page (Page Not Found)
         res.writeHead(404, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("404 - Page Not Found.")
   }
})

// Start the server
server.listen(port, hostname, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${hostname}:${port}/`)
})
```

Here, we’re using a switch to execute different branches of code, depending on the URL. In this example, we’re just adding the header and displaying a simple string to the visitor, but we can send any HTML code we wish. Alternatively, if we want to respond with JSON, we can change text/html to application/json . Note that we’re using the writeHead method here to both return a response code and mimetype, all in one.

### Interacting with a Database

Interacting with a database is a common task for a web server. Since JavaScript code in the browser cannot practically (or securely, for that matter) connect to the database itself, Node.js code can talk to the database and then deliver the results of data queries in JSON format to JavaScript in the browser via AJAX.

For this exercise, we’ll use a sample SQLite database. We don’t have the space to cover SQLite or SQL servers in general in this book, but the sample database and the SQLite package for Node.js will give us an overview of how the process of retrieving data via SQL and outputting JSON works.

The general order of operations in this process will be:

» Display the web page to the visitor

» Make an AJAX request to a web server for data

» The web server running Node.js connects to SQLite and asks for the data

» The SQLite database retrieves it and returns it to Node.js

» Node.js converts it to JSON and returns the data to the web browser

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

For an excellent introduction to SQLite, check out the best-selling *SQL QuickStart Guide* by Walter Shields.

Before we can get started, we’ll need to install SQLite. Fortunately, it’s very easy. Here are the instructions for each operating system we’re covering:

#### Windows

Navigate to [https://sqlite.org/](https://sqlite.org/) and click Download, then pick the sqlite-tools ZIP file for Windows and save it. You can extract the sqlite3.exe program and store it wherever you like (even on your desktop, if you wish), but note the path on your disk where you save it. For example, if you want to run it from your desktop, the full path to the program would be C:\Users\You\Desktop\sqlite3.exe (where You would be your Windows username).

The sqlite3.exe tool will allow you to open, modify, and even create new SQLite databases, if you wish. You don’t technically need it for this exercise, but being able to use SQLite databases can be helpful in your programming career, so I recommend having it on hand.

Now, you’ll need to install the npm package called sqlite3\. To do this, run a terminal inside Visual Studio Code:

```
npm install sqlite3
```

#### macOS

You’ll be pleased to know that macOS already includes SQLite. You can run the SQLite tool on the console via the sqlite3 command.

To install the npm package sqlite3, which JavaScript will use to interface with the database, open a terminal and run:

```
npm install sqlite3
```

#### Linux

Most Linux distributions will have SQLite pre-installed. You can test for this by opening a terminal and running the following command:

```
sqlite3
```

If it gives an error, you don’t have it installed, so run:

#### Debian / Ubuntu / Linux Mint

```
apt install sqlite3
```

#### Red Hat / Fedora

```
yum install sqlite
```

#### Arch Linux / Manjaro

```
pacman -S sqlite
```

After this, run…

```
npm install sqlite3
```

…to install the sqlite3 Node.js package to use in JavaScript.

Now that we have the sqlite3 package installed, we can use it in our program. But first, let’s download a sample database to use in our project. For that, we’ll use the sTunes.db database from *SQL QuickStart Guide* . You can find a copy of this database in the Digital Assets for this book.

![img](Images/DigitalAssets_icon.png) 

To access “sTunes.db Database”, please access your free Digital Assets at go.quickstartguides.com/javascript

We’ll need two files: the HTML file for the browser and the Node.js code to query the database and send the results via AJAX.

![img](Images/Important_icon.png) 

Technically speaking, you can place the sTunes.db file wherever you like, but to be compatible with the example code in this chapter, I recommend placing the file directly in your Source folder along with the rest of your code files from this book. If you choose to place it somewhere else, you’ll need to adjust the dbFile variable with the full path.

stunes.html

```
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
   <head>
      <meta charset="UTF-8">
      <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
      <meta http-equiv="X-UA-Compatible" content="ie=edge">
      <title>sTunes Tracks</title>
   </head>
   <body>
      <div id="content">
         <table id="tracks">
            <th>
               <td>ID</td>
               <td>Name</td>
               <td>Composer</td>
               <td>Price</td>
            </th>
         </table>
      </div>
      <script>
         const url = "http://localhost:3000"
         fetch(url).then(response => response.json())
         .then(data => {
            const tracksTable = document.getElementById("tracks")
            // Iterate over the results
            data.forEach(row => {
               console.log(row)
               // Create the needed DOM elements
               let resultRow = document.createElement("tr")
               let cellId = document.createElement("td")
               let cellName = document.createElement("td")
               let cellPrice = document.createElement("td")

               // Put the data in the columns
               cellId.innerText = row.TrackId
               cellName.innerText = row.Name
               cellPrice.innerText = row.UnitPrice

               // Append columns to the row
               resultRow.appendChild(cellId)
               resultRow.appendChild(cellName)
               resultRow.appendChild(cellPrice)

               // Append row to the table
               tracksTable.appendChild(resultRow)
            })
         })
      </script>
   </body>
</html>
```

node-stunes.js

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-node-stunes_js.png) 

```
const http = require("http")
const sqlite3 = require("sqlite3")

const address = "127.0.0.1"
const port = 3000

// SQL query to use
const sqlQuery = "SELECT * FROM tracks;"

const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
   res.statusCode = 200
   res.setHeader("Content-Type", "application/json")

// Set CORS headers to allow any page to send requests
// This is only for development, don't do this in production!
res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Origin", "*")
res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Methods", "GET, POST, PUT, DELETE")
res.setHeader("Access-Control-Allow-Headers", "Content-Type")

   // Connect to the sTunes database
   let db = new sqlite3.Database("./sTunes.db", sqlite3.OPEN_READONLY, (err) => {
      if (err) {
         console.error('Database connection error: ${err.message}')
      }
      console.log("Connection established.")
   })

   // Create an array to accumulate rows of results
   let results = []

   // Query the database
   db.serialize(() => {

      // Iterate through each row in the table
      db.each(sqlQuery, (err, row) => {
         if (err) {
            // If an error occurs, log to console
            console.error(err.message)
            } else {
            // Add the row (track) to the array
               results.push(row)   
            }
         }, (err, count) => {
         // This code is executed when all rows have been retrieved
         // We don't use it, but the count variable has the total
         // number of rows retrieved from the result.
         if (err) {
            // If an error occurred, log it in the console
            console.error(err.message)
         } else {
            // Send results to the web browser
            res.end(JSON.stringify(results))
         }
      })
   })
})

server.listen(port, address, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${address}:${port}/`)
})
```

In this code, we connect to the database using the sqlite3.OPEN_READONLY flag, meaning the database cannot be written to in this mode—only read. This is best practice unless you specifically need to modify the database. We then run the SQL query…

```
SELECT * FROM tracks;
```

…which will select all the columns from the tracks table. We then create an empty results array and iterate through the results, pushing (adding) the row to the end of the array. We do this as many times as there are results, then the array named results is converted into a string and sent to the web browser making the AJAX request.

The HTML file is relatively simple. It creates a table and a table header with ID, Name, Composer, and Price. There are other fields, but they reference other tables, so we don’t really need them for our example. The JavaScript in the <script> tags adds DOM elements for the table rows and cells filled with the results from the AJAX request, then appends each row to the table as we iterate over the results.

### ClydeBank Coffee Shop – Backend Shopping Cart

This coffee shop section will be a bit different in that we’re not going to necessarily introduce new code. Instead, I want to demonstrate the concept of handling the shopping cart and checkout in the backend with Node.js.

The reason this code won’t be specific is that shopping carts, and especially ecommerce systems, vary considerably. Everyone has their own implementation details, and they all provide sample code—almost certainly in JavaScript—to handle the checkout page from start to finish.

Since checkout procedures vary, let’s focus on adding products to the cart using Node.js rather than storing the entire cart in the cookie (for reasons we discussed in [chapter 12](#ch12.xhtml) ).

The frontend code might look something like this for addToCart :

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-16-07.png) 

```
// Add item to cart
function addToCart(itemName, itemPrice, itemQnty) {
   // Create an object named item with details of product
   const item = {
      name: itemName,
      price: itemPrice,
      quantity: itemQnty
   }

   // Make POST request to the server
   fetch("/cart/add", {
      method: "POST",
      headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
      body: JSON.stringify(item)
   })
   .then(response => response.json())
   .then(data => console.log(data))
   .catch((error) => {
      console.error(`An error occurred: ${error}`)
   })
}
```

To obtain the cart on the frontend, you would use code similar to this for getCart :

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-16-08.png) 

```
function getCart() {
   // Make the HTTP request to the server via fetch
   fetch("/cart", { method: "GET" })
      .then(response => response.json())
      .then(data => console.log(data))
      .catch((error) => {
         console.error("An error occurred: ", error)
      })
}
```

The data you obtain in both cases is logged to the console, but you can then store this data—perhaps in SQLite or another database—and recall it later if needed.

On the Node.js (backend) side, you could use code like this to handle getting the cart data and adding to the cart:

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-16-09.png) 

```
const http = require("http")
const url = require("url")

const hostname = "127.0.0.1"
const port = 3000

// Initialize the cart
let cart = {}

const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
   const parsedUrl = url.parse(req.url, true)

   switch(parsedUrl.pathname) {
      case "/":
         // Display the Home page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("Home page contents.")
         break
      case "/about":
         // Display the About Us page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("About Us page contents.")
         break
      case "/contact":
         // Display the Contact page
         res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("Contact Us page contents.")
         break
      case "/cart":
         // GET will return the cart contents
         if (req.method === "GET") {
            res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "application/json"})
            res.end(JSON.stringify(cart))
         // POST will add an item to the cart
         } else if (req.method === "POST") {
            let body = ""
            req.on("data", chunk => {
               body += chunk.toString()
            })
            req.on("end", () => {
               const item = JSON.parse(body)

               // If a cart item with the same name already exists,
               // increment quantity. If not, add item to cart
               if (cart[item.name]) {
                  cart[item.name].quantity += item.quantity
               } else {
                  cart[item.name] = item
               }

               res.writeHead(200, {"Content-Type": "application/json"})
               res.end(JSON.stringify({success: true}))
            })
         } else {
            res.writeHead(405, {"Content-Type": "text/plain"})
            res.end("Method not allowed")
         }
         break
         default:
         // Display a 404 page (Page Not Found)
         res.writeHead(404, {"Content-Type": "text/html"})
         res.end("404 - Page Not Found.")
      }
})

// Start the server
server.listen(port, hostname, () => {
   console.log(`Node.js is listening at http://${hostname}:${port}/`)
})
```

Please note that this code doesn’t remember the cart data beyond storing it in an array. If you need to keep the cart data between restarts of the Node.js server, you’ll need to store it in a database.

Chapter Recap

» JavaScript can communicate with servers via AJAX, using GET and POST requests, respectively, to receive and send data.

» CORS acts to limit AJAX requests to authorized domains.

» You can interact with databases like SQLite to store and retrieve data.

## | 17 |

## Building Web Pages with React

Chapter Overview

» React is a JavaScript library for building interfaces

» React uses Next.js to provide backend capabilities

» Components are reusable building blocks in React

React is a JavaScript library that is primarily focused on building user interfaces in JavaScript. While similar in spirit to our reusable HTML component design pattern we’ve explored in previous chapters, React brings the concept of creating reusable components to the forefront with its use of the innovative JavaScript XML (JSX) syntax. JSX is a syntax extension for JavaScript that lets you write code that looks like HTML right inside JavaScript.

React was written by Jordan Walke at Facebook. It was released to the public in May 2013 (June 2, 2013 on GitHub) and was used internally within Facebook and then later in the interface revamp of Instagram. Since then, it has become a wildly popular JavaScript framework. Even if you don’t plan on using React (but at some point you probably will), you’ll need to be familiar with it so that you can maintain and modify existing code.

While we won’t rebuild our framework in React, it’s still worth exploring. The goal here isn’t to cover React fully, but rather to offer a quick tour of its capabilities and some hands-on exercises that will help you become familiar with the framework. React is the front half of the full-stack framework; however, using the same language to build the frontend as well as the backend gives you a seamless development experience.

### Creating a New React Site

React has a bit of a different workflow than we’ve been using through most of this book, and that’s largely due to the most common way to run a React website—through Next.js. The Next.js portion of this stack provides the backend capabilities, offering tight integration with React.

React websites should live in a separate folder, so, in Visual Studio Code, open your Source folder ( *File* > *Open Folder…* > *Select Folder* ) and create a new folder within it called coffee-shop-react . To create this new folder, mouse up to the top of the Explorer pane in VS Code and a series of icons should appear. Click the Folder icon with the plus ( [figure 35](#ch17.xhtml_fig35) ).

![img](Images/fig35.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_35.png) 

The new folder icon circled in Visual Studio Code’s Explorer pane. The *New Folder…* tooltip is shown when you hover over the icon.

Once you’ve created the coffee-shop-react folder, right click on it and click *Open in Integrated Terminal* . This will open a terminal/console window pane in Visual Studio Code. In this window, type (at the -> prompt)

```
npx create-next-app@latest .
```

…and press ENTER.

![img](Images/Important_icon.png) 

The dot at the end of the command tells Node.js to create the React site within the coffee-shop-react folder. If you don’t specify it, a folder within coffee-shop-react called coffee-shop-react will be created, which is not what we want. Please don’t forget the dot!

You may receive a notice about the necessity of installing packages. If so, simply press ENTER to confirm. You’ll be asked some questions. Here are the answers to provide, pressing ENTER after each to confirm.

» What is your project name? coffee-shop-react

» Would you like to use TypeScript? No (toggle answer with arrow keys)

» Would you like to use ESLint? Yes

» Would you like to use Tailwind CSS? Yes

» Would you like to use a src directory? No

» Use App Router? Yes

» Would you like to customize the default import alias? No

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

Versions of React in the future might ask different questions. If you’re not sure of the answer, the default is probably fine.

Once this new project wizard process completes, you’ll have files ready to go for your React project ( [figure 36](#ch17.xhtml_fig36) ).

![img](Images/fig36.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_36.png) 

The resulting files from the create-next-app wizard.

### Understanding the React Application Structure

Let’s walk through the structure that was just created so we can understand the purpose and function of the files and folders within a typical React application.

We’ll start with the files in the root of the folder first, then work through the folders. We’ll cover many of these in more depth as we progress through the chapter ( [table 10](#ch17.xhtml_tab10) ):

![img](Images/table-10.png) 

| File / Folder | Purpose |
| .eslintrc.json | Configuration file for ESLint, which is an analysis tool that helps find bugs and design issues within JavaScript. |
| .gitignore | A file created to instruct Git, the source code version control system, which files should not be included in a repository and should remain only on your computer. |
| next.config.js | The configuration file for the Next.js web framework. |
| package-lock.json | A file automatically generated by npm that sets versions of dependencies to ensure the package will install and run correctly on another computer. |
| package.json | A configuration file that details the dependencies for the project. |
| postcss.config.js | Configuration file for PostCSS, which facilitates the transformation of styles with various JavaScript plugins, preventing conflicts. |
| README.md | A Markdown file that contains information about the project. This is especially helpful for other developers. |
| tailwind.config.js | The Tailwind CSS configuration file, a CSS framework that makes it easy to quickly add helpful styles to your HTML. |
| app | The folder containing the React JavaScript files. |
| node_modules | The dependency files for the project are stored within this folder. |
| public | This folder contains files that will be served directly to the browser rather than be processed as part of the React app. |

![img](Images/Note_icon.png) 

For more information on Markdown, please see [www.markdownguide.org](http://www.markdownguide.org)

Now let’s look inside the app folder. This is where the bulk of the React website’s code resides. You’ll see at least four files here by default ( [table 11](#ch17.xhtml_tab11) ):

![img](Images/table-11.png) 

| File / Folder | Purpose |
| favicon.ico | The icon displayed for the site in bookmarks. You can replace this with your website’s logo in ico format. |
| globals.css | This CSS file is meant to house CSS styles that will apply to your entire React site. Tailwind CSS will also include some boilerplate code here to import its styles and utilities. |
| layout.js | JavaScript code here generally applies to the entire site, providing navigation elements, header/footer components, and other site-wide features. |
| page.js | The primary landing page of the site. |

The page.js file in the app folder exports the Home component, which is part of what defines what appears on the landing page (i.e., the home page) of the site.

There are two folders that don’t appear in the default file structure, but are nevertheless quite important: the pages and components folders.

In the pages folder, you can place files, like about.js , and they will provide the code necessary for a page with the URL /about . As you might expect, pages/contact.js would serve /contact .

In the components folder, components are defined that can be used for the React website.

### Building Components

Components are at the heart of React. Just like in our framework, React encourages component reuse by making it simple to define reusable bits of code that make up your interface.

Let’s take a look at a simple component. Don’t type this code quite yet. I want to introduce the concept of components first before we actually start writing React code.

```
import React from "react"

function Greeting() {
   return (
      <div>
         <p>Hello, World!</p>
      </div>
   )
}
```

The first thing that might stick out to you about this code is that the name of the function, Greeting , starts with an uppercase letter. In React, component names begin with a capital letter. Also, the HTML within the function should be in all lowercase, although this is the standard we’ve already been following in this book.

What’s unique here is that this function returns HTML—not by writing to the document or using innerHTML-type functionality, but by embedding the HTML directly into the JavaScript code. This is JSX, which contains HTML, but also has other functionality that makes a component far more useful than simply being able to regurgitate HTML.

```
import React from "react"

function Greeting(props) {
   return (
      <div>
         <p>Hello, {props.firstName}!</p>
      </div>
   )
}
```

In this version of the Greeting component, we accept an argument, props , and then use the firstName property of that object to pass to the component the first name we want to greet.

Now that we’ve built this component, to use it, we simply use it in our HTML, like this:

```
<Greeting />
```

We just constructed something akin to a new HTML element within React! Pretty neat, huh? If we want to pass a value to it, we simply use this syntax:

```
<Greeting firstName="Robert" />
```

That was a bit of a simplification. If you’d like to see a more detailed component, check out the app/page.js file to see the Home component that is designed for you by default. It contains a fair bit of code, so we’ll save paper and not print it here, but this serves as a typical example.

You don’t have to use function to define a component. In the next example, you’ll notice that rather than use function to start a component definition, I use const . Both methods are acceptable. It generally comes down to personal preference, with one small distinction: If you use function , you can use components anywhere without having to define them first. If you use const , you must define the components first. Put your components within JavaScript files in the components folder, then import them at the top of files that use them, so this won’t be an issue.

![img](Images/MyTake_icon.png) 

I recommend choosing either the const or function method and sticking with it, at least within the same project—unless there’s a strong need to deviate from the convention you establish.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-17-01.png) 

```
import React from "react"

const AlertButton = ({ alertMessage }) => {
   const handleClick = () => {
      alert(alertMessage)
   }

   return (
      <button onClick={handleClick}>
         Click Me
      </button>
   )
}

const Greeting = () => {
   return (
      <div>
         <AlertButton alertMessage="Hello, World!" />
      </div>
   )
}
```

You have probably noticed we’re doing more than simply introducing a new convention ( const )—we’re nesting components and binding events to them! We’ll tackle each separately.

First, let’s address the concept of nesting components. This is an incredibly useful feature that lets you build even smaller, yet highly functional and flexible, components. I recommend nesting components as much as practical, because the smaller and more self-contained the building block you build, the more you can reuse it. For example, you can use this AlertButton component anytime you need this kind of functionality. By passing the message as a property, you give it the ability to adapt to a wide range of situations.

Second, let’s discuss binding events. JavaScript, at its heart, is event-driven, so binding functionality to events that happen is second nature to the language. React extends this functionality by letting us encapsulate the event within the component, creating a reusable object that can handle its own actions. The handleClick function, as its name suggests, handles the activity upon the click of the button, and this is bound to the button by passing it to the onClick event, with {handleClick} in curly braces.

### Using Components in Other Files

When you define a component, you’ll want to save it in the components folder. I say “want to” because you don’t strictly have to, but this is the convention with React, and it’s best to stick with that convention. The filename should be capitalized just like the name of the component.

For example, if we defined a Greeting component…

```
const Greeting = () => {
   return (
      <div>
         <p>Hello, World!</p>
      </div>
   )
}
export default Greeting
```

…we would name it Greeting.js and place it in the components folder. Note that I’ve added the export default Greeting line at the end. This tells JavaScript to make this object available to other files that include it.

To use the Greeting component, we’d preface the file that used it with…

```
import Greeting from "../components/Greeting.js"
```

The two dots before /components tell JavaScript to go up one directory (to the root of the React app), then go into the components folder to look for the file Greeting.js . This assumes that the file using the component (i.e., the file with the import ) would be in another folder, like pages —which is common practice anyway within a React app.

### Designing in React

As you’ve seen by now, the React paradigm is a bit different from standard JavaScript. We’ve previously discussed reusable components, but thinking in components is vital to designing React websites and applications. To illustrate this point quite literally, let’s look at a typical (and simple) page layout ( [figure 37](#ch17.xhtml_fig37) ):

![img](Images/fig37.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_37.png) 

A sample web page layout.

Just like the DOM structure resembles trees and their branches in describing the hierarchy of a web page, the same thoughtful nesting of components can save us time and make our design incredibly efficient. If I were going to determine the component structure of the sample web page layout in [figure 38](#ch18.xhtml_fig38) , I would arrange it in the following manner:

» Header

» Logo

» Primary Navigation

» Social Links

» Content

» Full-width Section

» Content Teasers

» Teaser 1

» Teaser 2

» Teaser 3

» Footer

» Social Links

Now let’s create the same structure with components. Since HTML and CSS aren’t our focus, we’ll skip styling and a lot of the HTML that would be needed to actually give these items a great visual appeal.

![img](Images/Snippet_icon-17-02.png) 

```
import Image from "next/image"

const Logo = () => (
   <Image src="logo.png" alt="Our Logo" />
)

const PrimaryNavigation = ({ links }) => (
   <div>
      {links.map((link, index) => (
         <a href={link.page}>{link.name}</a>
      ))}
   </div>
)

const SocialLinks = ({ socialAccounts }) => (
   <div>
      {socialAccounts.map((account, index) => (
         <a href={account.link}>{account.site}</a>
      ))}
   </div>
)

const FullWidthSlider = ({ slides }) => (
   <div>
      {slides.map((slide, index) => (
         <a href={slide.link}><img src={slide.image} alt={`Slide ${index}`} /></a>
      ))}
   </div>
)

const Teaser = ({ thumbnail, text }) => (
   <div>
      <img src={thumbnail} alt="thumbnail" />
      <div>
         {text}
      </div>
   </div>
)

const Header = ({ links, socialAccounts }) => (
   <div>
      <Logo />
      <PrimaryNavigation links={links} />
      <SocialLinks socialAccounts={socialAccounts} />
   </div>
)

const Content = ({ slides, teaserThumbnails, teaserTexts }) => (
   <div>
      <FullWidthSlider slides={slides} />
      <Teaser
         thumbnail={teaserThumbnails[0]}
         text={teaserTexts[0]}
      />
   </div>
)

const Footer = ({ socialAccounts }) => (
   <SocialLinks socialAccounts={socialAccounts} />
)
```

Let’s walk through some important aspects of this code.

First, you may notice that I define the components in the general order of least complex to most complex. This lets us nest components more easily. For example, the Header uses the SocialLinks component, but this could also exist anywhere else in the page, so it makes sense to define this ahead of time.

Even though the Logo component is simple and only used once on this page, defining this standalone item gives us expandability in the future. Let’s say we change the logo from a portable network graphic file (PNG file) to a scalable vector graphic file (SVG file). Doing so would be easier with this Logo component. It would also let us use the logo in multiple places on the page more easily. Perhaps in the future we could even accept a property that lets us use various versions to accommodate dark mode, black and white, and the like.

Second, we are using the map method to iterate over arrays passed to the components. We covered the map method in [chapter 14](#ch14.xhtml) , but in essence, we’re making use of the temporary variables assigned from the values in the array in each pass. This generates a list of links (in the case of PrimaryNavigation and SocialLinks ) and a list of links with images (in the case of FullWidthSlider ). The HTML we generate obviously needs more styling, but the concepts work well.

Let’s look at an example for handling products. Imagine a data structure of coffee products:

```
const inventory = [
   { productName: "Coffee", price: "3.95", inStock: true },
   { category: "Espresso", price: "4.49", inStock: true },
   { category: "Cappuccino", price: "4.95", inStock: false }
]

A component could iterate through the inventory like this:

const InventoryList = ({ inventory }) => {
   return (
      <div>
         {inventory.map((item, index) => (
            <div id={index}>
               <h2>{item.productName}</h2>
               <p>Price: ${item.price}</p>
               <p>In Stock: {item.inStock ? 'Yes' : 'No'}</p>
            </div>))}
      </div>
   )
}
```

The inventory data JSON can come from a remote server—it need not necessarily be included directly in your React website. And the data supplied by the remote server can come from an SQL server, like MySQL, Microsoft SQL, or even SQLite.

Chapter Recap

» React is a JavaScript library for building interfaces that was released in 2013 by Facebook, now Meta.

» React pairs with Next.js to provide backend capabilities. You use npm to create a new React project.

» Components in React allow you to design the structure of your site with reusable building blocks.

## | 18 |

## Managing Your Code with Git

Chapter Overview

» Source code management makes collaboration easier

» Git is a popular source code management system

» GitHub is a popular Git repository hosting service

Up until now, our programs have just been text documents on our machine. Each change we made overwrites the existing content. This might not seem like an issue, until we alter a working program at 11 p.m. and accidentally break something, causing a website to crash with no easy means of recovery. This may or may not be the voice of experience. But it is entirely preventable.

With version tracking, we have a log of all our code changes. We also get a backup if we sync with a remote location or provider. ***Source code management*** is the process of tracking changes in code and synchronizing them for purposes of collaboration.

Before I learned about source code management, anytime I worked on a program with others, we would work out a sometimes convoluted scheme to only work on files others weren’t editing. That strategy can be effective, but often results in lots of exchanged messages asking who’s working on what file. And the inevitable collision that can occur creates plenty of headaches for everyone. This approach has numerous flaws and didn’t carry over well to modern development methodologies.

In this chapter, we’ll learn about the Git source code management system, released to the public in 2005 by Linus Torvalds. If that name sounds familiar, it’s because Linus also started the Linux kernel, used in countless devices and in most of the world’s servers today. Since the release of Git, the software has enjoyed massive success, in part due to its fantastic ***branching*** support and the ease of ***merging*** those branches.

Branching is the process of copying code to a new, separate version in a software project. Unique branches move forward with new versions in parallel, and merging brings those versions back together, integrating the changes into a unified copy. Much like a road can add lanes in more populated or traffic-prone areas, then merge back into fewer lanes when it reaches the countryside.

Software code is stored in Git repositories. A ***repository*** is the container that stores source code. On a computer, a Git repository is just a folder of code (and possibly subfolders) with a .git folder in the main folder. This .git folder acts like a journal that contains the history of code changes. A Git repository can be pushed to a remote copy for collaboration and backup purposes.

GitHub, GitLab, and Bitbucket are popular Git repository hosting companies, but anyone can host a repository themselves, because Git is open source. In this chapter, we’ll focus on local repositories and syncing with GitHub; however, the techniques we explore here should be compatible with other Git hosting.

### Installing Git

Before you can use Git, you will need to install it on your computer. Visit [https://git-scm.com/](https://git-scm.com/) and click the *download* button to see instructions for your platform. Windows users will need to download the installer and run it. On macOS, you can install Homebrew (see [https://brew.sh/](https://brew.sh/) ) to install Git. Once you’ve installed Homebrew, run brew install git to install Git.

Debian/Ubuntu/Linux Mint users need to run apt install git . Linux Arch-based distributions such as Arch and Manjaro need to run pacman -S git . Red Hat-based distributions would run yum install git .

Once Git is installed, you should be able to open a terminal or console and run…

```
git version
```

…and you’ll see the version of Git installed. If you see a message like “command not found,” Git isn’t installed properly, so check the download/install instructions for your platform and try again.

Windows and macOS users can also install GitHub Desktop from [https://desktop.github.com/](https://desktop.github.com/) . GitHub Desktop is a wonderful Git management tool that makes it easy to graphically manage Git repositories. Though I find it very useful and have recommended it to others, I am not covering it in this chapter because it isn’t available for all platforms. You may not have it on the system you’re on (or on a remote system you’ve connected to), so it’s good to know how to use Git without it.

That said, if you’re running Windows or macOS and prefer a graphical solution, it might be a good fit for you.

Visual Studio Code also has Git support, but in the interest of providing you the most compatible and widespread coverage of knowledge, in this chapter we will focus on the command-line version of Git.

### Forking and Cloning the ClydeBank Coffee Shop Website

The first step in using existing code on GitHub is to ***fork*** it to our own account. Forking a repository copies it to our account and lets us work with the code, including making changes of our own, without interfering with the original copy. We can later request the changes we made be integrated into the original project with a ***pull request*** . A pull request asks the maintainer(s) of the original project for permission to merge our changes into the main project.

Before you can fork the project, you’ll need to make a free account on GitHub. Navigate to [https://github.com/](https://github.com/) and create an account, then navigate to [https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide](https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-QuickStartGuide) and click the *fork* button at the top right of the screen. This puts a copy of the repository in your account. Then you can ***clone*** , or download, the repository to your computer, where you’ll be able to access and edit the files.

When you click on the green <> Code icon ( [figure 38](#ch18.xhtml_fig38) ) above the code in GitHub, you’ll see the commands for cloning the repository. The simplest variant is HTTPS, but if you know how to use SSH keys, you can add your public SSH key to GitHub and use the command given in the SSH tab. Copy the HTTPS command to your clipboard or temporary notepad and have it ready for your console or terminal. First, however, we’ll need to change into our source directory.

![img](Images/fig38.png)  ![Images](Images/Fig_38.png) 

The Code sync button in the GitHub interface.

I make a folder called Source in my home directory, and I change into it with this on my Mac:

```
cd ~/Source
```

This works on Linux, too. The tilde ( ~ ) is short for home directory. If you’re a Windows user, run this from your command prompt:

```
cd %HOMEPATH%\Source
```

If you’re running Bash for Windows (i.e., Windows Subsystem for Linux), you can run the Linux commands in this section. The cd %HOMEPATH% command works only on the Windows command prompt or PowerShell prompt and not the Bash terminal.

Now that you’ve changed directory (i.e., cd ) into your Source directory, type git clone , press the spacebar, and then paste the command from the HTTPS tab of the Code pane in GitHub into the terminal, and run it. The completed command will look something like this:

```
git clone https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-CoffeeShopWebsite
```

When you fork a repository, a copy of it will be in *your* GitHub account. The command above represents the repository name in *my* account (note the rwoliver2 ). Your command will have the rwoliver2 substituted with your GitHub username, so be sure to use that copy instead of the exact link I have listed above.

If all has gone well, you now have JavaScript-CoffeeShopWebsite in ~/Source , so the full path should be ~/Source/JavaScript-CoffeeShopWebsite .

Now you can launch Visual Studio Code and open that folder to view and edit the code.

### Committing Changes to Your Repository

If you make changes to the code and want to commit them to your fork, run this:

```
git add filename.js
```

In this case, you’ll need to replace filename.js with the name of the file you want to add (e.g., main.js ). By *adding* the file, you are staging it to be committed. You can run this command on any number of files you wish (including entire directories), or you can run…

```
git add .
```

…to add all the files (and files in folders) within this repository to be staged for commit. (Note the period, which means the current directory.) You can run…

```
git status
```

…to see what changes are staged to be committed. If you run git status on a repository with no changes, you’ll see this:

```
On branch main
Your branch is up to date with 'origin/main'.

nothing to commit, working tree clean
```

If you modify a file, then run git status , you’ll see something like this:

```
On branch main
Your branch is up to date with 'origin/main'.

Changes not staged for commit:
   (use "git add <file>..." to update what will be committed)
   (use "git restore <file>..." to discard changes in working directory)
   modified:   main.js

no changes added to commit (use "git add" and/or "git commit -a")
```

In this case, we can see that main.js has been modified. This will include any file you have modified.

To commit this change to your local Git repository, you would first stage (add) the file (as above), then run the following:

```
git commit -m "Short message describing your changes."
```

The message, specified by the -m argument (in this case, “Short message describing your changes.”) can be anything, but it’s helpful (to your future self and others) to provide detail about what you changed.

You can commit multiple files, or tell Git to commit all modified files (which skips the need to add them) via:

```
git commit -a -m "A note about your changes."
```

The -a argument specifies “all,” meaning all changed files should be included in the commit.

![img](Images/Caution_icon.png) 

Be careful when specifying the -a flag with git commit . You may include files that you aren’t quite ready to commit.

### Pushing to Remote

The commit command commits changes to your local Git repository. However, if you want to sync them to GitHub, you’ll need to run this:

```
git push origin main
```

The git push portion is the actual command, which tells Git we want to push our work to a remote repository. The origin portion tells Git where to push the changes. If we clone a repository from GitHub, the origin will be set to GitHub. The main portion indicates which branch to push.

### Using Branches

Branches let you track separate sets of changes. For example, if you wanted to work on adding a particular feature, you could create a new branch for that.

```
git checkout -b branch_name
```

This new branch (named whatever you put in the command above for branch_name ) will now receive any changes you commit. To push that branch, you’d run:

```
git push origin branch_name
```

To switch branches, run…

```
git checkout branch_name
```

…substituting the name of the branch you want to switch to in place of branch_name .

If you want to create (and change to) a new branch, use the -b argument, as previously described.

![img](Images/Remember_icon.png) 

Branches are independent sets of changes (i.e., commits), so the changes you make in one branch won’t automatically carry over to others.

### Viewing Changes

To view the changes, or the differences between your current set of files and the last commit, run…

```
git diff
```

…which shows a “diff”, or difference summary, of the pending changes. You can press the Q key to exit the diff viewer.

### Viewing Your Commit Log

As your Git repository grows with changes, you can use . . .

```
git log
```

…to see a listing of all the commits you’ve made, which will look something like this:

```
commit 6dd258b5273bc4a13eee201fd6304efc61baef5a (HEAD -> main, origin/main, origin/HEAD)
Author: Robert W. Oliver II <118407+rwoliver2@users.noreply.github.com>
Date:   Sun Jul 3 23:11:07 2022 -0500

   Update README.md

commit a2af786f19023bd2ac2d023ce8b2f2ed664f733b
Author: Robert W. Oliver II <118407+rwoliver2@users.noreply.github.com>
Date:   Sun Jul 3 23:07:07 2022 -0500

   Initial commit.
```

As with the diff command, press Q to exit the viewer. If there are few commits, the results will be displayed, and you’ll be returned to the command prompt.

You can also view a list of the commits that have been pushed to GitHub by going to the repository and clicking the “commits” icon (preceded by an icon and the number of commits) at the top of the file display.

### Pull Requests

Now that you’ve committed changes to your repository and pushed them to your fork on GitHub, you can request that those changes be pulled into the official repository (in this case, [https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-CoffeeShopWebsite](https://github.com/clydebankmedia/JavaScript-CoffeeShopWebsite) ) with a pull request.

To create a pull request, find your fork of the repository on GitHub. Click the *Pull Requests* tab at the top and then click the green *New Pull Request* button. In the “comparing changes” section, specify the repository and branch to pull from on the right and choose the destination on the left.

You’ll then be prompted to enter a message that describes the request. Submitting the request doesn’t automatically merge the changes. The maintainer of the official repository makes that decision, so including details in the message as to why they should accept the changes is helpful.

### Syncing with the Official Repository

Over time, if other people are also merging changes into the official repository, it will become different from your fork of the repository. It’s a good idea to sync them as often as possible to prevent the two code bases from drifting far enough apart that a merge would require manual intervention.

To perform this sync, go to your fork of the repository in GitHub and click *Fetch Upstream* . If it says it’s behind, click *Fetch and Merge* . If all goes well, your repository will be automatically updated with changes from the official copy.

If you have altered your repository significantly, an automatic update may not be possible. In that case, you’ll have to complete further steps, which may include manually editing certain files. To prevent this, be sure to do regular syncs with the official repository.

[![img](Images/f0347-01.png)](http://www.quickclips.io/javascript-9) 

Chapter Recap

» A Git repository is a collection of files and the changes to those files over time.

» It’s a good idea to fork a repository you plan to use, especially if you make changes to its code. You can also create a pull request, asking the maintainer of the official project to incorporate your changes.

» Branches are independent sets of changes to the files in a repository. You can switch branches at any time and commit changes to those individual branches. You can also sync those branches with the repository hosted on GitHub or any other Git hosting service via the push command.

## Conclusion

Though your JavaScript career is just beginning, your effort throughout this book has given you a great deal of experience that will give you a tremendous advantage. Learning a new programming language—especially if you haven’t coded before—is no small achievement. You have a lot to be proud of!

We’ve covered the essentials, then toured JavaScript’s frontend and backend capabilities. We’ve also explored popular third-party libraries like jQuery and React. Not only have you learned the essentials of JavaScript, but you’ve also written code that you can use in your everyday projects.

Will you use your newfound knowledge to build a popular website? Perhaps you’re interested in creating a Node.js application to handle the requests for the next hot mobile game.

Whatever you choose, the options ahead for you are nearly limitless!
